Full QA Deep Trace · 2026-08-31

Family Cube QA Deep Trace

Every claim below is backed by a real inserted database row, a real (or precisely replicated) server action, and a cited line of the app's actual source code — not a description of how the app is supposed to work.

How to read this

Test data only. Every case ran against a disposable, made-up test family (ids prefixed zzq...), created fresh and fully deleted at the end of each trace, verified by a zero-row check. The real family's data was never touched.

Two ways an action was performed. Some app actions require being logged in as a real person — a thing a database inspection tool can't fake. For those, the exact server-side logic was read line-by-line and copied by hand, so the resulting data is identical to what a real tap would have produced. Steps say which method was used: real call or copied logic.

Findings are graded. FIXED means found and corrected the same day. GAP means a real inconsistency worth knowing about, not yet changed. HIGH means a security-relevant gap.

The security sweep

Before the trace even started, a real bug was found: one family member's phone could take an action — confirm a ride, decline a chore, approve a submission — and have the app record it as if a different family member had done it. Once the first instance was found and fixed, every other server action that takes "who did this" as an input was checked by hand, one by one, against the same rule: does this action verify who's really asking, or does it just trust whatever name/id the phone sends?

reassign_choreHanding a chore to someone else — no check at all that the caller is who they claim to be.FIXED
cancel_locked_assignmentReopening a stuck delegated task.FIXED
decline_kid_choreA parent turning down a kid's chore idea.FIXED
dispute_redoA kid escalating "I did do it right" to a second parent.FIXED
propose_later_dateAsking for more time on a chore — had no identity or even family check at all.FIXED
respond_to_parent_questAccepting/declining a delegated household task.FIXED
set_gp_withdrawnA grandparent passing on an invite.FIXED
submit_choreSubmitting finished work — the highest-stakes of the eight, since this path can pay out real coins immediately.FIXED
confirm/decline/reassign_eventThe original ride-assignment bug that started this whole sweep.FIXED (earlier)

All nine fixes use the same pattern: before doing anything, the server now asks "does the phone's own logged-in session actually belong to the person it says it's acting as?" — verified against the real login, not just trusted at face value. Every one of the roughly 30 other actions in the app that take an actor's identity were checked and already had this protection.

Chores

Creation, claiming, handoffs, terms changes, submission, approval, redo, and reward payout — 13 cases.

D1-1A plain chore, no coins, assigned straight to one kid
A parent adds "make your bed" for one kid — no reward attached.
1

Parent creates it via the New Chore screen, taps the kid's avatar directly (not "Bounty"), leaves coins at 0.

assigned_to_id=kid1, is_pool=false, coins_reward=0, status='todo'
Kid

Appears in their normal to-do list — but the spot where a coin badge usually sits is just empty, no "$0" or "no reward" label either way.

Teen / Grandparent

Invisible — not a pool item, not assigned to them.

ⓘ Minor

A zero-coin chore looks identical to "reward not set up yet" — no microcopy distinguishes "on purpose, no reward" from an oversight.

D1-2A paid job left open for anyone to grab
"Wash the car, 25 coins" — first kid or teen to claim it does it.
1

Parent taps the ⚡ Bounty chip instead of a person; a picker offers 1 / 2 / 3 / ∞ claimants; coins set to 25.

is_pool=true, coins_reward=25, assigned_to_id=null
Kid & Teen

Both see it with a Claim Quest button — the pool has no kid-vs-teen distinction.

Grandparent

Invisible — the grandparent pool only shows chores explicitly flagged for grandparents.

D1-3An errand meant only for a grandparent
"Pick up a prescription" — not for the kids.
1

Parent flips 👴 GP Welcome — this automatically zeroes any coin amount (grandparents never earn coins for errands).

is_pool=true, invite_grandparents=true, coins_reward=0
Kid / Teen

Explicitly excluded — sees nothing.

Grandparent

Sees it with button I'd Love To Help (or Reconsider? if they'd passed on it once already).

D1-4A chore meant to be "teens only"
A parent wants a chore only a teenager (not a young kid) can take — e.g. anything driving-adjacent.
1

Searched the entire chore-creation screen for a "teens only" control.

⚠ Real gap — not fixed

This feature does not exist. There is no "teens only" toggle anywhere in the chore-creation screens — only "Parent Only" and "GP Welcome." A same-named field exists in the database, but it's leftover from the ride-scheduling system and does nothing for chores. Setting it manually made no difference: a young kid could still see and claim a chore a parent believed was teen-restricted, with zero enforcement.

Verdict Dead/vestigial field. No functional "teens only" restriction exists for chores today.
D1-5A kid pitches their own chore idea
A kid asks "can I do X for Y coins?" and a parent has to approve or adjust it first.
1

Kid submits "Wash the dog." copied logic

status='pending_kid_proposal', assigned_to_id=kid1, coins_reward=0
Parent

A coin field defaulting to 5, a due date defaulting to the coming Saturday, button Set & Approve.

2

Parent approves at 15 coins. copied logic

status: pending_kid_proposal → 'todo', coins_reward=15
Kid

Now a normal chore worth 15 coins — no visible trace it started as a pitch.

3

A second pitch ("skip vegetables at dinner" — testing the limits!) is declined. copied logic

Row deleted entirely — it "was never a live chore"
Kid

The idea just disappears from their list — no reason shown on this particular path.

⚠ Finding

Two entirely separate "kid proposes a chore" systems exist side-by-side in the app — one that only becomes a real chore on approval, and one that's a real (draft) chore from the start. Likely two build attempts that were never merged into one. Not breaking anything today, but confusing for anyone maintaining the feature — and it's unclear which one a given kid actually lands on.

D1-6Grandma sponsors a special reward — can she keep it private from Grandpa?
Grandma funds a "video-call puzzle night" bonus for a grandkid. Can she control whether other grandparents in the family see it?
1

Searched the sponsor-a-quest flow for any "share with other grandparents" setting.

ⓘ Not quite the expected shape

There's no toggle to hide, because every sponsored quest is already private to its one sponsor by default — a second grandparent has no code path that would ever show it to them, toggle or not. This may be intentional (each grandparent's own private gift), but there's also no way for two grandparents to co-fund or even be aware of each other's sponsored quests — worth a product decision rather than treating as settled.

D1-8 – D1-10Grandparent claims an invited errand — offer, accept, decline
Does tapping "help" on a grandparent-only errand claim it instantly, or does a parent still have to say yes?
1

Grandma taps I'd Love To Help. real call

status: 'todo' → 'gp_offer_pending', gp_offer_by_id=senior1, assigned_to_id still null

This settles the question directly: it's a two-step offer, then a parent approves — not an instant claim.

Parent

Sees it awaiting an Accept/Decline decision.

2a

Parent accepts. copied logic

status → 'in_progress', assigned_to_id=senior1

Grandma gets a chat message: ✅ Your offer to handle "[title]" was accepted — go ahead!

2b

Different errand — parent declines instead, with reason "We already have a ride sorted, thank you!" copied logic

status → 'todo' (back in the open pool), rejection_reason set

Grandma gets: 💬 "[title]" wasn't accepted this time — "[reason]". Thanks for offering! — and the errand reappears in her own pool, still claimable.

D1-11Parent changes the reward mid-task — kid can accept or hand it back
Kid already claimed "Clean the garage" for 20 coins; parent bumps it to 30 (or changes something the kid doesn't want).
1

Parent proposes new terms.

status → 'terms_changed', pending_terms: {old: 20, new: 30}
Kid

A red box: The terms changed — Coins: 20 → 30 🪙 — buttons Still fine by me / Hand it back.

2a

Kid taps Still fine by me. copied logic

status → 'in_progress', pending_terms cleared — kid keeps it at the new terms
2b

Different chore — kid taps Hand it back instead. copied logic

status → 'todo', is_pool=true, assigned_to_id=null — toast "Handed back ✓"

The parent who proposed the change is notified it was turned down.

D1-12Handing a chore to one specific sibling
A kid can't do "take out recycling" and wants to hand it specifically to their teenage sibling — not just drop it in the general pool.
1

Kid opens Can't make it?, picks reason, then Hand it to someone specific → picks their sibling → Send it over. (A kid's picker only ever offers other kids/teens — never a parent or grandparent.) copied logic

pending_handoff_to=teen1, assigned_to_id still kid1 (not yet reassigned)
Sibling

A box: [name] wants to hand you this, with the reason quoted — buttons I've got it / Can't either.

2a

Sibling accepts. copied logic

assigned_to_id → teen1, is_pool=false
2b

Different chore — sibling declines instead. copied logic

assigned_to_id=null, is_pool=true — back in the GENERAL open pool, not re-offered to the original kid specifically
D1-13 – D1-14Kid submits finished work with a note; parent approves
1

Kid submits "Water the garden" with note "Watered all the pots, even the hanging ones!" real call (confirmed this one genuinely requires a real login — attempting it directly failed exactly as expected)

status → 'pending_approval', submission_note set, 24-hour auto-approve clock starts
Parent

Chore title, a +10 pts badge, a box headed CHILD'S NOTE with the exact text, and a countdown badge for the 24h auto-approval window.

2

Parent approves. copied logic (the payout half is protected by a second, independent database-level guard that blocks even direct writes without a real login — confirmed live, so the exact coin transfer couldn't be forced through, only proven correct by the underlying formula)

status → 'approved'  ·  by formula: +15 coins (10 base + 5 bonus), +5 XP
✓ Reassuring, not a bug

Reward payouts are protected twice — once in the server action itself, and again by a database-level rule that rejects any balance change that didn't come from a verified action. That's genuinely solid design, not a weakness.

D1-15Does a grandparent ever get coins for finishing a chore?
1
ⓘ It depends which of two features is involved

A plain grandparent errand (case 8-10's pharmacy run): never pays coins, by design, no matter who approves it — a grandparent's own errands are logged and thanked, not paid.

A grandparent-sponsored quest (case 6's kind of thing, where a KID does the work and the grandparent verifies it): DOES pay coins — to the kid, from a separate grandparent-funded wallet, not the grandparent themselves.

Someone testing casually could see one "grandparent chore" pay and another not, and assume it's a bug — it's actually two different, intentionally different features that happen to sound similar.

D1-16Does "partial credit" exist for half-finished chores?
1

Searched the entire chore system for any partial/prorated reward logic.

ⓘ Doesn't exist

Approval is strictly all-or-nothing — full reward, or send back for a redo. The word "partial" exists elsewhere in the app (a grocery-list feature, unrelated), but nowhere in chores.

D1-17 – D1-18Kid asks for more time — approved, and declined
Kid can't finish "Mow the lawn" by the deadline and asks for 2 more days.
1

Kid requests a new date with reason "Soccer practice ran late." copied logic

assigned_to_id → null, is_pool → true (released immediately!), pending_later_date set, due_date unchanged
⚠ Real gap, confirmed live

The moment a kid asks for more time, the chore is immediately dropped into the open pool — before the parent has even responded. Any other kid or teen could grab it out from under the original kid while the request is still pending.

2a

Parent approves the new date. copied logic

due_date updated — but assigned_to_id STAYS null
⚠ Real gap, confirmed live

Approving does not give the chore back to the kid who asked. If nobody happens to re-claim it in the meantime, the chore can end up belonging to nobody at all, at the new date.

2b

Different chore — parent declines the request instead. copied logic

due_date unchanged  ·  assigned_to_id STILL null
⚠ The clearest gap in this whole batch

"Declined, so the kid keeps their original date" is only half true. The date does stay the same — but the chore was already given up to the open pool the moment the request was made, and declining does nothing to give it back. In plain terms: a kid who asks for more time and gets told no doesn't just keep their deadline — they may have already lost the chore itself to someone else, who now has the kid's original due date instead of them.

Events & rides

Creation, driver/helper assignment, confirm/decline, passengers, pickup, privacy, and conflicts — 14 cases.

D2-1A plain event with no ride involved at all
"Piano Lesson" on the calendar — nobody needs to drive or do anything.
1

Confirms the driver/helper machinery is entirely opt-in — a plain event never touches it.

D2-2 – D2-3Picking someone else as driver vs. picking yourself
1

Parent1 books a ride and picks Parent2 as driver.

helper_status='pending' (someone ELSE was picked)
Parent2

Sees it in their own list with buttons Confirm I'll do it and Can't.

2

Different ride — Parent1 picks THEMSELVES as driver instead.

helper_status='confirmed' — immediately, never passes through "pending" at all

This is a deliberate rule: an early version made even a self-pick start as "pending," which read to a real parent as "my own assignment is waiting for someone else's permission" — confusing, and changed after that live confusion was reported.

D2-4Leaving a ride fully open
"Kid1 needs a lift to the library" — parent doesn't pick anyone, just opens it to whoever can help.
1

Works as designed: first to claim wins, matches the app's own "first to claim wins" copy.

D2-5, D2-7A kid's OWN ride request behaves differently from a parent's
Does it matter whether a ride was requested by a kid, or created by a parent?
1

Kid1 requests "Basketball practice" (a Sports-category activity, not Ride) through the kid-only request screen.

approval_pending=true, ride_required=true, driver_status='pending'
ⓘ A real, deliberate technical quirk worth knowing

When a parent creates a "Ride" category event, the driver is tracked in one set of fields. When a kid requests a ride themselves — even for a Ride-category activity — it's tracked in a different set of fields internally. Both eventually show up correctly on screen, but anyone testing "does ride assignment work" needs to test both a parent-made ride and a kid-requested ride separately — they don't share the same underlying mechanism, even though they look the same to a family using the app.

D2-6A tutor AND a separate driver on the same event
Kid1 has tutoring — an outside tutor runs the session, but Parent2 separately needs to drive them there.
1

Both roles set on one single event.

Tutor (external, not a family member)

No app access at all — this is expected, they're not a user.

Parent2

Sees their own driving assignment, completely independent of the tutor — confirming or declining one never touches the other.

ⓘ Worth knowing

When a screen needs to show "who is this assigned to" in a generic way (not the two specific cards built for this), it checks the tutor/helper field FIRST and only falls back to the driver if that's empty — so on an event shaped like this one, a generic screen would only ever mention the tutor, silently never showing the driver.

D2-9A grandparent declines a ride — which pool reopens?
Grandma was the pending driver for a basketball pickup but has a conflict and declines.
1

Grandma declines. real call

driver removed, open-to-grandparents → true, open-to-teens → stays whatever it already was
⚠ Real gap

Declining correctly reopens the pool the decliner came from (grandparent pool, since a grandparent declined) — but if the ride was never also open to teens, it now reopens to nobody, with no signal to the parent that it needs re-opening. It quietly looks the same as "nobody's looked at this yet."

D2-10 – D2-11Adding — and removing — a passenger
A sibling tags along on a ride, not as driver or helper, just along for the ride.
1

Both adding and removing a passenger leaves the driver assignment completely undisturbed — confirmed both directions.

D2-12Grandparent marks pickup done — does the parent find out?
1

Yes, two ways: a family-wide chat message announcing it, and — if the parent has the app open at that moment — a live, read-only status view of the trip. There's no evidence of a separate quiet push notification beyond the chat message, if a silent one was expected.

D2-13Full loop: kid asks → parent approves → opens it up → teen claims it
1

Kid requests "Swim class" pickup.

Parent

Card badge Needs a ride.

Teen / Grandparent

Nothing yet — still awaiting parent approval.

2

Parent opens it to helpers.

Teen & Grandparent

Both now see it in their own open-pickup pools.

3

Teen claims it. real call

driver_status='confirmed'

Everyone gets notified: the teen, the parent, and the kid.

ⓘ Minor, worth knowing

Once claimed, the underlying "open to grandparents/teens" switches technically stay flipped on — the app relies on the assigned driver's own status to hide the card from other pool members, not on those switches themselves. Doesn't cause a visible bug today, but any future screen that only checks those switches (without also checking who's assigned) would wrongly think an already-claimed ride is still open.

D2-14Can a parent turn down a kid's ride request, with a reason?
1

Searched every ride card and the calendar edit screen for a Decline-with-reason option.

⚠ Real gap

No such feature exists. The only way a parent can refuse a kid's ride request at all is to delete the event outright from the Calendar tab — a plain "Delete Event?" confirmation with no place to type a reason. The kid gets no notification and no explanation; the request just silently disappears from their schedule the next time it refreshes.

D2-15A medical appointment — who sees what
1

By design, matching earlier findings this session: assigned parent sees full detail always; an uninvolved grandparent sees a grey "Busy" placeholder with no details unless specifically shared for care; an uninvolved sibling sees nothing at all unless specifically shared with siblings.

D2-16A teenager's own personal plan — does a parent see it?
"Movie night with friends," an ordinary (not Medical) event a teen put on the calendar for themselves.
1
ⓘ Consistent with the E4 finding elsewhere in this report

Confirms it precisely: a teen's ordinary personal plan gets full, normal visibility to every parent by default — the app's own code explains that this kind of privacy needs the teen to manually flip a "Private" switch themselves; nothing about the event type does it automatically the way it does for Medical appointments.

D2-17The same parent double-booked on two rides
1

Two rides, 15 minutes apart, same parent driving both.

Produces a warning banner reading, verbatim: "[FirstName] assigned to 2 events" — dismissible.

ⓘ Worth knowing

The conflict check only looks at how many minutes apart the two rides start — it doesn't check whether the first ride's drop-off time actually overlaps the second's pickup. Two rides scheduled exactly 31 minutes apart (leaving zero travel time) would NOT trigger this warning, even though the parent still can't literally be in two places at once.

D2-18Editing only the time/location — does it disturb the driver's confirmed status?
1

Parent moves a confirmed ride's time by 15 minutes and changes the pickup spot — nothing about who's driving.

helper_status stays 'confirmed' — completely untouched
Verdict Works correctly — this is exactly the kind of accidental side-effect this session's earlier recurring-ride bug was about, and this single-event edit path was already safe.

Claim races & open pools

What happens when two people reach for the same thing at once, and how the different "who can claim this" pools behave.

The core mechanism, checked everywhere it's used
1

Every real claim action (a grandparent taking an errand, a teen grabbing a pool chore, a family member claiming a ride slot) uses the same underlying trick: the database only lets the write through if the item is still unclaimed at that exact instant. Two people tapping "claim" within the same second genuinely cannot both win — the second one's attempt is refused outright, atomically, at the database level.

✓ Fixed this session

Before today, the person who lost that race got no feedback at all — just a silent, confusing reversion with nothing telling them why. Now shows: Already taken by another grandparent.

2

One specific claim path — a grandparent "stepping in" to cover a ride whose original helper has gone quiet — does not use this same protection; it's a plain write with no race-guard.

⚠ Real gap

Two grandparents both tapping "I'll Step In" on the same unresponsive-helper ride within the same moment could both believe, on their own screens, that they've taken it — with whoever's write lands last silently winning on the server, and no error shown to the loser.

"Pass" behaves three different ways depending on who and where
1

Traced every "Pass" button in the app.

⚠ Real inconsistency

A grandparent's Pass is remembered permanently (saved to their profile, so that specific item stays hidden from them from then on) — but which of two different underlying mechanisms saves it depends on which screen the Pass was tapped from. A teenager's Pass is not saved at all — it only hides the item for as long as that screen stays open; closing and reopening the app brings it right back. Three different behaviors for what a family would expect to be the same one action.

2

Also checked: is there any way to "give back" a chore you've already claimed, before doing any of the work?

ⓘ Uneven coverage

Grandparents have a dedicated Backout button for this. Kids and teens do not — there's no equivalent "never mind, put it back" action for an ordinary claimed pool chore once they've grabbed it.

A path that skips the safety mechanism entirely
1

One of the main "claim this pooled item" actions used by kids, teens, and the Quests screen doesn't call the same carefully-built, audited server action that a matching one already exists for — it does a simpler, hand-rolled version instead.

ⓘ Not currently broken, worth confirming as intentional

Both approaches are individually safe against a double-claim, so nothing is exploitable today — but the more thorough version (which also logs who did what, and double-checks family membership) sits unused, while the simpler one is what's actually live. Worth a quick confirmation with whoever built it that this was on purpose, not an oversight from an in-progress migration.

Who sees what, side by side

The same piece of information, viewed from every family member's own screen at the same moment.

Medical appointments — the privacy ladder works correctly
1

Confirmed three levels exactly as intended: the assigned parent always sees everything; a grandparent sees only a grey "Busy" block unless specifically invited into the medical loop (at which point they see everything); an uninvolved sibling sees nothing at all unless specifically shared with them.

A teenager's personal plans have no real default privacy
1

The app's own internal design notes explicitly say a teen's personal social plans are supposed to get the same kind of privacy protection medical appointments get automatically.

⚠ The most concrete privacy gap found

In practice, nothing does this automatically. A teenager's personal calendar entry — a plan with friends, nothing to do with family logistics — is fully visible, in complete detail, to every parent, every sibling, and every grandparent by default. The privacy protection only kicks in if the teen (or a parent) manually flips a "Private" switch that a teen creating an event has no obvious reason to know exists.

One grandparent's sponsored gift is invisible to the other grandparent
1

Tested directly: does a second grandparent see "Grandma's bounty" labeled as hers, see it unlabeled, or not at all?

ⓘ Not at all — resolved definitively

A second grandparent has zero visibility into another grandparent's sponsored reward — not a stripped-down version, genuinely nothing. This may be intentional (each grandparent's own private gift), but the specific piece of logic that causes it was written for an unrelated purpose (keeping the "open invitations" list from double-counting already-claimed items), so this side effect has no explicit design decision backing it. Worth a quick confirmation.

Parents have no overview of kids' coin balances
1

Searched the entire parent Hub screen for any display of an individual kid's or teen's running coin balance.

ⓘ Genuine gap, plausibly intentional

None exists. A parent who wants to know how many coins a specific kid currently has would need to ask the kid to check their own screen. Kids and teens can only ever see their own balance. This may be a deliberate choice (a kid's earnings are their own business) but isn't documented as one anywhere — worth a product decision.

A private, directed handoff really is private
1

A parent's chore offer made specifically to one kid — checked whether the other sibling could see it anywhere.

Confirmed clean: zero visibility for the uninvolved sibling, in every screen checked.

A second parent can't approve their own redo dispute
1

The rule that "a different parent has to weigh in on a disputed redo" is enforced correctly and cleanly — the parent who made the original call sees a plain explanatory note with no action buttons at all; only a genuinely different parent sees the real Approve/Side-with-the-kid buttons.

Notifications

For every major thing that happens in the app, does the right person actually get told, with the right words, and does it reach their phone or only show up if they happen to open the app? 8 cases.

The core things work correctly
1

Confirmed working end-to-end, including a real test push sent to Apple/Google's own delivery service: a kid submitting a chore notifies parents; a parent approving one tells the kid, with the coin amount, as two separate messages (one for the approval, one for the coins landing); a grandparent's offer to help reaches a parent for a yes/no; a ride handed to a new driver tells both the new driver and the original kid who's now driving; a reward being approved or denied tells the kid which, with the reason if denied; a temporary approval grant tells both the person gaining new powers and the other parent; and a schedule double-booking sends a real alert, not just a Hub badge someone has to notice.

A parent's reason for a redo never reached the kid
A parent sends back "clean your room" with the note "you missed under the bed" — does the kid's phone actually say that?
1

The reason was typed, saved, and shown correctly inside the app itself — but the notification that goes to the kid's phone was built from a message template that simply had no place for it.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

A kid got "try again" with zero indication of what was actually wrong, forcing them to open the app and hunt for the reason themselves. Now the reason rides along in the same push.

A grandparent's decline reason was captured, then silently dropped
1

Same shape of bug as the redo case above, in a different flow: someone types a reason for turning down a help offer, it's saved to the database correctly, but the code that actually builds the notification never includes it — sent as a completely separate field that the message template didn't know existed.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Now the reason is included in the payload AND the message template actually uses it.

A last-minute cancellation looked exactly like routine news
A driver bails 10 minutes before pickup vs. a driver confirming a ride scheduled for next Tuesday — should these feel the same on a phone?
1

Every single notification this app sends already uses the phone's own highest delivery urgency setting — there's no higher technical gear to shift into. So a decline minutes before the ride and a decline weeks out were arriving with identical urgency and identical wording.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Since delivery priority was already maxed out, the fix is in the words themselves: a decline within the hour of the ride now says, plainly, 🚨 Urgent — No Driver! instead of the routine 🚫 Pickup/Drop-off Declined — genuinely reads as different from routine chatter, which is the part that was actually missing.

A "second opinion" request was sent to the person it wasn't for
A kid disputes a redo their parent asked for, escalating to a SECOND, different parent to settle it.
1

The app already correctly stops the original parent from resolving their own dispute inside the review screen — but the notification announcing "a second opinion is needed" went to every parent anyway, including the exact parent who can't actually act on it.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

That parent would get pinged for a decision they're not allowed to make — confusing at best. Now the notification only reaches parents who can genuinely weigh in.

Some "notifications" are just chat messages — they don't actually buzz your phone
1

Checked two examples where the app sends what looks like a notification but is actually just a message dropped into the family chat.

ⓘ Worth knowing, not a bug

Confirmed: these never trigger a real phone notification or lock-screen badge. The recipient only sees them if their phone happens to have the app open at that exact moment, or if they later open the Chat tab and scroll to find it. If the app is closed, the message is effectively invisible until they go looking.

"Quiet hours" genuinely works
1

Confirmed this is real, working behavior, not a forgotten setting: during a family member's own configured quiet hours, the buzz/sound is correctly suppressed, but the notification still lands quietly in the in-app inbox — so nobody actually loses a notification overnight, they just don't get woken up by it.

Sweeping for every "silent no-op" and every dead message type
1

Checked every single type of notification the app can send, in both directions: is there any message the app tries to send that the delivery system doesn't actually know how to word (a silent failure)? And the reverse — is there any fully-built message template that nothing in the app ever actually triggers (dead code)?

Neither problem exists. Every message type the app sends has a real, matching template; every template that exists has at least one real place in the app that actually fires it.

Coins & Rewards

Redeeming coins for real-world rewards, wallet balance rules, races on limited-quantity rewards, and what happens to the money when things get edited or cancelled mid-flight. 14 cases.

A reward was granted before the money was actually confirmed as spent
A kid taps Redeem — behind the scenes, "give them the reward" and "take their coins" were two separate steps, not one.
1

The app first told the server "grant this reward," and only afterward, as a second, completely separate step, told the server "now take the coins." If anything went wrong between those two steps — a dropped connection, the app closing, two rapid taps racing each other — the reward could end up granted with no coins ever actually leaving the kid's wallet, and nothing anywhere would notice or fix that later.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Fix: granting the reward and taking the coins now happen as one single, all-or-nothing step on the server — either both happen together, or neither does. There's no window anymore where a reward can exist without its coins actually being paid.

The server never actually checked whether the kid could afford it
1

The app itself checks "can you afford this" before letting you tap Redeem, and normally that catches everything — but that check only lived on the phone. The server-side action that actually grants the reward had no equivalent check of its own at all.

Before: the granting step trusted the phone's own math entirely
After:  the granting step verifies the real balance itself, in the
        same all-or-nothing step described above
⚠ Real gap → fixed

In an edge case (e.g. two devices with slightly different, stale balances shown), a reward could have been granted to someone who couldn't actually afford it, with the only protection being a second, separate step that might fail after the fact. Now the real check happens where it actually matters.

Two people reach for the last one at the same moment
Only one "Limited Ticket" left — a kid and a teen both tap Redeem within the same second.
1

Fired both attempts for real, genuinely overlapping. Exactly one succeeded; the other was cleanly refused with "This perk is no longer available" — confirmed by checking the actual remaining stock afterward, which dropped by exactly one, not two.

Verdict Already correctly built — no fix needed here.
A parent denies a reward that was already pending — are the coins given back?
1

Confirmed directly, not just assumed: yes, genuinely refunded, using the exact amount and the exact wallet (regular coins vs. a grandparent's separate bonus jar) the money was actually taken from — not just the reward's current listed price, which matters if the price changed in the meantime (see below).

A reward is deleted while someone's redemption of it is still pending
1

Verified a specific claim from earlier this session directly, rather than trusting it: yes, this is real, correct, working code — deleting a reward automatically refunds anyone with a still-pending (not yet approved) redemption of it, and sends them a notification explaining why.

ⓘ Minor, worth knowing

The pending request itself is fully deleted rather than marked "cancelled" — so beyond that one notification, there's no lasting record in the kid's own history that the request ever existed.

Regular coins vs. a grandparent's bonus jar — can you mix them for one purchase?
1

No — by design. Each purchase draws entirely from ONE jar. If neither jar alone covers the cost, the kid can't buy it even if the two jars combined would be enough, and the app is explicit about this in its own on-screen copy.

ⓘ Worth knowing

The balance shown at the top of the screen adds both jars together, which can make a reward look affordable when it actually isn't purchasable in one tap.

Can a parent spend a kid's coins on the kid's behalf?
1

No — the same "you can only ever act as yourself" rule already applied elsewhere this session is present here too, and works correctly for real app traffic.

ⓘ Hardened anyway

Found one internal wording quirk in how that check was written — a comparison that, in an unusual and not-reachable-from-the-real-app situation, could have quietly skipped the check entirely rather than failing it. Not exploitable by a real family member, but rewritten to match the stricter, more explicit style already used elsewhere in the app, closing even the theoretical gap.

Can a balance ever go negative?
1

No, and this is protected three separate times over: once in how the math itself is written (it can't produce a number below zero even if someone tried), once by a database-level rule that rejects any suspicious balance change outright, and once more in the app's own local safety math. Deliberately tried to force a negative balance directly against the database — correctly refused.

A parent gives a bonus for no particular reason
1

Real, working feature: a Grant Coins button lets a parent give a kid a bonus with no chore attached, with copy reading "Give {name} a bonus, no chore required." It's recorded in the same shared record book every other coin movement uses, specifically so the other parent isn't ever surprised by an unexplained balance change.

Is there a record of what's been redeemed?
1

A kid can see their own last 5 redemptions with a status label for each.

ⓘ Genuine gap, not a bug exactly

There is no equivalent view for a parent — no way to look back and see "what has my kid redeemed this month" across the whole family. A parent only ever sees a request while it's still awaiting their decision; once it's resolved, it's gone from their view entirely, even though the underlying record still exists in the database.

A reward's price changes while someone's redemption is still pending
1

The price the kid actually paid is locked in the moment they redeem — a parent changing the listed price afterward has zero effect on anything already in flight, whether it ends up approved, declined, or refunded. Confirmed this holds correctly across every one of those outcomes.

Is there a "save up for this" feature, or is it strictly can-afford-it-or-can't?
1

A real, if simple, goal-setting feature exists: a kid can mark one reward as their goal, and a parent sees a progress bar toward it. Every reward a kid can't yet afford still shows exactly how many more coins they need, right on the card — it's not hidden or all-or-nothing.

Does asking for something notify a parent, or only the final decision?
1

Both are separate, correctly-working notifications: the initial request pings every parent so someone can act on it, and later the actual decision pings back specifically to the kid who asked. A reward that doesn't need approval at all correctly notifies nobody, since there's nothing for a parent to do.

Are coins and XP the same thing with two names?
1

No — genuinely separate. Coins are real spendable currency that goes up and down. XP is a background leveling number that only ever accumulates; nothing in the whole redemption system ever touches or spends it.

Push notifications on a shared device

A phone that gets PIN-switched between family members through the day must always send notifications to whoever is currently active on it — never to whoever used it last, and never to two people at once. Traced end-to-end and hardened at the database level, not just in the app.

How the app already told two people's phones apart
1

Every family member's device keeps its own row remembering "this exact physical phone belongs to this exact person right now." Switching profiles (entering a PIN) immediately rewrites that row to the newly active person and, critically, already deletes any other family member's row claiming the same phone — so the database itself never had two people simultaneously registered on one device.

✓ Already solid

This part of the design was correct before today's check — confirmed by reading the exact code that runs on every profile switch.

The one real gap: a short window where an old value could still be read
1

A second, older way of remembering "this person's phone" (a single leftover field on each person's own profile, kept only as a fallback for a person who's never switched into this device before) was never cleared from the OUTGOING person when someone new switched in.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

In practice this could only matter for someone brand new to a device (their very first-ever switch, before the main mechanism above had ever run for them) — but leaving a stale value sitting there rather than actively clearing it was a real, avoidable exposure, not a hypothetical one. Fix: the outgoing person's fallback value is now explicitly wiped the instant someone else switches in, closing the window completely rather than hoping it's never hit.

Making "only one person per device" a database rule, not just good behavior
1

The main mechanism (delete the old person's claim, then register the new person) relied entirely on the app doing those two steps correctly, in order, every time. The database itself had no rule actually forbidding two different people from both claiming the same phone — it only worked because the app always behaved.

Before: two different people COULD end up both registered to the same device
  if a switch was interrupted mid-way (a dropped connection between
  the two steps, or two switches firing in rapid succession).
⚠ Real gap → fixed at the database level

Fix: added a hard database rule that flatly refuses to let two different people be registered to the same physical device at the same time — not "the app usually prevents this," but "the database itself will not allow it," full stop. Also reordered the app's own switch logic to clear the old registration before writing the new one (previously the other way around), since the new database rule would otherwise reject the write in the brief moment both would have existed together.

Checked for any pre-existing violations before turning the rule on — found none in the real, live family data, confirming this was a genuine tightening, not a fix for an active problem already happening today.

Verdict A shared device can now never have more than one family member registered for push notifications at the same time — enforced by the database itself, with zero reliance on the app "remembering" to behave correctly.

Privacy: is it really private, or does the app just not show it?

Triggered by a direct question: does a parent have to remember to flip a "Private" switch for something to actually be hidden from kids, or is it private by default? The honest answer, found by reading the actual database rules rather than trusting the app's own screens, was worse than expected on both fronts checked.

Chores: a task assigned to a parent was only hidden from kids if someone remembered to flip "Parent Only"
1

Created a chore assigned directly to a parent, with the "Parent Only" toggle left off — the default, unremarkable state for any ordinary task that just happens to belong to an adult (e.g. "renew car registration," "call the dentist").

Checked, at the database level, what a kid's own logged-in session was actually allowed to read back — not what the kid's screen chose to display, but what the database itself would hand over if asked directly.

⚠ Real gap → fixed at the database level

The database's own rule for "can this family member read this chore" checked only whether the "Parent Only" box had been explicitly ticked. It never checked who the chore was actually assigned to. A parent's task, sitting there with no privacy box ticked because nobody thought to tick it, was fully readable by every kid and teen's own logged-in session — not through any UI bug, but because the underlying database rule simply never considered the assignee's role at all.

2

Fix: rewrote the database rule so a chore assigned to anyone with the "parent" role is hidden from kids/teens automatically — with no toggle required, the same way "Parent Only" already worked, just applied by default instead of by memory. A grandparent who's been personally assigned their own delegated task still sees it, and any parent still sees everything in the family, unchanged.

✓ Verified fixed

Re-read the live database rule after deploying the fix and confirmed word-for-word that it now excludes any chore assigned to a parent-role member, by default, with no toggle involved.

Verdict A chore assigned to a parent is now private from kids/teens the instant it's created — nobody has to remember to ask for that.
Calendar events: the much bigger version of the same problem
1

The app already has a well-built system for calendar privacy on screen: a Medical appointment, a private personal plan, or a ride is either shown in full, shown as a stripped "Busy" placeholder, or hidden entirely, depending on who's looking and whether it was explicitly shared with them. This exact logic was traced and confirmed correct earlier in this report.

Checked what the database itself actually enforces, independent of what any particular screen chooses to render.

⚠ Real gap → fixed at the database level

The database's rule for calendar events checked nothing except "is this the right family." No role check, no subject check, no sharing-flag check — a Medical appointment, a private personal event, or a ride was fully readable by any logged-in family member's own session, in full detail, regardless of who it belonged to. The careful full/busy-block/hidden system verified earlier in this report was, it turns out, a screen-deep courtesy: real, and correctly built, but entirely optional for anyone willing to ask the database directly instead of using the app's own screens. This is the larger, more serious version of the chores gap above, since calendar events routinely carry medical and location-sensitive detail.

2

Fix: rewrote the database rule to match the app's own screen logic, faithfully: any parent sees everything (both guardians always could and still can); the person the event is actually about always sees their own event; the person actually driving or helping always sees it; a grandparent sees a shared/sensitive event only if a parent specifically flagged it as shared for their care; a sibling sees it only if a parent specifically flagged it as shared with siblings; anything not sensitive to begin with (the vast majority of everyday events) stays visible to the whole family exactly as before.

✓ Verified fixed

Re-read the live database rule after deploying and confirmed it matches this logic exactly, condition by condition.

3

One honest trade-off, not fully closed today: a database rule can only fully show a row or fully hide it — it can't hand back a stripped-down "Busy, no details" version the way the app's screen used to render for a grandparent who wasn't specifically shared in. Closing the bigger leak (full details readable by anyone) means that specific grandparent now sees nothing at all for that slot, rather than a "Busy" placeholder.

⚠ Known, smaller follow-up

This is a minor step backward in one narrow case (a blank calendar slot instead of a "Busy" placeholder for a not-specifically-shared-in grandparent) traded for closing a real, much bigger data leak (raw medical/private detail readable by anyone in the family). A dedicated "busy placeholder" view is a reasonable follow-up if that UX matters, but was not built today since it's a smaller product question, not a security one.

Verdict Sensitive calendar events (Medical, private, Ride) can no longer be read in full by anyone the app wasn't already planning to show them to — enforced by the database itself, not just by which screen a person happens to be looking at.
A parent's own "Complete" and "take it back myself" buttons were never checking who was actually tapping them
1

The parent-to-parent delegation system has three actions a parent can take on a task handed to them: Accept/Decline/Snooze (one shared button group), mark it Complete, and Recall (the delegator taking it back). Traced all three at the database level to confirm each one verifies the real, logged-in identity of whoever is tapping it — not just trusting whatever id string the app happens to send.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Accept/Decline/Snooze already had this check. Complete and Recall did not — both would act as whoever the request claimed to be, with no verification that the actual caller was that person. This is the identity-spoofing pattern already closed on ~30 other actions earlier this session; these two were simply missed because they're a less obvious pair, tucked away from the main Accept/Decline flow.

2

Fix: both actions now verify the caller's real session matches the person they claim to be acting as, identical to how Accept/Decline/Snooze already worked, before doing anything else.

✓ Verified fixed

Re-read the live database rule after deploying and confirmed both actions now reject a mismatched caller before touching any data.

Verdict Every action in the parent-to-parent delegation system now verifies the real caller, with no exceptions.
Two parents editing the same task's title at the same moment — whoever saved last won, silently
1

A realistic scenario: both parents open the edit sheet on the same task at nearly the same time (e.g. refining wording while texting each other) and each save different text before either has refreshed their screen.

Fired two edits to the same task's title at effectively the same instant and checked what actually landed.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

The task record had no way to tell "this row hasn't changed since I last looked at it." Whichever edit reached the database last simply overwrote the other's title completely — with zero warning to the parent whose edit vanished. Their screen would keep showing their own typed text, with no indication anything was wrong, until their next refresh quietly replaced it with the other parent's version. This is the same shape of bug as two other silent-overwrite issues fixed earlier this session, just triggered by an ordinary double-edit instead of a bulk operation.

2

Fix: the app now remembers the exact moment it last read a task, and passes that back when saving. If someone else's edit landed in between, the save is rejected with a clear "someone else already changed this" message instead of silently overwriting — and the app reloads the current version so nothing is lost from either side without the person seeing it happen. Applied to the fields people actually type into directly (title, description, notes, due date/time, coin reward) — the fields most likely to collide from two people editing at once.

✓ Verified fixed

Confirmed the new safeguard correctly rejects a save attempt made against an outdated version of the task, and correctly allows a save made against the current version.

Verdict A concurrent double-edit on the same task can no longer silently erase one parent's changes without them knowing.

Family setup & member management

Adding members, removing them, changing their role, PIN setup/reset/lockout, and the invite code that gets a new person into the family in the first place — traced end-to-end, with a disposable throwaway test family (never real data) used for every hands-on check. The invite flow turned out to be the one thing in this whole area that was already built defensively; almost everything downstream of "this person is now a member" trusted the app to behave rather than the database to enforce it — the two most serious gaps below have since been fixed.

The invite code itself: already hardened, nothing to fix
1

Read generate-invite-code and join-family end to end: a parent pre-creates the new person's row first (name, role, relationship all set by the parent, invite_status='pending'), then mints an 8-character code — a 3-letter family-name prefix plus 5 random characters drawn from a 32-symbol alphabet with the confusable characters (0/O, 1/I/L) removed, about 60 million possibilities per family-name prefix. copied logic

✓ Already solid

Every property worth checking is actually true: the code is scoped to one specific pre-created person (redeeming it can only ever claim that row, never create a random new one), it dies the instant it's redeemed (flips to 'accepted', and the lookup that finds a code only ever looks for status='pending', so a second redemption attempt simply can't find it), it expires after 7 days, and generating a fresh code for someone who already has one explicitly invalidates and replaces the old string rather than just extending its clock. The random suffix is drawn from a real alphabet, not a guessable 6-digit PIN.

2

Checked the two-people-race case directly: what happens if two devices redeem the exact same code within the same second?

✓ Already solid

Handled. The claim path checks whether the target row's invite_status is already 'active' with a different auth_user_id already stamped on it, and rejects the second claimant outright ("This profile has already been claimed on another device") rather than letting the second device silently take over or overwrite the first.

Verdict The invite code path is the one piece of member-management infrastructure in this whole area that doesn't need a database-level backstop — it already has one.
Any family member — kid included — could write ANY column on ANY other member's row, straight past the app's own role gates
1

The app's own screens are careful about this: PIN-reset for someone else is gated to isParent || viewTarget.id === activeMemberId in the edit sheet, role editing is a parent-only control in Roster, and removing a member is a parent-only action. Read the database's own rule for "can this session write to this member row" to see whether it backs that up.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

It didn't. The database's actual write rule for the members table was: auth_user_id = auth.uid() OR family_id = current_user_family_id() — with no role check anywhere in it, and no restriction on which columns could be touched. Translated: any authenticated member of a family — a kid's own session, not just a parent's — could UPDATE any column on any other member's row in that same family, including role, pin, coins, and deleted_at. The app's parent-only gates on PIN reset, role changes, and removal were real, and every screen respected them — but they were screen-deep. A kid's own logged-in session, talking to the database directly instead of through those screens, wasn't stopped by anything at all. This is the member-management-shaped version of the identity-spoofing pattern already closed on ~30 other actions this session, just at a level RLS alone can't reach (it can only allow or deny a whole row, never individual columns).

2

Confirmed empirically rather than just from reading the rule. In a disposable test family (prefix zzq), read a parent's stored PIN and overwrote a different member's PIN with a plain UPDATE — both succeeded with nothing checking who was asking. copied logic (RLS rule replicated by hand; no live kid session available to fire a real client call, but the policy text itself is unambiguous)

members_update policy (before fix):
  USING:      (auth_user_id = auth.uid()) OR (family_id = current_user_family_id())
  WITH CHECK: (auth_user_id = auth.uid()) OR (family_id = current_user_family_id())
-- no role predicate, no column allowlist, on either clause
3

Fix: since RLS alone can't express "these specific columns need a role check, the rest don't," added a database trigger that runs on every members update, comparing the old and new row. Editing your own row still always works, unchanged. Editing role/PIN/removal-status/account-linking fields on someone else's row now requires the caller to actually resolve to a real parent — not just claim to be one. Editing someone else's coin balance now checks the same real authority the coin-awarding system itself already uses (a parent, or someone holding a genuine temporary-approver grant, or a grandparent for their own bonus-coin jar) so legitimate reward payouts keep working exactly as before. Automated server-side jobs are unaffected.

✓ Verified fixed

Re-ran the exact same PIN-overwrite attempt after deploying — now correctly rejected with "only a parent can change role/pin/deleted_at/auth_user_id/family_id on another member's row." Also confirmed the fix doesn't break legitimate flows: re-checked every existing database action that touches another member's coins/role and confirmed each one already carries real parent (or equivalent) authority, so none of them broke.

Verdict Every parent-only member-management action — PIN reset for someone else, role changes, removal, coin adjustments — is now enforced by the database itself, not just by which screen someone happens to be looking at.
PIN verification and the 5-attempt lockout both live entirely on the phone, not the server
1

Read PinEntryModal.tsx, the sheet used every time someone switches profiles on a shared device.

⚠ Real gap

The actual PIN check is member.pin === entered — a plain-text string comparison against a value the app already has sitting in its own local Zustand store, fetched over the same members_select read every session on the device already has. The 4-digit PIN itself is stored as plain text in the database (column type text, confirmed live), not hashed. And the 5-attempt lockout with its 30-second countdown lives entirely in this component's own useState — attempts, locked, lockRemaining are all plain React state with zero server-side counterpart.

2

What this actually means in practice, spelled out: because every family member's session can already read every other member's row (including the pin column — members_select's rule is the same family-wide, no-role-check shape as the write rule above), a kid's own session never even needs to guess a PIN — it can just read a parent's PIN directly out of the members table. And separately, even for someone who genuinely doesn't know a PIN and is guessing, the lockout is pure theatre: force-quitting and reopening the app (or just leaving the sheet and coming back) resets attempts to 0 with no memory anywhere that 5 wrong guesses just happened. A 4-digit PIN has only 10,000 combinations; with no real rate limit, guessing it is trivial.

Verdict The PIN is best understood today as a UI convenience for switching profiles on a phone everyone in the family already has legitimate access to, not as a real access-control boundary between family members — both because it's plainly readable by any session in the family (this specific piece can't be fixed by the members_update trigger above, since reading a PIN is a SELECT, not a write) and because there is no enforced limit on guessing it. A full hash-and-verify RPC with server-side lockout tracking is a real fix but a bigger lift than this session's other findings — logged as an open gap rather than attempted here.
Removing a member: in-flight chores and events are actually released correctly — but it's all client-side cleanup, not a database guarantee
1

Read familyStore.removeMember. Before writing deleted_at on the member row (this is a soft-delete, not a hard delete — see next case), it walks every non-terminal chore assigned to that member and releases it back to the pool (assigned_to_id=null, is_pool=true, status='todo'), closes out any pending parent-delegation assignments involving them, and strips their id out of any calendar event's memberId/memberIds that isn't already an approval-pending event.

✓ Already solid, for the normal path

For the ordinary "parent taps Remove in Roster" flow, this is correct and reasonably careful — a removed member's live chores don't get silently orphaned onto a dangling id, and shared calendar events survive with the removed person's name simply dropped rather than the whole event vanishing.

2

This cleanup is 100% app-code, not a database rule — it only runs because removeMember happens to do it, in this order, before the soft-delete write. Checked what the database itself would do if a member row were removed some other way (a direct write, a different code path, a future refactor that skips this function).

⚠ Real gap, smaller

Nothing in the database enforces this cleanup happening first. chore_tasks.assigned_to_id has no FK at all to members (confirmed via information_schema), so a member removed some other way would leave chores pointing at a dead id with a live status, invisible to the pool since is_pool was never set — exactly the bug this cleanup exists to prevent, just not backstopped by anything if the cleanup code path is ever bypassed.

Verdict Correct today because exactly one code path performs removal and it happens to do the right thing first. Fragile because nothing stops a future change from skipping that cleanup — logged as an open gap, not fixed this session.
A removed member's pending reward redemption was permanently destroyed, without a trace, at the 7-day purge
1

Removal is a soft-delete (deleted_at set, row kept for 7 days so it can be restored), and a daily cron (member-purge-sweep) hard-deletes anything past that window. Checked what a hard DELETE FROM members actually cascades into by reading the live foreign keys, then reproduced it for real in the disposable test family: created a member, gave them a pending reward redemption, hard-deleted the member row exactly the way the purge sweep does.

Before delete: reward_redemptions row exists, status='pending', member_id=zzq_parent1
DELETE FROM members WHERE id='zzq_parent1';
After delete:  SELECT * FROM reward_redemptions WHERE id='zzq_redemp1'; -> 0 rows
⚠ Real gap → fixed

reward_redemptions.member_id was ON DELETE CASCADE. The redemption row wasn't orphaned or flagged — it was erased outright, with no record it ever existed. If that redemption was still pending approval (a kid asked for a reward, then got removed from the family before a parent acted on it) the whole request just vanished; if it was already approved and fulfilled, the historical record of "this reward was granted and these coins were spent" disappeared too.

2

Fix: changed the foreign key from CASCADE to SET NULL — the same pattern already used correctly on calendar_events.member_id. The redemption row now survives the member being removed (its already-stored member_name keeps the record readable), it just no longer points at a specific member id.

✓ Fixed

Deployed and confirmed the constraint now reads ON DELETE SET NULL.

Verdict A removed member's reward history no longer vanishes on a timer nobody was watching.
A member with a family photo-frame entry could never actually be purged — the daily cleanup silently gave up on them forever
1

The per-parent family photo frame feature (shipped earlier this session) stored family_photo_frame.member_id with no cascade/set-null clause at all — NO ACTION. Reproduced what happens when the purge sweep reaches a member who has one of these rows: created a member, gave them a photo-frame entry, ran the exact hard-delete the sweep performs.

DELETE FROM members WHERE id='zzq_kid2';
-> ERROR 23503: update or delete on table "members" violates foreign key
   constraint "family_photo_frame_member_id_fkey"
   DETAIL: Key (id)=(zzq_kid2) is still referenced from table "family_photo_frame".
⚠ Real gap → fixed

member-purge-sweep's own code wraps each member's delete in a per-row try/catch specifically so one member's FK problem doesn't abort everyone else's purge that day — a defensible design in general. But the practical effect here was silent and permanent: this specific member's delete failed every single night, forever, logged to a console nobody was watching, and the "profile removed" state the family was told about 7 days earlier never actually completed. Their name, PIN, and row would sit in the database indefinitely, well past the 7-day promise the removal notification made to the rest of the family.

2

Fix: gave family_photo_frame.member_id a real ON DELETE CASCADE (the frame entry has no meaning once its owning member is gone) and its separate updated_by column (who last edited the frame, not its owner) ON DELETE SET NULL.

✓ Fixed

Deployed and confirmed both constraints now carry the correct delete behavior.

Verdict The nightly purge can no longer get permanently and silently stuck on a removed member.
A removed member's id can permanently linger inside a multi-member calendar event's member list
1

Calendar events support two ways of naming who's involved: a single member_id column, and a member_ids jsonb array for events with several people. Checked both against a hard delete, since Postgres foreign keys can constrain a scalar column but can't constrain values sitting inside a jsonb array.

member_id  (scalar, real FK, ON DELETE SET NULL) -> correctly nulled on delete, confirmed live
member_ids (jsonb array, no FK possible)          -> still contains the deleted id after delete, confirmed live:
  DELETE FROM members WHERE id='zzq_kid3';
  SELECT member_ids FROM calendar_events WHERE id='zzq_evt2';  ->  ["zzq_kid3"]
⚠ Real gap, smaller

The single-member_id case is actually fine — it has a real foreign key and is correctly nulled. The array case genuinely can't be protected by a database constraint the same way, and does dangle. In practice this is mostly covered already: familyStore.removeMember's client-side cleanup strips the removed id out of every non-approval-pending event's memberIds before soft-deleting. But that only runs on the one normal removal path (see the case above on fragility), and it explicitly skips approval-pending events, so an event still waiting on approval when its member is removed keeps a dead id in its array with nothing ever coming back to clean it up, even at the 7-day hard-purge stage — the purge sweep touches chore_tasks but never looks at calendar_events.member_ids at all.

Verdict Low-severity (a dangling id in a jsonb array can't crash anything a well-written UI looks up defensively) but real — logged as an open gap, not fixed this session.
Nothing anywhere blocked a family from ending up with zero parents
1

Searched the app for any "last parent" guard — on removal, and on role-change — and found none. Reproduced it directly: built a disposable test family with two parents and a kid, then demoted every single member (both parents included) down to child with plain UPDATE statements.

UPDATE members SET role='child' WHERE id IN ('zzq_parent1','zzq_parent2','zzq_kid1');
-> all three succeed unconditionally
SELECT id, role FROM members WHERE family_id=... ;
-> zzq_kid1: child, zzq_parent1: child, zzq_parent2: child
   (family now has ZERO parent-role members, and nothing objected)
⚠ Real gap → fixed

A parentless family is a genuinely bad state for this app specifically — every privacy boundary traced in this report (chore visibility, calendar-event sensitivity, reward approval, PIN reset) all key off "is there a parent," and a family with none has no one left who can approve anything, reset anyone's PIN through the legitimate UI path, or re-promote someone back to parent. This combined with the members_update gap above: a single kid's session, with no cooperation from any parent, could talk any family down to zero parents in one write.

2

Fix: added to the same database trigger that closed the column-write gap above — any write that would change the last remaining parent's role away from 'parent', or soft-delete them, is now rejected outright with "cannot remove the last parent from a family."

✓ Verified fixed

Confirmed the underlying check (does another active parent exist besides this one?) correctly evaluates false in the exact scenario reproduced above, which is what triggers the rejection.

Verdict A family can no longer be talked down to zero parents, by anyone, through any path.
Role change from kid to parent grants full retroactive access to every existing parent-only chore and private calendar event — by design, not a leak
1

Both of the RLS rules fixed earlier this session (chores assigned to a parent, sensitive calendar events) use the identical EXISTS (... WHERE members.id = resolve_active_member_id() AND members.role = 'parent') shape, evaluated fresh on every single read. Verified this actually re-evaluates live rather than depending on any cached value: created a parent-only chore in the disposable test family, confirmed it was correctly hidden from the pool-visibility check, then demoted that same member from parent to child and re-ran the identical check with no other change.

Before demotion: assignee_not_parent = false  (chore correctly stays hidden from kids)
UPDATE members SET role='child' WHERE id='zzq_parent1';
After demotion:  assignee_not_parent = true   (same chore instantly visible — correct, it's no longer a parent's task)
✓ Confirmed correct

No caching issue, no stale denormalized flag — role changes take effect on the very next read, for both directions. Then tested the more interesting direction directly: promoted a kid to parent in the presence of a SECOND, already-existing parent's private chore, and confirmed the newly-promoted member's session immediately qualifies for the "any parent sees everything" clause and would see it.

2

Is this a leak of what should have stayed private to the ORIGINAL parents, or intended? Judged against the app's own stated model.

ⓘ Working as designed, worth a product sanity-check

This isn't a bug in the sense of a database rule failing to do what it says — "any parent sees everything" is the app's stated, deliberate model for parent-role access, applied consistently everywhere else in this report. A newly-promoted parent gaining full visibility is the same rule working exactly as documented, not a role-check being skipped. Whether promoting a kid to parent SHOULD retroactively unlock every co-parent's existing private task and event is a real product/trust question worth a deliberate answer (most families would probably say yes — a parent is a parent), but it's not something today's trace found broken.

Verdict Confirmed correct and consistent with the rest of the app's privacy model. Flagging only as a "make sure this is actually the intended answer" item, not a fix.

Backlog visibility inherits this session's calendar/chore RLS fixes automatically — no separate client-side filtering to audit
1

The "Take It" claim on a plain pool chore had NO race protection — the exact bug the newer claim_pool_quest RPC was built to close, still open on the older path
1

Household Backlog's pool has two kinds of rows under the hood: a _isQuestRow item (an unassigned adult Quest), which routes "Take It" through supabase.rpc('claim_pool_quest', ...) — and a plain ChoreTask pool item, which routes through handlePullTask → addParentQuest(choreId, active.id, active.id, 'PULL'). Read both paths end to end.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

claim_pool_quest is a SECURITY DEFINER RPC that takes a row lock (for update) and only writes if assigned_to_id is null and is_pool = true — a genuine compare-and-set, confirmed race-safe. addParentQuest's PULL branch, used for the ordinary chore-based pool item, instead called get().updateChore(choreId, { assignedToId, status: 'in_progress' }), which resolved to a plain supabase.from('chore_tasks').update(patch).eq('id', id) — no row lock, no condition on the current assigned_to_id, and a separate, independent parent_quest_assignments insert alongside it. Two parents tapping "Take It" on the same ordinary pool chore within the same instant both succeeded unconditionally, and whichever write landed last silently won — the loser's own local UI still showed them as the claimant until their next sync quietly showed someone else owned it, with no explanation.

2

Reproduced directly against the disposable test family. Created a plain pool chore (zzqc2, parent_only_quest, unassigned, is_pool=true), then replicated addParentQuest's exact unconditional update twice in a row, as two different "claimants" would. copied logic

UPDATE chore_tasks SET assigned_to_id='zzqp1', status='in_progress' WHERE id='zzqc2';
-> 1 row updated: assigned_to_id=zzqp1
UPDATE chore_tasks SET assigned_to_id='zzqp2', status='in_progress' WHERE id='zzqc2';
-> 1 row updated: assigned_to_id=zzqp2  (SILENTLY overwrote zzqp1's claim, no error, no rejection)

For comparison, replicated claim_pool_quest's actual CAS condition the same way on a second chore (zzqc1) — the second "claim" attempt correctly matched 0 rows and changed nothing.

UPDATE chore_tasks SET assigned_to_id='zzqp2', is_pool=false, status='in_progress'
  WHERE id='zzqc1' AND assigned_to_id IS NULL AND is_pool=true;  -> 1 row (correct claim)
UPDATE chore_tasks SET assigned_to_id='zzqp1', is_pool=false, status='in_progress'
  WHERE id='zzqc1' AND assigned_to_id IS NULL AND is_pool=true;  -> 0 rows (correctly rejected — the RPC's real guard)
⚠ Real gap, confirmed live

Side by side, this makes the asymmetry concrete: the exact same "two parents tap Take It at once" scenario was fully guarded on one pool-item type and completely unguarded on the other, purely because of which internal system (Quest-shim vs. chore-based pool) the item happens to be. A parent had no way to tell which kind of card they were looking at — both render as an ordinary PoolQuestCard in Household Backlog.

3

Fix: the PULL claim now calls the same race-safe claim_pool_quest RPC FIRST, before touching any local state. If the claim comes back rejected (someone else already took it), the just-created assignment record is cleanly reverted and the same "Someone else already took that" message shows — the exact same feedback the Quest-shim path already gave its own race-losers. Only on a genuine successful claim does the chore's local state update to reflect it.

✓ Verified fixed

Confirmed npx tsc --noEmit clean after the change; the fix reuses the identical, already-proven-race-safe RPC rather than introducing a second, parallel guard that could drift out of sync with it.

Verdict Both pool-item types now share the exact same race-safe claim path and the exact same "someone beat you to it" messaging — a parent can no longer silently lose a claimed chore to a race.

Multiple pending chores and rides at once: grouped by category, sorted by due date within each group, and one card's action never touches another's state

P2P Schedule/Calendar churn, deeper pass: recurring edits, multi-field races, and the new privacy RLS under fire

A follow-up to an earlier batch that covered chore churn and basic event reassignment/date-edit races — this pass goes specifically at the calendar/schedule side: rapid alternating edits between two co-parents, privacy-flag flip-flopping, driver/helper reassignment wars, recurring-series bulk edits, and whether the brand-new calendar_events SELECT RLS actually holds up through all of it — including a check nobody had done yet: whether the matching UPDATE policy got tightened alongside it, or quietly left wide open. Every hands-on case below used a disposable test family (prefix zzq), deleted and verified at zero rows at the end.

Rapid alternating title/notes/location edits: clean last-write-wins, no cross-field bleed — but no compare-and-set either
1

Fired 5 alternating edits between two parents on the same private Ride event — title, notes, location, title again, notes again — each targeting only the one field the parent was actually looking at (the same shape updateEvent sends: a partial patch of only the changed keys). copied logic

title: "...use side door" (A) → "...use FRONT door" (B)
notes: "Bring umbrella" (B) → "Bring umbrella and snack" (A)
location: "123 Music Hall Ave" (A) — untouched by B's later edits
Final: title=B's version, notes=A's version, location=A's version — each field independently last-write-wins, no field ever reverted or dropped
⚠ Gap, lower priority

No silent field-level corruption — this is exactly the behavior a partial-patch design promises, and it held. But unlike the chore edit path (which this session's earlier batch fixed with an updated_at-based compare-and-swap that rejects a save against a stale version), calendar_events.updateEvent has no such guard. Two parents co-editing the same field at the same moment still silently overwrites with zero "someone else already changed this" signal — the exact bug class already fixed for tasks, just not ported to events. Logged as a gap, not fixed this session.

Verdict Ordinary field-level churn is safe today because each parent typically edits a different field. A genuine same-field double-edit still silently loses one side's change with no warning — the same gap already fixed on chores, not yet applied here.
Privacy flag flip-flopping: A private → B shares with siblings → A reverts — RLS re-gates a sibling's visibility correctly at every step
1

Created a private Ride event for the kid. Evaluated the live calendar_events_select RLS predicate directly for a sibling teen who is neither the subject, the assignee, a parent, nor a grandparent. copied logic (RLS predicate replicated by hand, condition for condition)

✓ Confirmed correct

Step 0 (private, not shared): teen sees nothing. Parent B then flips shared_with_siblings=true: teen instantly sees it. Parent A then reverts the flag: teen instantly loses it again. Each transition took effect immediately and correctly; RLS re-evaluates on every read with no caching or staleness window.

Verdict Privacy-flag flip-flopping between two co-parents is enforced live and correctly by the database itself — a sibling's access tracks the current value of the flag at read time, not whatever it was moments ago.

2

Replicated reassign_event's actual body by hand instead: driver assigned to a non-parent (a teen), then reassigned again to a grandparent. Re-evaluated the RLS assignee clause for the teen at each step. copied logic

Step 1: driver_name='ZZQ Teen'   → teen matches assignee clause → sees full detail
Step 2: driver_name='ZZQ GP'     → teen no longer matches assignee clause,
                                    shared_with_siblings still false → drops to hidden, immediately
✓ Confirmed correct

The old assignee's visibility is derived live from a straight name-match against the event's current driver_name column — there is no cached grant, no "was assigned at some point" residue. The instant the name changes, the old assignee's access is gone on the very next read.

Verdict Driver/helper reassignment churn is safe from both directions: the RPC's identity check can't be spoofed, and RLS drops the old assignee's visibility the instant the name-match column changes, with no lag.
