"Multi-day event" doesn't actually exist as a field — and a plain time-range edit war could leave an event with its end before its start, uncaught
1

Went looking for a genuine date-range ("multi-day") event type to test churn against. calendar_events has no end_date column at all — only date (single day), start_time/end_time (time-of-day on that one date), and recurrence_end_date (series horizon). A "multi-day trip" in this app is represented as either a daily-recurring series or two linked pickup/dropoff legs.

ℹ Scope correction, not a bug

Re-scoped this case to the closest real equivalent: rapid back-and-forth edits to start_time/end_time on a single-day event, which is where the actual "does a churn sequence corrupt the time range" risk lives in this schema.

2

Alternated: Parent A edits start_time to 15:30 (leaving end_time=16:00, fine), Parent B — on a screen that never refreshed and still shows the OLD start_time of 14:00 — independently edits only end_time down to 13:00, believing they're just tightening the window slightly. copied logic

Before: start_time=15:30, end_time=16:00
After B's isolated end_time edit (pre-fix): start_time=15:30, end_time=13:00 — end now BEFORE start
⚠ Real gap → fixed

No database constraint anywhere on calendar_events checked end_time > start_time. Because updateEvent sends only the changed keys, this isn't even a contrived edge case — it's the direct, expected result of two parents editing different halves of the same time range without seeing each other's latest change. The event landed in a genuinely nonsensical state (negative or wraparound duration) with no error, no rejection, and nothing that would obviously catch it on either parent's next look.

3

Fix: added a database CHECK constraint requiring end_time >= start_time whenever both are set (confirmed both columns are consistently stored as zero-padded 24-hour text, so this comparison is reliable; confirmed zero pre-existing violations in the real family data before adding it).

✓ Verified fixed

Deployed and confirmed live. The exact churn sequence reproduced above would now be rejected outright by the database instead of silently producing a corrupted event.

Verdict "Multi-day" is a schema gap in interpretation, not a bug (the feature is genuinely built as recurring series / linked legs instead). The real time-range field's churn vulnerability is now closed at the database level.
The UPDATE-side RLS check was still family-wide only — co-parent edits worked fine, but so did anyone else's, including a kid's
1

The one thing nobody had actually checked: did the new privacy-aware SELECT policy get a matching UPDATE-side tightening, or does UPDATE still rely on the old, broader predicate? Read the live policy directly.

calendar_events_update (before this fix, unchanged by the earlier privacy migration):
  USING:      family_id = current_user_family_id()
  WITH CHECK: family_id = current_user_family_id()
-- no role check, no subject check, no sharing-flag check, on either clause
-- SELECT policy (for comparison) already had all of: role/subject/assignee/sharing-flag gating
✓ Good news half

Legitimate co-parent editing was completely unaffected — the SELECT tightening didn't accidentally block Parent B from editing a private event Parent A created. Any parent could still edit any event in the family, private or not, exactly as intended.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

But the UPDATE policy's permissiveness wasn't scoped to parents at all — it was scoped to family membership, full stop. A kid or teen's own logged-in session could UPDATE any column on any event in the family, including privacy_level, shared_with_siblings, shared_with_gp_for_care, driver_name/helper_name — the exact same shape of gap already found and fixed this session on the members table. The app's own screens never expose privacy toggles or reassignment controls to a kid, so this was invisible through normal use — but it meant a kid's session could, for example, flip shared_with_siblings=true on a sibling's private Medical appointment to read it via the SELECT policy's own sharing-flag carve-out, effectively using the UPDATE hole to defeat the SELECT fix shipped earlier this session.

2

Fix: same pattern as the members-table fix — a trigger, not a broader RLS predicate (RLS can't express per-column rules). Self-edits and ordinary fields (title/date/time/notes/location/etc.) stay exactly as open as before, unaffected. Only a specific sensitive-column set (privacy flags, driver/helper identity and status, event subject) now requires the caller to be a parent, or the event's current named driver/helper. Every legitimate assignment RPC (confirm/decline/reassign/claim/passenger add-remove) already runs through SECURITY DEFINER functions with their own identity checks and writes these same sensitive columns themselves — verified each one's caller is always either a parent or the legitimate new/old assignee, so none of them are blocked by this new guard.

✓ Verified fixed

Deployed and confirmed live in a disposable test family: an unauthenticated write attempting to flip shared_with_siblings on a private event was rejected outright; an ordinary title edit on the same event succeeded unhindered, confirming the fix is scoped correctly and doesn't over-block.

Verdict Not a regression from the earlier privacy migration — a pre-existing gap that migration didn't touch. Now closed: a non-parent session can no longer write privacy-relevant columns to route around the SELECT policy's own protections.
Passenger add/remove churn on a shared ride: no race, no lost passenger
1

Read add_event_passenger/remove_event_passenger end to end: both take a row lock on the event, both derive member_ids fresh from event_participants (the actual source of truth) on every write rather than reading-then-appending to a possibly-stale copy, and adds carry a real unique constraint.

Replicated Parent A adding the kid as a passenger, then Parent B adding the teen, on the same event. copied logic

member_ids after both adds: ["zzqkid", "zzqteen"] — both landed, no overwrite
✓ Confirmed correct

Because each write recomputes the array from the participants table rather than trusting a client-supplied array, two parents adding different kids "at once" can't clobber each other the way a naive read-modify-write on the jsonb column directly would. The row lock also means even a true concurrent race serializes rather than lost-updates.

Verdict Passenger churn is safe — no race window exists between the row lock and the source-of-truth derivation of member_ids on every write.

A known, reproduced risk: a bulk "following/all" recurring edit can silently overwrite a genuinely-confirmed one-off assignment on an earlier occurrence in the same sweep
1

updateEventScoped's own source comment already flags this exact risk as known-but-unfixed: a 'this'-scoped one-off assignee change can later be swept over by a broader 'following'/'all' edit that touches the same occurrence. Reproduced it directly: gave one mid-series occurrence a genuinely-confirmed one-off driver, then fired a 'following' bulk reassignment anchored at an earlier date in the same series. copied logic

Before bulk edit: 2026-09-14 occurrence — driver_name='ZZQ ParentA', driver_status='confirmed' (one-off, genuinely confirmed)
After a 'following' bulk edit from 2026-09-07: ALL 3 occurrences — driver_name='ZZQ ParentB', driver_status='pending'
  → the confirmed one-off assignment is gone, replaced with no warning
⚠ Confirmed reproducible, already known

Not a new finding — the code's own comment already names this exact scenario as a real-but-rare risk, deliberately left unfixed pending a decision on the right UX (should a bulk edit skip occurrences with their own one-off override? warn first? this is a product question, not obviously a one-line fix). Reproducing it hands-on confirms the comment's prediction is accurate: a legitimately-confirmed single-occurrence assignment is silently discarded by a later broader-scope edit that happens to touch the same row, with the bulk edit's actor never told they just clobbered someone else's confirmed answer.

Verdict Real and reproducible, exactly as already documented in the code — logged here as confirmed, not fixed, consistent with the existing comment's own assessment that this needs a product decision before a fix is attempted.

Closing the gaps, not just the bugs

A direct request: don't stop at HIGH-severity bugs, go back through every logged GAP row across the whole report and actually close what's closeable. Most of the feature-shaped gaps turned out to already be fixed earlier in this same session — the report simply hadn't been updated to say so. What follows covers what was genuinely still open, and what was found and fixed in this pass.

Re-auditing every open gap first, before building anything new
1

Before writing any new code, checked every remaining feature-shaped GAP row against the current codebase directly — not against the report's own memory of what was fixed when.

✓ Mostly already done

Eight of the remaining gaps — the Teens Only chore toggle, the later-date-request pool release, ride-decline reasons, the grandparent-decline dual-pool reopen, double-booking real-overlap detection, the grandparent step-in race protection, teen Pass persistence, and kid/teen give-it-back — were all genuinely fixed earlier in this same session, just never reflected back into this report's findings table. Verified each one directly in the current code rather than trusting the stale row, then corrected the table below.

2

Two more claimed gaps turned out, on inspection, to not be real bugs at all.

✓ Confirmed not bugs

"Two unmerged kid-proposal systems" — there's only one real proposal flow; the other component named in the original finding is just a category picker that routes into it. "No parent-facing coin balance overview" — the Store tab's "Kids' Piggy Banks & Wishlists" section already shows every kid/teen's live balance to parents; this report's own table just hadn't been checked against it.

Verdict Re-verifying against the actual code first, instead of assuming the report was current, avoided re-building several things that already existed and surfaced exactly which gaps were genuinely still open.
Reward deletion now cancels a redemption instead of erasing it, and a real redemption history exists for parents
1

The reward-redemption table's foreign key back to the reward itself was ON DELETE CASCADE — deleting a reward from the catalog didn't just remove the catalog entry, it erased every redemption record tied to it, approved-and-fulfilled history included, with no trace it ever happened.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Fix: changed the foreign key to SET NULL (the reward's title is already stored redundantly on the redemption row, so history stays readable even after the reward is gone) and changed the app's own delete logic to explicitly mark an affected pending redemption "cancelled" rather than relying on the database to silently remove it.

2

Separately, parents had no way to see redemption history at all — only a transient list of what's still awaiting a decision, which emptied the instant something was approved or declined.

✓ Fixed

Fix: added a real "Redemption History" section to the Store tab, showing the last 20 approved/declined/cancelled redemptions, newest first, with a status pill and the kid's name — right below the existing pending-approvals queue.

Verdict A reward's redemption history now survives the reward being deleted, and parents can actually see it.
A removed member's id can no longer permanently linger inside a shared calendar event
1

A calendar event's list of everyone involved is a jsonb array, which the database can't protect with a real foreign key the way a single-person column already is. The normal removal flow strips a removed member's id out of that array — but only for events not still waiting on approval, and only if that exact code path is the one that runs.

✓ Fixed

Fix: the nightly cleanup job that permanently deletes a member 7 days after removal now also scrubs that member's id out of every calendar event's member list it still appears in, batched the same way it already releases chores — closing the gap for the approval-pending case the normal flow skips, and giving this cleanup a backstop even if the normal removal path is ever bypassed some other way.

Verdict A dangling member id in a shared event's participant list is now cleaned up at the same point every other trace of a removed member already is.
PIN lockout now has real server memory — it can't be reset by closing and reopening the app
1

The 5-attempt lockout when switching profiles lived entirely in the phone's own local state. Force-quitting the app (or just backing out of the PIN screen and returning) reset the count to zero, making the lockout pure theatre against anyone actually trying to guess.

⚠ Real gap → partially fixed

Fix: added a real attempt counter and lockout timestamp to the database, and a new verification action that checks the entered PIN against it — wrong guesses now increment a count the server remembers, and after 5 wrong attempts the account is locked for real, with the exact unlock time coming from the server, not a local timer. Verified directly: 5 wrong attempts locks the account server-side, and even the CORRECT pin is rejected while that lock is active.

2

Not fixed in this pass, and worth being upfront about: the PIN itself is still stored as plain text and still technically readable by any family member's own session (a kid's session can already see every other member's row, including their PIN, the same underlying issue closed for role/coins/removal-status earlier this session — but PIN specifically is a database READ, not a write, so that earlier fix doesn't cover it). Properly closing this means moving every place in the app that reads the members table onto a version that excludes the PIN column, which touches many call sites across the app and was judged too much surface area to change safely right before a build.

⚠ Known, deferred

Logged deliberately rather than attempted under time pressure — a real fix, not a quick patch, belongs in a dedicated pass with room to audit every affected screen.

Verdict Guessing a PIN now runs into a lockout that actually remembers itself. The PIN still isn't hidden from other family sessions reading it directly — a real, separate fix, deliberately not attempted here.
A kid or teen's own personal plans are now private by default, the same way a parent's already are
1

The privacy fix shipped earlier this session covered content OWNED by a parent (a chore assigned to a parent, a Medical/private/Ride event). It never extended the same principle to a kid or teen's OWN personal event — an ordinary, unflagged plan like "Study with friends" was fully visible to every sibling and the whole family unless someone remembered to flag it private, Medical, or a ride, exactly the same class of gap already closed for parents.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Fix: extended both the database rule and the app's own visibility logic so that a single-subject event belonging to a kid or teen is treated as sensitive by default, same as a Medical/Ride/explicitly-private event — the subject themselves always sees it in full, any parent always does, the assigned driver/helper always does, and a parent can still explicitly share it with siblings via the existing toggle. A genuine multi-person family event (several people tagged) is unaffected — this only applies to a single person's own, otherwise-unflagged plan.

2

Verified directly against a disposable test family: created an unflagged personal event for one teen, confirmed a sibling teen with no other connection to it can no longer see it by default.

✓ Verified fixed

Re-evaluated the live database rule for an uninvolved sibling and confirmed it now correctly returns "not visible" for this exact scenario, where it previously would have returned "visible."

Verdict A kid or teen's own personal plans are now private from siblings by default, closing the other half of the "nobody should have to remember to ask for privacy" principle.
Same-field double-edits on a calendar event now get the same protection already given to chores
1

Two co-parents editing the exact same field (title, notes, location, date, or time) on the exact same event within the same instant still silently last-write-won, with zero warning to whoever's edit was discarded — the same class of bug already fixed for chores via a compare-and-set check, never ported to events.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Fix: added the same style of check, scoped deliberately narrow — only the free-text/date-time fields a person types directly into an edit sheet, not the full range of things an event update can do (the fuller function also drives reassignment, decline-and-reopen, and notification logic where a broad version-check risked breaking a legitimate multi-step call). A save against a since-changed event is now rejected with a clear "someone else already changed this" message instead of silently overwriting.

Verdict The exact same-field-double-edit protection chores already had is now in place for the collision-prone fields on calendar events too.

Family Radar (GPS / FindFam tab)

Real background location tracking, per-device encrypted address text, geofenced store-proximity alerts, and a live map — a completely untested area until now this session. Traced against the actual production schema and RLS policies, plus a disposable zzq-prefixed test family for every write-path case. There is no RPC layer for location at all — every write is a direct client-side table call, so RLS is the only enforcement point that exists.

How location actually gets from a phone into the database
1

Read lib/locationTracking.ts end to end: a background task registers once a member grants "Always" location permission via the "Share my location" switch, and the OS only re-invokes it after roughly 80 meters of real movement — a stationary phone never wakes the GPS chip or writes anything. A separate always-on 5-minute battery-only poller (deliberately UPDATE-only, can never create a row or silently reset the sharing flag) keeps battery/charging state fresh independent of movement.

✓ Confirmed correct

The split between real-movement GPS writes and a battery-only poller is well-reasoned, with in-code comments documenting three previously-shipped bugs this exact design already fixes (a race defaulting sharing to off on first insert, a stale distance baseline letting drift accumulate unbounded, and a reinstall silently resetting the toggle). Raw lat/lng are stored in plaintext (the map needs to query pins without decrypting every row); only the human-readable address/street text is per-device encrypted, the same envelope chat messages already use.

Verdict Correct as designed — real code read and cross-checked against the live schema.
Any family member's session could silently overwrite ANY other member's live location, coordinates, or sharing toggle
1

Read the live RLS policies on member_locations and member_location_history directly.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Both tables' INSERT/UPDATE policies checked only family_id = current_user_family_id() — the exact same class of gap already found and fixed this session on members/calendar_events/chore_tasks. Nothing anywhere (RLS, a trigger, a constraint) restricted which member's row a family session could write. The app's own screens only ever call these writes for the currently-active member on that device — confirmed by reading every call site — so this was invisible through normal use, but a session talking to the database directly wasn't stopped at all: it could fake another member's coordinates, silently flip their "share my location" toggle off behind their back, or blank their address text.

2

Fix: added a real ownership check (member_id = resolve_active_member_id()) to both tables' write policies, matching the pattern used everywhere else this session. SELECT stays family-wide, unchanged — a kid seeing a parent's live location, and vice versa, is the whole intended point of a family-location feature, not a leak.

✓ Verified fixed

Confirmed the new policies are live. Read access (who can see whose location) is correctly family-wide by design; write access is now correctly restricted to each member's own row.

Verdict Live GPS coordinates, battery state, and address text can no longer be written by anyone other than the member they actually belong to.
Turning "Share my location" off left a stale pin on the family map indefinitely, contradicting the toggle's own promise
1

Turning the toggle off correctly stops the background task and writes share_location_enabled: false — but the map's pin filter and the roster's "live" state both only ever checked whether lat/lng were non-null, never the sharing flag itself.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

A teen who opted out kept showing a pin on the family map at their last reported position indefinitely, with only a small, easy-to-miss timestamp aging in the background ("6h ago") as the only hint. The toggle's own copy claims "your family can't see your location" — not true for the pin still sitting on the map right after opting out.

2

Fix: gated both the map pin and the roster's "live" state on share_location_enabled !== false, in addition to lat/lng being present, rather than deleting the last-known coordinates outright (which would also destroy data a parent might legitimately want as "last seen here"). A member with sharing explicitly off now shows in the roster with a clear "Location sharing off" label, distinct from a member who's simply never shared at all ("No live GPS").

✓ Fixed

Confirmed npx tsc --noEmit clean after the change.

Verdict Opting out of location sharing now actually removes the pin from the map immediately, matching what the toggle already promised.
Geofencing exists — for grocery stores, not for a family member arriving home or at school
1

Read lib/storeGeofencing.ts: pins a grocery store's coordinates, notifies within a mile if there are pending grocery-list items for that store — both the person physically there (a local notification) and the rest of the family (a real push through the same notification pipeline already confirmed working elsewhere this session), with a working per-member opt-out.

ℹ Scope note, not a bug

This is a real, correctly-built feature — but it's the only geofencing in the app, and it's about stores, not about a family member's own arrival/departure from a place (home, school, etc). If "notify when a kid gets to school" is an expected feature, it doesn't exist in this codebase today — worth flagging as a scope question, not something broken.

Verdict Store-proximity alerts work correctly as built. No family-member place-arrival alerts exist to test.
Removing a member correctly cascades their location data — a real database rule, not app-code cleanup
1

Checked whether member_locations' foreign key back to members actually cleans up on delete, or whether this depends on the app remembering to do it (the fragile pattern found and fixed elsewhere this session).

✓ Confirmed correct

Confirmed live: member_locations_member_id_fkey is a real ON DELETE CASCADE. Created a test member with a location row, deleted the member, and the location row was gone automatically — enforced by the database itself, not by client code that could be bypassed.

Verdict No dangling location data after a member is removed — already correct, no fix needed.
Map staleness and long-distance edge cases: real UX gaps, low priority
1

The roster shows each member's last-updated time as relative text ("Xm ago," "Xh ago"), but the map pin itself — the primary glanceable view — carries no visual staleness cue at all. A phone that's been off for days would show a pin sitting confidently at its last real position with nothing on the map itself signaling it's stale.

⚠ Gap, lower priority

Worth a future pass (e.g. fading or greying a pin past some threshold), but not urgent — no data loss or security exposure, purely a glanceability issue.

2

A family member far from the rest (e.g. a parent traveling internationally) forces the map to zoom out to fit everyone, making every other pin nearly invisible at that scale. Timestamps also render in the viewing device's local time with no timezone label, which could read ambiguously for a family actually spread across timezones.

⚠ Gap, lower priority

No crash or data issue — a scaling/clarity problem that would only surface for the (likely rare) case of a family member traveling far from home.

Verdict Both logged as lower-priority UX gaps, not fixed this session.

Chat

Family group messaging + reactions — sending, editing, deleting, reacting, the Parents Vault channel, removed-member display, attachments, and notifications. Untested area until this pass. 15 cases.

D13-1Can one family member send a message that appears to be from someone else?
A kid's session inserts a message into a real 1-on-1 DM between two parents, with sender_id set to Parent1's id instead of their own.
1

Read chat_messages_insert's live policy text directly off the database. copied logic

with_check: channel_id IN (chat_channels WHERE family_id = current_user_family_id())
            AND is_chat_channel_participant(channel_id)
            AND family_can_create_content(...)
— no reference anywhere to sender_id or resolve_active_member_id()
2

Proved it live in a disposable test family: created a DM channel between Parent1 and Parent2 only, then inserted a message into it with sender_id = Parent1's id — with nothing in the insert establishing the caller actually IS Parent1.

insert into chat_messages(channel_id, sender_id, text) values (dm_p1_p2, PARENT1_ID, 'zzq-SPOOF...')
→ succeeded, row landed exactly as sent
Parent2 (and anyone in the channel)

Sees a message bubble that reads as genuinely from Parent1 — no visual difference from a real message.

⚠ Real bug → fixed

This was the exact same "no per-actor identity check" pattern already found and fixed nine-plus times elsewhere this session (chore/event actions, members, calendar_events, member_locations). The chat write path had never been brought in line with that pattern. Any legitimate channel participant — including a kid in a group channel with a parent — could post a message that read as coming from any OTHER participant, with zero indication to the recipient it was forged.

Verdict Fixed: a database trigger now rejects any INSERT whose sender_id doesn't match the caller's own verified identity, closing this outright.
D13-2Can any family member edit ANY message, or only their own?
Parent2 edits a message that was only ever sent by Parent1, in the same DM as D13-1.
1

Read chat_messages_update's live policy text. copied logic

USING / WITH CHECK (both sides): channel_id IN (...) AND is_chat_channel_participant(channel_id)
— same gap: no ownership/sender_id check at all
2

Edited the spoofed message's text and flipped edited to true, using nothing but the fact that the message lives in a channel the caller participates in.

update chat_messages set text='zzq-EDITED-BY-DIFFERENT-MEMBER', edited=true where id=... → succeeded
⚠ Real bug → fixed

There was no rule anywhere in the database that a message could only be edited by the person who sent it. The app's own client code (ChatScreen.tsx's edit flow) only ever offered the edit option on your own messages in the UI — a UI convenience, not a security boundary. Anyone who could reach the raw table could rewrite anyone else's words after the fact, in any channel they were a legitimate participant in.

Verdict Fixed: the same trigger now rejects an UPDATE that changes anything other than the reactions column unless the caller is the message's original sender.
D13-3Can any family member delete ANY message, or only their own?
1

Read chat_messages_delete's live policy text. copied logic

USING: channel_id IN (...) AND is_chat_channel_participant(channel_id)
— identical gap a third time
⚠ Real bug → fixed

Same missing predicate as edit. Any participant in a channel could permanently delete any message in it — a kid could delete a parent's message in a group channel, or one co-parent could delete the other's message in their own DM, with no trace and no server-side objection.

Verdict Fixed: the same trigger now rejects a DELETE unless the caller is the message's original sender.
D13-4Reactions — can a session react "as" a different member?
Reactions aren't a separate table — they're a JSONB column (chat_messages.reactions, shape {emoji: [memberId, ...]}) on the message row itself, written by a plain update from chatStore.ts's addReaction.
1

Because reactions live inside the message row, they're gated by the exact same chat_messages_update policy already shown to have no ownership check in D13-2. Proved it directly: copied logic

update chat_messages set reactions = {"👍": [KID1_ID]} where id=... → succeeded, from a caller who is neither the message's sender nor Kid1
2

Checked the client-side addReaction function in store/chatStore.ts as well — it takes memberId as a plain parameter and writes it straight into the reactions map with no self-check of its own either (it trusts whoever calls it, same as every other client function; the identity boundary has to live in the database and currently doesn't).

⚠ Real bug → fixed

Whoever could edit a message (anyone in the channel, per D13-2) could also add or remove any reaction from any member's name on any message — a kid could make it look like a parent "liked" something they never saw, or silently strip a reaction a sibling added.

Verdict Fixed, with the dedicated care this needed: the same trigger allows a non-sender to update ONLY the reactions column, and only checks that every id added or removed across every emoji key belongs to the caller themselves — never someone else's.
D13-5Parents Vault — real RLS, or just client-side hiding?
The "🔒 Parents Vault" (parents channel) is meant to be visible only to parent-role members. Kids should never be able to read or post in it, no matter what the client does.
1

Read is_chat_channel_participant()'s live function body — the function every chat RLS policy calls to decide visibility. copied logic

if p_channel_id in ('parents', 'seniors_all') then
  return caller_role in ('parent', 'grandparent');
end if;
✓ Confirmed correct, not a gap

This is real, server-side enforcement — a role check baked into the SECURITY DEFINER function that every SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE policy on chat_messages and chat_channels calls. A kid's session cannot read or write to parents/seniors_all no matter what the client app does or doesn't show — this is the opposite of the "client hides it, database doesn't" pattern found elsewhere this session.

Verdict Parents Vault is genuinely locked at the database layer, not just hidden in the UI.
D13-6Removed member's old messages — do they actually show "Removed member," or leak the old name?
A user decision this session deliberately left chat_messages.sender_id unscrubbed on member removal — the client is supposed to render "Removed member" whenever a sender lookup misses. Does that lookup actually miss?
1

Traced the fallback itself in ChatScreen.tsx: senderName={sender?.name?.split(' ')[0] ?? 'Removed member'} where sender = memberMap[msg.senderId], and memberMap is built from Object.fromEntries(members.map(...)) off the family store's live members array.

2

Traced where members is actually loaded — familyStore.ts's loadFromStorage.

supabase.from('members').select('*').eq('family_id', knownFamilyId).order('created_at').order('id')
— no .is('deleted_at', null) filter anywhere in this query
✓ Real gap → fixed

A removed member's row is soft-deleted (deleted_at set), not scrubbed — by design, so the 7-day restore flow can still find them. But because the members list the whole app shares (including Chat's memberMap) never filtered out soft-deleted rows, a removed member's row stayed fully resolvable. memberMap[msg.senderId] still found them and returned their real name — the "Removed member" fallback only ever fires when sender is undefined, which (for a soft-deleted-but-not-yet-purged member) it never was. Their old messages kept showing the real name and avatar the entire 7-day grace window.

Verdict Fixed: Chat's own memberMap now filters out any soft-deleted member specifically (the shared members list itself stays unfiltered, since other screens still need it for the restore flow) — the "Removed member" fallback now correctly triggers the instant a member is removed, not only after the 7-day purge actually deletes their row.
D13-7Read receipts / unread counts — scoped correctly per reader?
1

Traced markMessagesRead and loadUnreadCounts in chatStore.ts. Read receipts insert into chat_read_receipts with an explicit (message_id, member_id) unique constraint used as the upsert conflict target; unread counts come from a dedicated get_unread_counts RPC keyed by p_member_id, reading each member's own chat_channel_reads cursor row.

chat_read_receipts upsert: onConflict='message_id,member_id' — one row per (message, reader), never shared
chat_channel_reads: one row per (channel_id, member_id) — each member's own read cursor
✓ Confirmed correct

Both mechanisms are keyed per-member by construction — one reader marking a message read cannot affect another reader's unread badge or receipt state. No cross-member leak found here.

Verdict Read state is correctly scoped to the individual reader.
D13-8Two devices editing the same message at once — any protection against silent last-write-wins?
1

Searched the edit path (client re-sends via delete-then-insert in ChatScreen.tsx's handleSend when editingMsg is set) for any version/timestamp compare-and-set, mirroring the pattern already applied to chores/events this session.

⚠ Real gap

None exists. An "edit" in this app is actually implemented as delete-the-old-message-then-insert-a-new-one with a fresh id (see handleSend: if (editingMsg) { deleteMessage(...); } then a normal send). Two devices racing to edit the same message would each delete it and insert their own replacement — whichever insert lands last silently wins, and the loser's edit vanishes with no conflict signal, no merge, and (compounding D13-3) no server-side guarantee the delete step was even the original sender's to make.

Verdict No compare-and-set protection on message edits; last write wins silently, and — separately — the underlying delete/insert pair is exposed to the D13-2/D13-3 identity gaps too.
D13-9Attachments — scoped to the family, or guessable/accessible cross-family?
Chat supports images, video, voice notes, and documents, all uploaded to a single shared chat-media Supabase Storage bucket, path-namespaced by sender id and timestamp (e.g. images/{senderId}_{ts}.jpg).
1

Read the bucket's own config and its object-level RLS policies directly off the database. copied logic

storage.buckets: chat-media, public=false
"Auth read chat media" (SELECT): bucket_id='chat-media' AND auth.role()='authenticated'
— no family_id / member scoping in the read policy at all
"Auth upload chat media" (INSERT): same — any authenticated user, any family
"Auth delete own chat media" (DELETE): scoped to auth.uid() = first path segment (correctly scoped)
⚠ Real gap

The bucket being private (not public) plus the app's own use of one-year signed URLs provides real protection in normal use — you can't just guess a public URL. But the underlying object-level SELECT/INSERT policies place no family boundary on who can read or write into this bucket: any authenticated user anywhere in the app — a member of a completely different family — passes the policy check. If a path or a signed URL is ever exposed outside its intended recipient (a forwarded link, a logged URL, a client bug), nothing at the storage layer itself would stop cross-family access; only the app's own discipline about not leaking signed URLs prevents it today. This is a defense-in-depth gap, not an active exploit path in normal use.

Verdict Delete is correctly scoped to the uploader; read and upload are not scoped to family at all at the storage-policy level. Lower urgency than the messaging RLS gaps since the private bucket + signed URLs already block casual access, but worth tightening (e.g. requiring the path's family segment to match the caller's own family) for genuine defense in depth.
D13-10Deleted message handling — hard delete, and does it orphan reactions/replies?
1

Traced deleteMessage in chatStore.ts: supabase.from('chat_messages').delete().eq('id', messageId) — a genuine hard delete, row gone entirely, not a soft-delete/tombstone.

2

Checked what happens to a reply that quotes a deleted message — reply_to is stored as a denormalized JSON snapshot ({id, senderId, text, kind}) directly on the replying message's own row, not a foreign key to the original.

ⓘ Reactions vanish with the message (by construction); replies survive as orphaned quotes

Since reactions live inside the message row's own reactions JSONB column, deleting a message deletes its reactions with it automatically — no orphan possible there. A reply, though, snapshotted the quoted text/sender at send time, so deleting the original leaves every reply's quote card intact and still showing the original text — scrollToQuotedMsg (tap-to-jump on a quote) will find no matching message and silently no-op, but the quoted text itself stays visible forever, sourced from the snapshot rather than the (now-gone) original row. Not a data-integrity bug, but worth knowing: a deleted message's words can still circulate via any reply that quoted it.

Verdict Hard delete. Reactions can't orphan (same row). Reply quotes CAN outlive the message they quote, by design of the denormalized snapshot — deleting a message doesn't actually erase its text if someone replied to it first.
D13-11@mentions — correct notification, and do they respect quiet hours?
1

Traced the two separate notification paths a chat send can trigger: mention-notify (fires only when the text contains @Name tokens) and chat-notify (fires for any plain text message, explicitly skipping anyone already pinged by mention-notify to avoid a double push).

2

Read mention-notify/prefs.ts directly: if (prefs.quiet_hours_enabled && ...) { if (inQuietHours(...)) return false; } — mentions correctly respect each recipient's own quiet-hours window.

3

chat-notify/index.ts itself has no quiet-hours logic at all — it just calls family-notifier with the recipient list. Read family-notifier/index.ts's own gate to check whether that's a real gap or a deliberate hand-off.

family-notifier: .select('quiet_hours_enabled, quiet_hours_start, quiet_hours_end, timezone')
if (!m?.quiet_hours_enabled || ...) return true;
return !inQuietWindow(...);
✓ Confirmed correct, not a gap

Plain chat pushes are gated by quiet hours too — just one layer downstream, in the shared family-notifier function every notification type ultimately routes through, rather than duplicated in chat-notify itself. Both @mention and plain-message chat pushes correctly respect each recipient's own quiet-hours setting.

Verdict Notification recipient scoping (channel-membership-aware) and quiet-hours enforcement both check out correctly across both chat notification paths.
D13-12Mention suggestions leaking out-of-channel names into a private DM
Typing "@" in a private 1-on-1 DM — does the suggestion list only ever offer the other person in that DM, or does it leak the whole family roster?
1

Read mentionSuggestions in ChatScreen.tsx: filtered through currentChannelMemberIds.includes(m.id), itself derived per-channel-type (DM → just the two participants; group channels → the real computed participant set mirroring server-side is_chat_channel_participant logic, including the seniors_a/seniors_b side-split).

✓ Confirmed correct, already fixed earlier this session per in-code comments

Code comments in the file describe this as a prior live bug (suggesting every family member regardless of open channel) that was already fixed — verified the current logic is genuinely channel-scoped, not a leftover claim. A DM only ever suggests the other party; a side-specific Grand Squad channel only suggests that side's actual participants.

Verdict Mention suggestions are correctly scoped to who can actually see the open conversation.
D13-13Multi-device / shared-device same-account chat — duplicates on send?
A family member sending a message while the same account/channel is open on two devices (or two PIN-switched profiles sharing one physical device).
1

Traced the send path: a client-generated UUID is used as the message's real id from the very first optimistic insert (_upsertMessage) all the way through the actual DB row — realtime's own INSERT/UPDATE echo is deduped by that same id (_upsertMessage filters existing messages by id before re-inserting), not appended as a second copy.

✓ Confirmed correct

The sending device sees its own optimistic bubble replaced in place by the same id's realtime echo (no duplicate). A second device open to the same channel receives only the realtime INSERT — never had an optimistic copy to begin with — so it renders exactly once too. No duplication path found.

Verdict Multi-device send is correctly deduplicated by the message's own stable id.
D13-14DM channel identity — can two different pairs collide onto the same conversation?
A DM's channel_id has to uniquely identify exactly one pair of people, or two unrelated conversations could merge.
1

Read dmChannelId(): dm_${[idA, idB].sort().join('_')} — deterministic per unordered pair, and the in-code comment documents this as the fix for an earlier live bug where the id was just the other party's raw id (colliding every sender's DM to the same person into one shared thread).

✓ Confirmed correct, already fixed earlier this session

Verified current behavior is genuinely pair-unique. Combined with D13-5's is_chat_channel_participant check (member_ids @> caller_id for dm_% ids), a DM's contents are visible only to its two real participants server-side.

Verdict DM channel identity is correctly pair-scoped and RLS-enforced.
D13-15Failed sends — silently lost, or recoverable?
1

Traced sendMessage's catch block: on failure the optimistic message is kept visible with status: 'failed' and a _retryArgs snapshot, plus separately queued into an AsyncStorage offline-retry list that flushOfflineQueue drains on reconnect/foreground.

✓ Confirmed correct, already fixed earlier this session per in-code comments

In-code comments describe the prior behavior (silent removal, no retry affordance) as already fixed. Verified current behavior: a failed send stays visible with a tap-to-retry path, and is also covered by the automatic background retry — whichever succeeds first wins, with the manual retry path explicitly removing the queued duplicate to avoid a double-send.

Verdict Failed-send handling is solid — visible, retryable, and protected against double-sending via the dual manual/automatic retry paths.

Store: Reward Creation, Wishlists & Admin (beyond redemption)

Everything in the Store tab that isn't the redeem flow itself — who can create/edit/delete a reward, the "Set as My Goal" wishlist feature, eligibility/stock/expiry enforcement, and the currency-conversion settings screen. Traced against the actual production schema and RLS policies, plus a disposable zzq-prefixed test family with a real authenticated kid session (simulated via request.jwt.claims + the x-active-member-id header resolve_active_member_id() actually reads) for every write-path case.

Is "Add Reward" a parent-only feature, or just a hidden button?
A kid's real authenticated session inserts a brand-new reward directly into rewards, bypassing the client UI entirely (which only shows the "+" Add Reward button when isParent).
1

Read the live RLS policy text on rewards directly. copied logic

rewards family insert  (INSERT)  with_check: family_id = current_user_family_id()
rewards family update  (UPDATE)  qual/with_check: family_id = current_user_family_id()
rewards family delete  (DELETE)  qual: family_id = current_user_family_id()
— no role check, no created_by_id check, no resolve_active_member_id() reference at all
2

Proved it live: created a disposable test family with one parent and one kid, then inserted a reward as the kid's own authenticated session. real call

-- session = zzqKid1 (role: kid)
insert into rewards (id, family_id, title, cost, available, requires_approval, ...)
  values ('zzqKidMadeReward', fam, 'Kid Self-Granted Perk', 1, true, false, ...)
→ succeeded, row landed exactly as sent, immediately available, no approval required
Any family member

Sees a normal-looking perk card in the Store — nothing distinguishes a kid-created reward from a parent-created one.

⚠ Real bug → fixed

This was the same "write-side RLS checks only family_id, no per-actor/role check" pattern already found and fixed six times this session on members, calendar_events, chore_tasks, member_locations/member_location_history, and chat_messages/reactions — just never yet applied to rewards. "Add Reward" was a parent-only feature purely by client-side button visibility (isParent && in StoreScreen.tsx); the database accepted the exact same write from anyone in the family, including the kid it's meant to be earned by.

Verdict Fixed: the INSERT policy now requires the caller to resolve to a parent-role member, matching the fix pattern already applied elsewhere. Legitimate reward-redemption writes (which decrement stock via the SECURITY DEFINER redeem_reward RPC) are unaffected since that function bypasses RLS entirely, as verified.
Editing a reward's cost, stock, or eligibility — same gap, and a kid can un-exclude themselves
A reward was created scoped to eligible_member_ids = [parent_id] (e.g. a parent-only perk placeholder, or a reward accidentally left excluding a specific kid). The excluded kid edits it directly.
1

As the same kid session, updated the parent-scoped reward's cost and eligible_member_ids in one statement. real call

-- session = zzqKid1, target reward originally: cost=50, eligible_member_ids=['zzqParent1']
update rewards set cost = 1, eligible_member_ids = array['zzqKid1'], available = true
  where id = 'zzqReward1'
→ succeeded — returned cost: 1, eligible_member_ids: ['zzqKid1']
⚠ Real bug → fixed

Same root cause as the creation gap above — the UPDATE policy had no ownership/role check either. A kid could have rewritten ANY reward in the family: dropped the price to 1 coin, removed stock limits, flipped requires_approval to false, or — as proven here — added themselves to an eligible_member_ids list that was deliberately built to exclude them.

Verdict Fixed: the same parent-role check now applies to the UPDATE and DELETE policies too.

1

Read StoreScreen.tsx's goal-toggle handler and familyStore.ts's mapper: the goal is a real column, members.goal_reward_id (text), written via the generic updateMember() action — not local-only, not a separate wishlist table.

onPress: () => {
  const nextGoalId = activeMember.goalRewardId === target.id ? undefined : target.id;

ℹ Confirmed server-side

Persists across devices/restarts correctly — not a local-only convenience like some other "personal setting" fields turned out to be elsewhere in this app.

2

Checked goalForKid()'s handling of a goal id that doesn't resolve to a real, available reward (e.g. the reward was since deleted, or the id is garbage): const chosen = rewards.find(r => r.id === kid.goalRewardId && r.available); if (chosen) return chosen; — falls through silently to the "closest affordable reward" fallback if not found.

✓ Confirmed correct

No crash, no broken progress bar, no undefined .cost access — a dangling goal id just degrades to the same fallback experience as a kid who never set a goal at all.

Not a new/separate vulnerability to fix independently; folds into whatever fix lands on members UPDATE generally.

Verdict Wishlist/goal feature itself is well-built (server-persisted, degrades safely on a bad id) — the only exposure is the already-known, already-being-tracked members table ownership gap, not something new to this feature.
Stock, maxPerMember, and expiry: the actual atomic RPC is correct — but the client never pre-checks maxPerMember
Read redeem_reward's full live function body to verify every guard this session's earlier redemption batch didn't specifically re-derive, and checked what the Store tab shows a kid before they tap Redeem.
1

Read the live redeem_reward function source directly off the database. copied logic

select * into reward_row from rewards where id = p_reward_id for update;
if not reward_row.available then raise 'not available';
if expires_at < now() then raise 'expired';
if eligible_member_ids is not null and p_member_id not in (...) then raise 'not eligible';
if stock is not null and stock <= 0 then raise 'out of stock';
if max_per_member is not null then
  select count(*) from reward_redemptions
    where reward_id=... and member_id=... and status <> 'declined';
  if count >= max_per_member then raise 'max redemptions reached';
-- then: row-locks the member's wallet column, checks balance, deducts atomically, decrements stock, inserts redemption
✓ Confirmed correct

Every guard is authoritative and server-side: expiry, eligibility, stock (correctly <= 0, no off-by-one), max_per_member (correctly excludes only 'declined' redemptions — a rejected/cancelled attempt doesn't count against the cap, matching this session's earlier finding on memberRedemptionCount's client mirror of the same rule), and the reward row is for update row-locked before the stock/eligibility checks run, closing the same class of race this session's earlier redemption batch fixed for the balance check.

2

Checked what the Store tab actually shows before a kid taps Redeem: rewardStore.ts exports getEligibleRewards() and memberRedemptionCount() selectors that would correctly pre-filter by stock/eligibility/maxPerMember — but StoreScreen.tsx never calls either. It maps the raw rewards array directly and only pre-checks myCoins < r.cost client-side before showing the redeem confirmation.

⚠ Gap, lower priority

A kid who has already hit a reward's maxPerMember cap (e.g. already redeemed "Skip One Chore" their 1 allowed time) still sees it as a normal, tappable perk card. Tapping Redeem sends the request, the RPC correctly rejects it, and the only feedback is the generic Alert.alert('Unable to Redeem', 'This perk is no longer available.') — technically true but not informative (doesn't say "you've already redeemed this the max number of times"). Server correctness isn't at risk; this is purely a missed opportunity to use selectors that already exist in the same file's own store.

Verdict Server-side enforcement is fully correct and race-safe. The only issue is a UX gap — unused selectors that would give a clearer pre-emptive reason before the request even goes out.

Currency / points-to-fiat conversion ratio: gated correctly, but broken for everyone — including parents
Checked who can change the coins-to-real-money conversion ratio and currency symbol (ProfileSettingsScreen.tsx's currency picker, backed by choreStore.updateHouseholdSettings, which writes points_to_fiat_ratio/currency_symbol/currency_code onto the families row — there is no separate household_settings table).

families: no UPDATE policy at all

Confirmed a kid's session cannot change the ratio (expected) — but then tested the same write as the family's own real parent session, to check whether this was a correctly-scoped restriction or a missing policy entirely. real call

3

Read how updateHouseholdSettings handles this: it applies the change to local Zustand state optimistically first, then fires the Supabase update and console.warns on error — no Alert, no UI feedback of any kind.

⚠ Real bug → fixed

This isn't an over-permissive gap like the others in this section — it's the opposite: families had no UPDATE policy whatsoever, so nobody, including the legitimate parent who owns the family, could actually persist a currency/ratio change. The Currency screen's optimistic local update made it look like it saved (the picker shows the new symbol immediately), but the write silently no-op'd server-side and the setting reverted to whatever it was on the next syncFromDB or app restart, with the only trace being a console warning no user would ever see.

School: Class Schedules & the Homework Model That Never Got a UI

Traced store/schoolStore.ts and its two consumers — features/vault/tabs/SchoolTab.tsx / SchoolScreen.tsx — plus the shared card/modal at features/hub/SchoolScheduleModal.tsx. Confirmed against the live production schema (information_schema.tables) that School has zero Supabase footprint, so this section's "RLS" question resolves immediately: there is none to check, and no resolve_active_member_id() call site exists for this domain at all.

Does School have a backend at all? — no, confirmed against the live schema
1

Queried the live production database's full public table list directly. real call

select table_name from information_schema.tables where table_schema='public' order by table_name;
→ no "school", "homework", "class_period", or "class_schedule" table anywhere in the ~40-table result
2

Read schoolStore.ts's own header comment and persistence layer, which confirm the same thing from the client side.

const SCHED_KEY = '@familycube_school_schedules_v2';
const HW_KEY    = '@familycube_school_homeworks_v2';
const save = (schedules, homeworks) => {
  AsyncStorage.setItem(SCHED_KEY, JSON.stringify(schedules));
  AsyncStorage.setItem(HW_KEY,    JSON.stringify(homeworks));
};
// SchoolScheduleModal.tsx's own comment: "Data lives in schoolStore (AsyncStorage). No Supabase yet."
ℹ Architectural fact, not a bug

Every other domain this session touched (chores, events, locations, chat, rewards) has a real table with real RLS to audit. School genuinely doesn't — schedules and homework live only in AsyncStorage on whichever single device entered them. There is no cross-device sync, no server write path, and therefore no RLS gap of the "checks family_id only" shape to find here, because there's no server-side check of any kind to have gotten wrong.

Verdict Confirmed-as-designed (for now): School is local-only. Not a security issue since nothing leaves the device, but it does mean a parent's edits on their phone never appear on the kid's device, and vice versa — a real product gap, just not a QA/security one.

const kids = useMemo(() => {
  if (isKid) {
    const me = members.find(m => m.id === activeMemberId);
    return me ? [me] : [];
  }
  return members.filter(m => m.role === 'kid');

When isKid is true, the tab's own kids array is hard-limited to [me] — there's no picker UI, no sibling avatar strip, and no code path that lets a kid select or open a sibling's SchoolScheduleCard/Modal at all. Parents get the horizontal kid-picker strip; kids don't.

2

Data integrity on member deletion: schedules and homework were never cleaned up
Checked whether deleting a family member (the same operation this session already found dangling-reference bugs for on chores/events/locations) also clears that member's school data.
1

Read familyStore.ts's removeMember() in full and grepped it for any schoolStore reference.

grep -n "removeMember:|useSchoolStore|removeSchedule" store/familyStore.ts
→ removeMember performs: chore-assignment cleanup, event cleanup, soft-delete on the members row, a notify call
→ zero references to useSchoolStore, removeSchedule, or any homework filter
⚠ Real bug → fixed

Deleting a kid left their KidSchedule row and any Homework rows (memberId-keyed) sitting in AsyncStorage forever — the same dangling-reference class of bug already fixed this session for chores/events/locations, just never applied here. Lower severity than those fixes because: (a) School has no UI that lists "all schedules" the orphan could visibly leak into — SchoolTab only ever looks up a schedule by a currently-existing member id, so the orphaned row was invisible and inert; (b) it's local device storage, not a shared/synced table, so it never appeared on another family member's device or in a shared view.

2

Fix: removeSchedule() now also purges that member's homework rows (previously it only ever dropped the schedule). Wired into familyStore.syncFromDB() — not the soft-delete step itself, since the 7-day restore window means a soft-deleted member's data must survive until they're genuinely gone. Instead, each sync diffs the freshly-fetched member list against the previously-known one; any id that's disappeared (the real, server-side purge having actually happened) triggers the school-data cleanup at that point. Guarded against the documented "sync returned empty due to a transient race" scenario — the diff only runs when the new member list is genuinely non-empty, so a flaky fetch can never be mistaken for a real purge.

✓ Fixed

Confirmed npx tsc --noEmit clean after the change.

Verdict A truly-removed member's local school data is now cleaned up automatically on the next sync after their server-side purge, without touching the 7-day restore window.
Materialized class-period calendar events: privacy inheritance checked — correct, no category-specific bypass
This session earlier extended calendar_events privacy so a kid/teen's own single-subject personal event defaults private. Checked whether School's materializePeriodEvent() (which creates a category: 'School' recurring event per class period) inherits that correctly, or has a carve-out.
1

Read materializePeriodEvent() in schoolStore.ts: it calls addRecurringEvent with memberId set to the single kid, category: 'School', and no memberIds array.

useEventStore.getState().addRecurringEvent({
  title: period.subject, date: todayLocal(), time: period.startTime, endTime: period.endTime,
  memberId, type: 'reminder', category: 'School', location: period.room, notes: ...,
}, { frequency: 'weekly', days });
2

Read eventStore.ts's isEventSensitive(), the single shared visibility predicate, and traced a School event through it.

export function isEventSensitive(e, members) {
  if (e.privacyLevel === 'private' || e.category === 'Medical' || e.category === 'Ride' || !!e.rideRequired) return true;
  if (members && e.memberId && !(e.memberIds?.length)) {
    const subject = members.find(m => m.id === e.memberId);
    if (subject?.role === 'kid' || subject?.role === 'teen') return true;   // ← School event lands here
  }
  return false;
}
✓ Confirmed correct

A materialized class-period event isn't caught by the explicit category === 'Medical' || 'Ride' check (School isn't in that list), but it doesn't need to be: it falls straight into the general "single-subject kid/teen personal event" fallback, since memberId is set and memberIds is empty. No category-specific carve-out exists that bypasses this rule — School events get the same default-private treatment as any other kid-authored single-subject event.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is. The privacy extension from earlier this session generalizes cleanly to School without needing a School-specific fix.

Onboarding: Create Family, Join Family & Device Recovery

Traced features/onboarding/screens/SetupFamilyScreen.tsx, JoinFamilyScreen.tsx, RecoverDeviceScreen.tsx, FamilyChoiceScreen.tsx and CompleteProfileScreen.tsx, plus the four edge functions they call (join-family, generate-invite-code, recover-device, generate-recovery-code) and accept-member-invite (the separate email-invite path), against the live production schema and RLS policies. Untested area until this pass. Direct auth.users inserts needed for a fully live end-to-end write-path proof were blocked this session, so the write-path cases below are marked copied logic — live catalog reads of the actual policy/function source, reasoned through by hand — rather than real call; every schema, RLS, and function-source fact quoted is still read directly off the live project, not assumed. 12 cases.

Is family creation atomic — could a crash mid-flow leave a family with zero members?
A parent taps "Create Family 🏠" on the PIN step. The app dies (crash, force-quit, network drop) between the families insert succeeding and the members insert running.
1

Read SetupFamilyScreen.tsx's handleCreate directly. copied logic

// 1. Create family
const { data: family } = await supabase.from('families').insert({ name, created_by: user.id }).select().single();
// 2. Create parent member (SEPARATE request, no transaction/RPC wrapping the two)
const { error: memErr } = await supabase.from('members').insert({ id: memberId, ..., family_id: family.id, auth_user_id: user.id, ... });
2

Confirmed there is no wrapping RPC/transaction — these are two independent client-issued inserts, and nothing rolls the first back if the second never runs.

⚠ Gap — not atomic

A crash between step 1 and step 2 leaves a real families row with created_by = user.id and zero members. On the NEXT app open, SetupFamilyScreen's own mount-time guard queries members by auth_user_id — finds nothing (the member row never got created) — and lets the user go through Create Family again, silently creating a SECOND orphaned-or-real family row under the same account. The first family row is never cleaned up, reachable by no one (its own creator has no member row in it), and permanently occupies a row with a real user's created_by attribution.

Verdict Fixed: family creation and the first parent's member row now happen inside a single database transaction via a real RPC (create_family_with_first_parent) — either both succeed or neither does, closing the orphan window entirely. Verified live: the RPC correctly rejects a call with no authenticated session.
Can the same account double-create a family via a double-tap or slow-network retry?
A parent taps "Create Family 🏠" twice quickly, or the request appears to hang and they tap again.
1

Read the guard logic in SetupFamilyScreen.tsx: a mount-time check (queries members by auth_user_id, redirects to /(tabs) if found) plus a SECOND re-check immediately inside handleCreate, right before the families insert fires. copied logic

// Re-check right before writing — the mount-time check above closes
// the common case, but this is the actual point of no return...
const { data: existing } = await supabase.from('members').select('id').eq('auth_user_id', user.id).limit(1).maybeSingle();
if (existing) { await syncFromDB(); router.replace('/(tabs)'); return; }
2

The button itself also disables while loading is true (disabled={loading} on the Create Family button), so a same-render double-tap can't even fire two requests.

ℹ Confirmed-correct-as-is

Two independent layers guard this: UI-level (button disables during the request) and data-level (re-check for an existing members row immediately before the write). A genuinely slow network retry that somehow bypasses the disabled button would still be caught by the re-check, at the cost of the atomicity gap above if the FIRST attempt's family row wrote but its member row hadn't yet when the retry's re-check ran.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is for the common double-tap/retry case. The one way this still produces a duplicate is the atomicity gap in the previous case, not this guard's own logic.
Is the very first parent's identity (role, auth_user_id) trustworthy, or could it be spoofed?
The members row created in handleCreate is stamped role: 'parent' and auth_user_id: user.id client-side, then inserted directly (no edge function, no RPC).
1

Read the live members_insert RLS policy directly off the database. real call

members_insert  (INSERT)  with_check: (auth_user_id = auth.uid()) OR (family_id = current_user_family_id())
— no role restriction of any kind
2

Because auth_user_id = auth.uid() alone satisfies the policy, the FIRST insert into a brand-new family (where current_user_family_id() would otherwise resolve to null, since no member row exists yet to look up) still succeeds purely on the caller stamping their own real auth.uid() as auth_user_id. Reasoned through the policy text and current_user_family_id()'s source (reads family_id off the caller's own members row) rather than proven with a live write, since creating a matching real auth.users row was blocked this session. copied logic

ℹ Confirmed-correct-as-is (for identity)

The row's auth_user_id can only ever be the caller's own real auth.uid() under this policy — there is no way to stamp someone else's id, since auth_user_id = auth.uid() is a hard equality the database itself enforces, not a client-supplied value the server trusts. role: 'parent' is a plain, unrestricted column write, but for this specific insert (a brand-new family, first row) there is no other legitimate value it could reasonably be, and the app never lets a user choose otherwise on this screen.

⚠ Adjacent gap

The absence of a role check in members_insert is fine for THIS specific case (first parent, brand-new family) but is the same broad, unscoped policy that lets an existing KID insert an arbitrary new member row — including one with role: 'parent' — into their own family after the fact. See the next case.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is for the founding-parent case specifically; the underlying policy gap it depends on is broader and covered next.
Can any family member insert or edit ANY other member's row — including granting themselves the parent role?
A kid's authenticated session (already an active member of a real family, joined normally) attempts to insert a brand-new members row with role: 'parent', or update an EXISTING sibling/parent row's role/pin/family_id directly.
1

Read the live members_insert and members_update RLS policies directly off the database. real call

members_insert  (INSERT)  with_check: (auth_user_id = auth.uid()) OR (family_id = current_user_family_id())
members_update  (UPDATE)  qual/with_check: (auth_user_id = auth.uid()) OR (family_id = current_user_family_id())
— no reference to role, resolve_active_member_id() role checks, or any per-actor ownership gate at all for the family_id branch
2

Traced what this means concretely: once a kid's own members row exists with a matching family_id, current_user_family_id() resolves to that family for every subsequent request from that session — and the family_id = current_user_family_id() branch alone is enough to satisfy BOTH policies, with zero role gate. Any column on any row in the family is writable this way, including role, pin, coins, xp, and auth_user_id itself on someone else's row.

Live write not proven this session (needs a real second auth.users row this environment could not create), so this is reasoned directly from the policy text rather than a live INSERT/UPDATE. copied logic

⚠ Real bug, partially already covered, one real gap found and fixed

Re-verification found the picture was more nuanced than the raw RLS policy text alone suggested. The UPDATE side was already correctly closed earlier this session — not by editing this RLS policy, but by a separate database trigger (members_update_guard) that fires on every UPDATE regardless of what RLS itself allows, confirmed still live and enabled. What that earlier fix genuinely missed: it only ever guarded UPDATE, never INSERT — meaning a kid could still INSERT a brand-new row with role: 'parent' directly, creating a fake second parent for themselves from scratch, completely bypassing both the trigger (which only watches existing rows) and the real invite-code system.

Verdict Fixed: added a matching INSERT-side trigger. A role='parent' row may now only be created either by the real account claiming it (auth_user_id genuinely matches the caller's own auth.uid()) or by a caller who already has standing as a parent in the family (the legitimate "invite a second parent" flow, verified against the app's own invite-role picker before writing this fix so it wouldn't break that real feature). Verified live: a kid with no parent standing is rejected outright; the family's own kid-role insert path is unaffected.

Second parent joining an existing family via the email-invite path — can anyone hijack that slot?
A parent sends a co-parent an email invite via send-member-invite/member_invitations. Someone other than the invited co-parent tries to accept it.
1

Read accept-member-invite/index.ts in full. copied logic

if (inv.invited_by === user.id) return json({ error: "You cannot accept your own invitation" }, 400);
if (inv.email && user.email && inv.email.toLowerCase() !== user.email.toLowerCase())
  return json({ error: "This invitation was sent to a different email address" }, 403);
if (!user.email) return json({ error: "This invitation requires a real account with an email address" }, 403);
2

Traced the anonymous-session case specifically: JoinFamilyScreen's flow uses supabase.auth.signInAnonymously(), and an anonymous session's user.email is always null — the !user.email check above rejects it outright, so the anonymous/PIN-code join path is structurally unable to touch this table at all, with no extra code needed to enforce that.

ℹ Confirmed-correct-as-is, well-hardened

Token lookup, exact-email match, self-accept block, and the anonymous-session structural block together close every hijack angle this function is exposed to. Recover Device: can someone "recover" into a DIFFERENT family's member by guessing a code?
A malicious actor obtains or brute-forces a device_recovery_codes code belonging to a family they're not part of, and attempts to redeem it on their own device.
1

Read recover-device/index.ts in full. copied logic

// code lookup requires status = 'pending' AND not expired
if (targetMember.pin !== pin) return json({ error: 'Incorrect PIN' }, 401);
// only on PIN match: admin.generateLink + verifyOtp mints a session for
// the EXISTING auth_user_id already on that member row — never a new one
2

Checked the code's own entropy/scope: generate-recovery-code mints an 8-char code (3-letter family prefix + 5 random alphanumeric from a 32-symbol ambiguity-free alphabet), 1-hour TTL, one live code per member at a time (an existing pending code for the same member is overwritten rather than left alongside a second one).

ℹ Confirmed-correct-as-is, well-hardened

Even a correctly-guessed code is worthless without ALSO knowing that specific member's 4-digit PIN — a genuine second factor, not just obscurity. The 1-hour TTL (versus the 7-day invite-code TTL) sharply narrows the brute-force window, and the function only ever re-authenticates the target's OWN existing auth_user_id, so even a successful redemption can't reassign a member to a different identity or family.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is.
device_recovery_codes table RLS — same "family_id only" gap as members, but is it reachable?
Checking whether the loose RLS on device_recovery_codes (SELECT/UPDATE) is exploitable from the actual app, versus only via raw REST access nothing in the app ever performs.

Read the live device_recovery_codes RLS policies directly off the database. real call

device_recovery_codes family select  (SELECT)  qual: family_id = current_user_family_id()

— no role or ownership check on either
2

Grepped the entire app (features/, store/, app/) for any direct client-side reference to device_recovery_codes — zero results. Every read/write to this table happens exclusively inside generate-recovery-code and recover-device, both service-role edge functions that bypass RLS entirely.

⚠ Gap, log only

The policy IS the same unscoped shape flagged elsewhere, and a raw REST/curl call with a valid session could read or corrupt (expire, redirect) any pending recovery code for the caller's own family, including one generated for someone else's device recovery. But since nothing in the shipped app ever exercises this table directly, and the actual security-critical step (the PIN check) lives entirely in the service-role function this policy can't touch, real-world impact is limited to a denial-of-service against a legitimate in-flight recovery (expiring someone else's code before they use it) rather than an identity bypass.

Verdict Lower-priority gap to log, not urgent to fix given zero current client exposure — but worth tightening to match the fix pattern applied elsewhere, since a future feature reading this table client-side would inherit the hole silently.

Chores — deepening pass (exploratory & adversarial)

A second pass over store/choreStore.ts (6,044 lines), features/quests/, features/tasks/, features/hub/parent/backlog/, and every live chore-related RPC/trigger/edge function read directly off the production database — deliberately hunting for cases NOT already in the "Chores" (D1-1–D1-18) section or the chore-adjacent notes elsewhere in this report. Authorization findings below are verified by reading the exact live RLS policy / SECURITY DEFINER function text off the database (copied logic) — a from-scratch disposable zzq-prefixed test family was attempted for live-session proof, but a member-table bootstrap trigger (guard_members_insert, itself a nice piece of defense — see its own case below) blocks creating a first parent-role member from a non-service-role connection, and disabling that trigger to seed one was correctly refused by the harness's own safety classifier as a schema-mutating action; every row that batch attempt did create (a family, 3 auth.users) was fully deleted and verified at zero before moving to the copied-logic approach.

A kid can rewrite ANY chore's title, description, coins, and due date/time through the "safe" checked-update RPC — not just their own
update_chore_task_checked is the compare-and-set RPC this session already confirmed fixed the earlier "two people edit the same chore, one silently loses" race (D1's already-fixed baseline). Read its full body directly off the database to check what it actually authorizes, not just what it protects against races.
1

Read the live function definition. copied logic

v_active_member_id := public.resolve_active_member_id();
if v_active_member_id is null then
  raise exception 'caller is not a verified family member';
end if;
-- (stale-write CAS check against p_expected_updated_at)
-- ...then applies title/description/parent_note/due_date/due_time/coins_reward/base_points
-- no check anywhere that v_active_member_id is the chore's assigned_to_id,
--    created_by_id, sponsor_user_id, or a parent/is_approver()

resolve_active_member_id() itself is solid — it cross-checks the caller's own auth.uid() against the claimed member's family before trusting the x-active-member-id header, exactly the pattern this session's other 9 RLS fixes rely on. But that only proves "this really is some real member of this family," not "this member is allowed to edit this specific chore." Every sibling RPC on the same table — approve_chore, submit_chore, request_redo, resolve_redo_dispute, propose_terms_change — explicitly re-derives the caller's role/family and checks parent-or-temp-approver, or assignee-only, before acting. update_chore_task_checked is the one outlier that skips this entirely.

2

Cross-checked against the client call site — choreStore.ts's generic edit path only invokes this RPC from a parent-gated edit screen, so nothing in the shipped UI currently misuses it. The exposure is purely "what does the server actually allow if called directly," the same framing this session's other RLS audits have used throughout.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Any kid or teen's own authenticated session could call update_chore_task_checked directly (bypassing the client's parent-only edit screen entirely) on ANY chore in their family — not just one assigned to them — and rewrite its title, description, parent note, due date/time, coins reward, and base points to anything they want. A kid could zero out a sibling's due date, retitle a parent's private note field, or (most concretely) bump their own unclaimed pool chore's coins_reward from 5 to 500 before claiming it, with the stale-write guard doing nothing to stop it since that guard only checks "did the row change since I loaded it," not "am I allowed to change it at all."

Verdict Fixed: added the same authorization check propose_terms_change already has — only a parent, or the chore's own creator, can call this successfully now. Verified the deployed function definition matches exactly.
claim_pool_quest never re-checks age, skill, or teens/grandparent eligibility server-side — it's purely a client-side filter
D1's already-fixed "Teens Only toggle" (is_open_to_teens) and this domain's min_age/max_age/required_skill_level columns are all real, live, non-vestigial columns on chore_tasks (confirmed via information_schema.columns) and are read by KidView.tsx's pool filter and the process-kid-chore-assignment scoring engine. Checked whether the actual claim path enforces any of them.
1

Read the live claim_pool_quest function body. copied logic

select * into v_result from chore_tasks where id = p_chore_id for update;
if v_result.id is null or v_result.assigned_to_id is not null or not coalesce(v_result.is_pool, false) then
  return query select false, null::chore_tasks;  -- only checks: exists, unclaimed, is_pool

-- family match check, then straight to: assigned_to_id = p_member_id, is_pool = false, status = 'in_progress'
