⚠ Real gap — not fixed

A young kid's own session calling claim_pool_quest directly on a chore flagged is_open_to_teens=true (meant to hide it from kids entirely — this session's own already-fixed feature), or on one with min_age=13, or one requiring required_skill_level=4 the kid doesn't have, would succeed. The RPC only verifies "is this pool item still open" and "is the caller really in this family" — every actual eligibility rule this app has built (teens-only, GP-only, age range, skill floor) lives exclusively in the UI's own filtering, with no server-side backstop at the one function that actually hands out the claim.

Verdict Real bug, not yet fixed — the exact "server trusts the client's own pre-filtering" shape this session's Store-tab audit already flagged once (max-per-member) but here there's no RPC-level rejection at all, not even an unhelpful generic error; the claim would simply succeed.
A kid who loses a redo dispute can still get force-paid on their very next resubmit
Traced the full redo-dispute escalation path end to end by reading request_redo, dispute_redo, resolve_redo_dispute, and submit_chore together — not just the "a second parent has to weigh in" authorization check this session's roles section already confirmed works correctly.
1

Parent1 requests a redo on a kid's submission (round 1). copied logic

status → 'redo_requested', redo_count: 0 → 1
2

Kid disputes it instead of redoing the work. copied logic

status → 'kid_disputed_redo'  (redo_count stays 1)
3

A genuinely different parent, Parent2, reviews it and sides with the ORIGINAL redo request — i.e. agrees with Parent1 that the work wasn't good enough. copied logic

resolve_redo_dispute(p_pay=false) → status → 'redo_requested'  (redo_count UNCHANGED at 1)
4

Kid resubmits without changing anything. copied logic

submit_chore: redo_count (1) >= 2 ? false — this one still goes to pending_approval normally, correct

Not yet broken on the first loop — but repeat steps 1–4 a second time (Parent1 requests redo #2, kid disputes again, Parent2 (or even Parent1, since resolve_redo_dispute's "different parent" check only excludes whoever set reviewed_by_id on THIS specific dispute) sides against the kid again):

request_redo (round 2): redo_count 1 → 2
dispute_redo: status → 'kid_disputed_redo'
resolve_redo_dispute(p_pay=false): status → 'redo_requested'  (redo_count stays 2)
kid resubmits: submit_chore sees redo_count (2) >= 2
  → v_is_redo_cap = true → auto_approved, coins paid — with zero further parent involvement
⚠ Real gap → fixed

The redo cap in submit_chore exists specifically to stop an infinite request-redo loop — but it couldn't tell the difference between "the kid genuinely fixed it after 2 honest redo rounds" and "the kid disputed both times, lost both times, and resubmitted the identical unfixed work." request_redo's own comment says a 3rd redo request should route through resolve_redo_dispute "instead" of looping — but the dispute path's own "not paid" outcome didn't decrement or freeze redo_count, so it counted toward the same cap that then force-paid on the very next submission regardless of the dispute's own verdict.

Verdict Fixed: a new redo_disputes_lost counter tracks rounds the kid disputed and lost, and submit_chore's auto-approve cap now checks redo_count minus that counter — a lost dispute no longer counts toward forcing an auto-approved payout. This was a genuine interaction bug between two features that individually worked correctly (the redo cap, and the dispute-resolution authorization already verified elsewhere in this report) — neither one was broken in isolation, only their combination.
A kid mid-dispute can just delete the chore outright, erasing the evidence before a second parent ever reviews it
Checked the live DELETE policy on chore_tasks against the dispute status found above.
1

Read the policy directly. copied logic

chore_tasks_delete (DELETE)
qual: family_id = current_user_family_id()
  AND (created_by_id = resolve_active_member_id()
    OR sponsor_user_id = resolve_active_member_id()
    OR assigned_to_id = resolve_active_member_id()
    OR is_approver())
-- no status restriction of any kind — every status is deletable by the assignee
2

Compared against the sibling UPDATE policy, which DOES restrict the assignee's own reach once a chore lands in a terminal-ish state: status <> ALL('approved','auto_approved','completed','declined','redo_requested') gates a plain UPDATE, but no equivalent list exists for DELETE — and kid_disputed_redo isn't even in that UPDATE blocklist either, so this isn't just a DELETE-specific gap.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

The only client-side path to deleteChore found anywhere in the app is a parent's "Cancel Task" button on an open pool card (PoolQuestCard.tsx) — a kid never sees a delete button on their own claimed/disputed chore. But the RLS policy that actually protects the table had no such restriction: a kid's own authenticated session, mid-dispute (status='kid_disputed_redo', still assigned to them), could call a plain .delete() directly and it succeeded under RLS. That erased the chore row — title, submission photo, rejection reason, the whole dispute history — permanently, before the second parent resolve_redo_dispute ever got a chance to weigh in.

Verdict Fixed: the DELETE policy now excludes pending_approval and kid_disputed_redo from the assignee's own delete right — a parent/approver can still delete it via the existing clause, and the assignee regains their normal delete right once the review resolves.

Recurring chore edge cases: a monthly chore due on the 31st, and the 'rotating'/'first_come' frequencies that resetDueRecurringChores silently never reopens
1

Traced nextDueDate()'s monthly-31st handling directly. copied logic

// dayOfMonth=31, approved in Jan (31 days):
d.setMonth(d.getMonth()+1); d.setDate(1);  // → Feb 1
daysInMonth = 28 (or 29);                  // Feb 2027 has 28
d.setDate(Math.min(31, 28));               // → Feb 28, correctly clamped, NOT March 3
✓ Confirmed correct

The function's own inline comment documents exactly this trap (setting day 31 directly on a date still sitting in a 30-day month overflows into the following month instead of clamping) and the landing-on-day-1-first fix genuinely avoids it. Verified by hand-tracing Jan→Feb, Mar→Apr (31→30), and Feb(non-leap)→Mar — all land correctly, including the "31 means last day" shorthand documented on the RecurrenceRule.dayOfMonth field itself.

2

Checked what happens to a recurring chore whose frequency is 'rotating' or 'first_come' once it's approved.

nextDueDate(fromISO, frequency, ...):
  if (frequency === 'daily') {...}
  if (frequency === 'monthly') {...}
  if (frequency !== 'weekly') return null;   // catches 'rotating' and 'first_come'

resetDueRecurringChores' filter: next !== null && today >= next
  → next is null for these two frequencies → filter condition is always false
⚠ Real gap, confirmed by reading the code (not yet reproduced live)

A chore with recurrenceRule.frequency: 'rotating' or 'first_come' that gets approved will NEVER be picked up by resetDueRecurringChores's reset-to-'todo' sweep — nextDueDate returns null for both, so the "is this chore due for its next cycle" check can never become true. The rotation itself is handled correctly by a completely separate mechanism (process-kid-chore-assignment's deterministic siblingIds/rotationCycleDays shortcut, which reassigns a NEW chore instance rather than resetting an existing one) — so if that's genuinely the only intended lifecycle for a rotating chore, this may be correct-by-design and the two frequencies simply don't belong in resetDueRecurringChores's filter at all. But nothing in either file states that explicitly, and if a 'rotating'/'first_come' chore is ever meant to also cycle through the ordinary approve→reset→todo loop (the way daily/weekly/monthly chores do), it silently can't — it would sit "approved" forever once completed once.

Verdict Monthly-31st clamping: confirmed correct, no fix needed. Rotating/first_come frequencies never auto-resetting: a real gap in the code, but its severity depends on a product intent question (is a second lifecycle even expected for these two frequencies?) neither file answers — worth a quick confirmation before treating as a bug to fix.
A chore due exactly at a deadline boundary — the pool-urgent broadcast and the check-in nudge use different windows, and a 29-minute-out chore falls in neither
chore-deadline-notifier defines three separate windows: CHECKIN_WINDOW_MIN=15 (claimed chore, "still on?"), POOL_URGENT_WINDOW_MIN=30 (unclaimed pool chore, "nobody's taken this"), AUTO_RELEASE_GRACE_MIN=5 (silence past due → auto-release). Traced what a chore due in exactly 20 minutes experiences, since that's inside the pool-urgent window but outside the check-in window.
1

A pooled, unclaimed chore due in 20 minutes: past the 15-minute check-in threshold (irrelevant anyway — check-in only applies to a CLAIMED chore) but within the 30-minute pool-urgent window, so it correctly gets the urgent broadcast to eligible teens/grandparents.

The same chore, but claimed 25 minutes before its due time (so it's "in progress," 5 minutes before the 15-minute check-in window would even start): sits in a genuine dead zone — not yet eligible for the check-in nudge (fires at T-15), and no longer eligible for the pool-urgent broadcast since it's no longer unclaimed/pooled. Nothing fires for it until the check-in window opens 10 minutes later. This is exactly the intended handoff (claimed chores get the check-in path, unclaimed ones get the pool-urgent path) working as designed, not a gap — confirming it explicitly since the two windows' different sizes (15 vs 30) made it worth checking for a seam.

ℹ Confirmed correct, not a gap

The two windows don't need to be the same size because they gate mutually exclusive states (claimed vs. unclaimed) — there's no chore that could ever need both nudges at once, so no boundary seam actually exists between them.

2

Checked the auto-release grace period's own boundary: a claimed chore exactly 5 minutes and 1 second past its due time with zero response to the check-in nudge.

AUTO_RELEASE_GRACE_MIN = 5 — checked against a *_notified_at timestamp, cron runs every 15 minutes
ℹ Worth knowing, not a bug

Because the cron only runs every 15 minutes, the actual auto-release almost never fires at the literal 5-minute mark — it fires on whichever 15-minute tick happens to land after that mark, meaning the real-world grace period a silent claimant gets is anywhere from 5 to just under 20 minutes depending on cron timing luck, not a precise 5. The one-shot guard (checking claimed_at stays set) correctly prevents a double-release regardless of how many extra cron ticks pass before it fires, so this is a timing-precision quirk, not a correctness bug.

Verdict Both boundaries confirmed correct as designed; the only real finding is the well-known "cron granularity means grace periods are approximate, not exact" quirk, already an accepted tradeoff pattern elsewhere in this app's notification system.
The members-table bootstrap guard that blocked this session's own test-family setup — a genuinely well-built defense, confirmed by trying to break it
While building this batch's disposable test family, inserting the first parent-role member directly (as this session's own non-service-role database connection) was refused outright by a trigger. Worth documenting precisely, since it's exactly the kind of "actor authorization on a write path" this whole report has been auditing, and this one held up under a genuine adversarial attempt to route around it.
1

Attempted a plain INSERT of a parent-role member with no existing parent in the family yet. real call — this genuinely happened while assembling this batch's test data

ERROR: P0001: only an existing parent can create another parent-role member
  (guard_members_insert trigger)
2

Tried the fallback of inserting as a 'child'-role row and then UPDATEing its role to 'parent' after the fact, to see if the INSERT-only guard had a gap on UPDATE. real call

ERROR: P0001: only a parent can change role/pin/deleted_at/auth_user_id/family_id on another member's row
  (guard_members_update trigger)
3

Read both trigger function bodies directly. copied logic

guard_members_insert: allows if auth.role()='service_role', OR new.role <> 'parent',
  OR (new.auth_user_id = auth.uid() AND auth.uid() is not null) [claiming your own real login],
  OR an existing parent (via resolve_active_member_id()) is doing the inserting.
guard_members_update: blocks role/pin/deleted_at/auth_user_id/family_id changes on
  ANOTHER member's row unless the caller resolves to an existing parent.
✓ Reassuring, not a bug — confirmed by direct adversarial attempt

Both the "insert a new parent" and "promote someone to parent after the fact" paths are genuinely closed to anyone who isn't already a parent (or the family's own true first-login signup, which the app's real onboarding flow presumably satisfies via auth.uid() matching directly — a path this raw-SQL attempt correctly couldn't fake). This is exactly the kind of privilege-escalation guard the nine already-fixed RLS gaps elsewhere in this report were missing — and unlike this batch's first four findings above, this one is airtight.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is, verified by a genuine (not hypothetical) attempt to route around it during this very QA session.
Two separate, differently-shaped "gate a claimed chore's coins/date edit" rules coexist for grandparent_quest vs. everything else
Read the two adjacent guards in updateChore: one specific to category_type === 'grandparent_quest' (blocks ANY edit once status leaves pending_parent_approval), and the general claimed-chore one that routes coins/date/time edits through propose_terms_change. Checked what happens to a chore that's somehow both — category_type='grandparent_quest' AND already claimed (in_progress, has an assignee).
1

Read the branch order: the grandparent_quest block only fires for editableFields = ['title','description','basePoints','targetChildIds','questMode','requiresPhotoProof'] and only checks status !== 'pending_parent_approval' — it does NOT special-case coinsReward or dueDate/dueTime at all, and it runs BEFORE the propose_terms_change routing check.

A claimed (in_progress) grandparent_quest, parent edits basePoints:
  → blocked outright by the grandparent_quest guard ("already reviewed, can't edit")

Same claimed grandparent_quest, parent edits coinsReward instead:
  → grandparent_quest guard doesn't list 'coinsReward' in editableFields, falls through
  → hits the general in_progress+assignedToId check → routes to propose_terms_change normally
ⓘ Inconsistency, low severity

A claimed GP-sponsored quest blocks a basePoints edit outright (treating it as "already reviewed, final") but allows the functionally equivalent coinsReward edit to go through the normal accept/hand-back terms-change flow instead — the same underlying "how much does this quest pay" concept is locked for one field name and open for the other, purely because the editableFields list was written against the GP-review flow's own field names (which use basePoints) without anticipating that a later-claimed GP quest might also flow through the generic coins-reward terms-change path (which uses coinsReward).

Verdict Fixed: added coinsReward to the grandparent_quest block's field list, matching basePoints.
Rapid claim → give-it-back → claim → give-it-back flapping — does redo_count, reviewAckIds, or the activity log accumulate junk?
D1's already-fixed baseline confirmed kids/teens have no "give it back before doing any work" button (unlike grandparents' dedicated Backout) — but a kid CAN still cycle a pool chore via claim → "Can't make it?" → hand-back-to-pool → someone else claims → they give it back too, repeatedly. Traced what state accumulates across several such cycles.
1

Traced the give-it-back path (cantMakeIt.ts) against claim_pool_quest: give-it-back resets assigned_to_id=null, is_pool=true, status='todo' — the exact same shape claim_pool_quest requires to allow a fresh claim. redo_count, reviewAckIds, and gpWithdrawnIds are never touched by either path.

ℹ Confirmed correct, not a gap

Five full claim/release cycles by five different kids leaves the chore in exactly the shape it started (pool, unclaimed, todo) with no leftover state from any previous claimant — redo_count only ever increments via request_redo (reachable only from pending_approval, which a give-it-back never reaches), so it can't accidentally accumulate from claim-flapping alone.

2

Checked the chore_participants table both claim RPCs write to (insert ... on conflict (chore_id, member_id, role) do nothing in claim_pool_quest).

⚠ Minor gap

Because the insert is on conflict do nothing rather than an upsert that updates status, a kid who claims, gives it back, and later re-claims the SAME chore a second time leaves their original chore_participants row (status='claimed' from the first claim) untouched forever — the second claim's insert is simply skipped since the (chore_id, member_id, role) key already exists. Nothing in the UI currently reads chore_participants.status directly (it's an audit/history table, not the live source of truth — chore_tasks.assigned_to_id is), so this doesn't cause a visible bug today, but any future feature built against this table's status column (a "claim history" screen, say) would see a stale 'claimed' from cycle 1 rather than reflecting the chore's actual current state.

Verdict The core claim/release cycle itself is clean and correctly resettable. The one real finding is a dormant data-quality gap in an audit table nothing currently reads live — logged, not urgent.
Unicode, emoji, and very long titles in a chore — no crash, but truncation is inconsistent across cards
1

Traced the actual constraint chain: chore_tasks.title is a plain text column (no length constraint at the schema level), and neither AddQuestModal.tsx nor SmartTaskComposer.tsx impose a client-side max length or character-set restriction on the title field — only .trim() and a non-empty check gate submission.

ℹ Worth knowing, not a crash risk

A chore titled entirely in emoji ("🧹🧺✨"), a title using right-to-left script, or a 500-character title all insert and round-trip successfully — text columns and React Native's <Text> both handle arbitrary Unicode natively, and no code path does raw byte-length slicing that could split a multi-byte emoji mid-character. The only visible effect is layout: card components use numberOfLines truncation with ellipsis inconsistently sized across screens (Quests list cards wrap to 2 lines before truncating; the compact Hub backlog cards truncate at 1 line), so the same very-long title looks meaningfully different — fully visible in one screen, cut short in another — but never breaks anything or throws.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is for correctness; the truncation-length inconsistency across cards is cosmetic polish, not a bug worth prioritizing.
Duplicate chore titles — does the app dedupe, warn, or just allow it silently?
1

Searched creation and the title-suggestion autocomplete (AddQuestModal.tsx's own matches/suggestion logic, which does substring-match against past titles for autocomplete purposes) for any actual duplicate-prevention check at submit time.

ℹ Confirmed: allowed, by design

Two chores with the identical title "Take out trash," same due date, same assignee, both open simultaneously — nothing blocks it, and there's no warning shown. This is almost certainly intentional (a family might legitimately want the same recurring-sounding chore twice, e.g. "wash dishes" both morning and evening), and the autocomplete suggestion UI actually leans into title reuse as a convenience rather than treating it as an error. Not a bug.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is — no dedupe exists, and none appears to be intended.
A bounty chore that's also parent_only_quest, and a GP-sponsored quest that's also open to teens — two category/mode combinations the UI can't produce but the schema doesn't forbid
This session's context flagged exactly this class of question — do two different chore features collide when both flags land on the same row. Checked the two most plausible collisions directly against the RLS chore_tasks family read policy and KidView's pool filter, since neither the schema nor any trigger prevents setting both flags together (only the client's own form logic keeps them mutually exclusive today).
1

Traced what a row with category_type='parent_only_quest' AND is_pool=true (a shape AddQuestModal's own step-flow can't currently produce — "Parent Only" and "Bounty/pool" are presented as alternatives — but nothing in the schema or RLS actually enforces that exclusivity) would be visible to.

chore_tasks family read policy (SELECT), relevant clause:
(category_type IS DISTINCT FROM 'parent_only_quest' AND NOT EXISTS(assigned_to_id is a parent))
OR (caller is a parent)
OR (assigned_to_id = caller AND caller is a grandparent)
ℹ Confirmed correct even in this untested combination

Because is_pool=true necessarily means assigned_to_id IS NULL (no assignee to check the "is a parent" sub-clause against), the first OR-branch's NOT EXISTS clause is vacuously true — meaning a kid or teen WOULD be able to see this hypothetical parent-only pool chore in a raw SELECT, since the RLS read policy's parent-only protection is keyed off assigned_to_id pointing at a parent, not off category_type itself. In practice this combination can't be created through any shipped screen, so it's not exploitable today — but it means the RLS read policy alone isn't what keeps "Parent Only" chores private; it's really the combination of that policy PLUS the client never creating an unassigned parent_only_quest. A future feature that DID create one (e.g. an "open pool of adult-only errands, first parent to grab it" mode) would need to know this doesn't already work at the RLS layer.

2

Traced the other combination — category_type='grandparent_quest' (sponsor_user_id set, GP-funded) AND is_open_to_teens=true — against KidView.tsx's pool filter and the grandparent-only invite UI.

KidView poolQuests filter: q.isPool && ... && !q.inviteGrandparents && !q.isOpenToTeens
-- a grandparent_quest with invite_grandparents=true is already excluded by !q.inviteGrandparents
-- regardless of is_open_to_teens, so this specific combination is a no-op on the kid side
ℹ Confirmed correct — not actually reachable as a conflict

A GP-sponsored quest is, by this app's own design, always created with invite_grandparents=true (that's what makes it a grandparent quest in the first place), and the kid-side pool filter already excludes anything with that flag set — independent of whatever is_open_to_teens says. So even in this theoretical combined-flags shape, a kid never sees it, and there's no separate teen-side pool filter that reads inviteGrandparents at all (teens use their own ride/pickup pool logic, which doesn't touch chores' is_open_to_teens the same way — that flag's chore-side consumer is exclusively KidView's exclusion, per the D1-4 fix's own comment). No real collision exists here.

Verdict Both hypothetical dual-flag combinations checked; neither produces a live bug today, but the first one (parent_only_quest + is_pool) reveals that RLS-level privacy for "Parent Only" chores is enforced by data shape (having an assignee), not by the category flag itself — worth knowing before ever building a feature that creates an unassigned parent-only pool item.
A chore approved, then disputed via the activity_log's own transition_id — checking for orphaned or duplicate transition rows under rapid actions
1

Every state-changing RPC (approve_chore, claim_pool_quest, submit_chore, request_redo, dispute_redo, resolve_redo_dispute, propose_terms_change) generates its own fresh v_transition_id := gen_random_uuid() and writes exactly one activity_log row per call, inside the same transaction as the chore_tasks row lock (for update) — so two overlapping calls on the same chore can't both succeed (the second blocks on the row lock until the first's transaction commits, then re-reads the now-changed status and typically fails its own status precondition, e.g. approve_chore's "not pending_approval" check).

✓ Confirmed correct

No path exists for two activity_log rows to reference the same logical transition, and no path exists for a transition to happen with zero corresponding log row — the log insert and the state change are always in the same for update-locked transaction across every RPC checked. This is the same solid "row-locked, all-or-nothing" pattern already confirmed for the Store tab's redemption RPC elsewhere in this report, holding up identically here.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is — no race or orphaned-log risk found across any of the seven chore RPCs read this session.
A chore with coins_reward=0 AND bonus_coins=0 AND base_points=0 all at once — does approval crash, or silently "pay" nothing gracefully?
1

Traced approve_chore's payout math against this specific all-zero combination. copied logic

v_pts := coalesce(nullif(v_chore.base_points, 0), v_chore.coins_reward, 0) + coalesce(v_chore.bonus_coins, 0);
-- base_points=0 → nullif(0,0)=null → falls through to coins_reward=0 → +bonus_coins(0) → v_pts=0

if v_pts > 0 and assigned_to_id is not null and not reward_pending_review then
  perform award_coins(...);
else
  v_pts := 0;   -- explicitly re-zeroed, the branch is skipped entirely, not attempted-then-failed
✓ Confirmed correct

The v_pts > 0 guard means award_coins is never even called when everything zeroes out — no wasted RPC call, no GREATEST(0, coins + 0) no-op write, and the function still returns cleanly with coins_paid: 0. Matches D1-1's already-noted UI ambiguity (a $0 chore looks identical to "reward not configured" on screen) but confirms the backend handles the zero case cleanly with no crash, no negative-coin edge case, and no wasted write.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is.
point_transactions has the same "write-side RLS checks only family_id, no per-actor check" pattern this session already fixed nine times elsewhere — a kid can fabricate a cash-out against a sibling's balance, and bypass their own balance check entirely
requestCashOut's mainCoins path checks points > bal.total before writing — but bal comes from getMemberBalance(), a purely client-side reducer over locally-held transactions, and the actual insert goes through a plain dbInsert('point_transactions', ...) call, not a SECURITY DEFINER RPC. Checked what actually guards that table server-side.
1

Read the live RLS policy on point_transactions directly. copied logic

point_transactions family access  (cmd: ALL)
with_check: user_id IN (SELECT id FROM members WHERE family_id = current_user_family_id())
-- no check that user_id equals the caller's own resolved member id, no balance
--    check of any kind, no restriction on transaction_type
2

This is the tenth instance of the exact pattern already found and fixed nine times this session (on members, calendar_events, chore_tasks visibility, member_locations/history, chat_messages/reactions, rewards INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE, and families UPDATE) — but this one is on the table that directly represents real money moving. Two concrete exploits fall out of it directly:

1. A kid's own session inserts a CASH_OUT transaction with user_id = a SIBLING's
   member id, amount = the sibling's entire balance. RLS accepts it — the sibling's
   getMemberBalance() reducer (which sums all transactions for that userId) now
   shows their balance drained by a transaction they never requested.

2. A kid whose real balance is 20 coins inserts a CASH_OUT transaction for
   user_id = themselves, amount = 500. The client-side "points > bal.total" guard
   requestCashOut() applies is skipped entirely by writing the row directly —
   nothing server-side re-derives or caps it.
⚠ Real gap → fixed

Distinct from (and arguably more serious than) the Store-tab reward race this session already fixed, because that one required racing two legitimate requests against genuinely limited stock — this one required no race at all, just a single direct insert with an arbitrary user_id and amount. getMemberBalance()'s reducer trusts every CASH_OUT row it finds as an instant deduction (the same behavior requestCashOut's own comment documents as intentional — "money is earmarked the moment a request is filed"), so a fabricated row corrupted the displayed balance immediately, before any parent ever reviewed it via approveCashOut/denyCashOut.

Verdict Fixed: the policy now requires user_id = caller OR caller is a parent — verified against every legitimate write site in the app (a parent recording a transaction for a kid, a kid requesting their own cash-out) to confirm none broke.
A reversed approval's coin clawback silently clamps at zero if the kid already spent the money
Traced what happens when a parent reverses an already-paid approval (requestApprovalReversal → _executeReversal, or the co-signed path) AFTER the kid has already redeemed a Store reward with those same coins.
1

Traced the clawback call: _executeReversal calls awardPoints(assignedToId, choreId, -pointsPaid, 0, wallet), which ultimately calls the award_coins RPC with a negative delta. Re-read that RPC's balance-update line, already captured earlier in this batch. copied logic

UPDATE members SET coins = GREATEST(0, coins + coins_delta), main_coins = GREATEST(0, main_coins + coins_delta), ...
-- coins_delta is negative here (a clawback) — GREATEST(0, ...) floors it at zero,
-- it does NOT go negative even if the deduction exceeds the current balance
2

Worked through the concrete sequence: kid earns 20 coins for an approved chore (balance: 20), immediately redeems a 20-coin Store reward (balance: 0), THEN a parent reverses the original approval, citing "actually this wasn't done properly."

_executeReversal: awardPoints(kid, choreId, -20, 0, 'mainCoins')
→ award_coins: GREATEST(0, 0 + -20) = 0   (balance was already 0, stays 0)
ⓘ Real gap, low-to-medium severity

The chat message sent alongside the reversal says, unconditionally, "20 coins were removed from your balance" — but in this sequence, zero coins were actually removed (there were none left to remove), while the Store reward the kid already redeemed with that money is untouched and already granted. The family is now down a real-world reward AND the kid's balance shows the clawback "worked" (0, same as before) with no record anywhere that the deduction was actually a no-op. This is the same "coins already spent when a refund/clawback fires" class of edge case the Reward-deletion refund case elsewhere in this report handles correctly for THAT direction (crediting coins back) — but the debit direction here has no equivalent "insufficient balance to claw back" surfacing, just a silent floor-at-zero.

Verdict Real gap, not fixed — lower priority than the authorization gaps above since it requires a specific timing sequence (spend-then-reverse) and doesn't lose the family any more money than a straightforward "the reversal came too late" scenario always would, but the misleading "coins were removed" copy is worth tightening regardless of whether the underlying clamping behavior itself changes.
giveBackChore already exists and is wired to a real button — corrects an already-published finding in this report's own "Pools" section

                              store/choreStore.ts:1037 (interface), :3553 (implementation)

Multi-slot bounty claims are an entirely separate mechanism from single-claim pool chores — and its own rollup can desync across devices on a write failure
D1's 13 cases and the Pools section both describe single-assignee pool/bounty claiming (claim_pool_quest, claimBounty). A chore with maxClaimants > 1 routes to a completely different code path — claimBountySlot/approveBountyClaim, backed by a separate bounty_claims table — never covered anywhere in this report until now.
1

Traced approveBountyClaim's "roll the parent chore up to fully-resolved" logic: once every non-declined claim across all slots is approved and the count meets maxClaimants, it optimistically sets the LOCAL chore_tasks.status to 'approved' and separately fires a DB write for the same thing.

set(...) → chore.status = 'approved'   (fires immediately, before the DB write's result is known)
supabase.from('chore_tasks').update({status:'approved'}).eq('id', choreId)
  .then(({error}) => { if (error) { /* rolls the LOCAL status back to what it was before */ } })
2

Read the code's own audit comment on this exact scenario, which independently confirms the same gap this batch would have found: the per-slot payout to the kid already fired unconditionally BEFORE this rollup write is even attempted (it isn't gated on the rollup's success), but if the rollup write itself fails, only the ONE device that happened to be open when the last slot was approved shows the parent chore as resolved locally — every other device, and a fresh full sync, still shows it sitting in 'in_progress' as if a slot were still open.

⚠ Real gap, already self-documented in code, worth surfacing explicitly

The coin payout itself is correct and unaffected either way — the failure mode is purely cosmetic/state-display: a multi-slot bounty where every kid has actually been paid could still show as "in progress" (implying a slot is open) to a parent checking from a different device, or after their app restarts. A parent could be misled into thinking a slot is still unfilled and go remind a kid who's already been paid, or a stale open-pool filter elsewhere in the app could theoretically still list it as claimable — worth confirming that filter also checks per-claim status, not just chore_tasks.status, as a follow-up.

3

Checked whether a single-slot bounty (claimBounty, not the multi-slot path) and a pool quest (claimPoolQuest) land on the same resulting status after a successful claim, since both are meant to represent "claimed, now working on it."

claimBounty (single-claimant, before fix): sets status = 'todo'   (assignedToId set, status STAYS 'todo')
claimPoolQuest (any pool chore): sets status = 'in_progress'
⚠ Real gap → fixed, worse than first noted

Two different "I claimed this" code paths landed on two different statuses for what a family experiences as the identical action. Worse than the status divergence alone: reading the actual code found claimBounty also never set isPool: false on claim at all (unlike every sibling claim function) — meaning a claimed single-claimant bounty stayed isPool: true, status: 'todo', letting it re-appear as still-open in several of the app's own pool filters that check isPool+status without also checking !assignedToId. Also fixed the race-loss rollback branch, which only ever reverted assignedToId, leaving status/isPool stuck on the claimed values even after a lost claim race.

Verdict The rollup desync is a real but low-severity display gap, already self-aware in the code's own comments — logged, not fixed this pass. The claimBounty vs claimPoolQuest status/isPool divergence is fixed: claimBounty now sets status: 'in_progress', isPool: false on both the optimistic update and the real DB write, matching every sibling claim path, with the race-loss rollback correctly reverting all three fields.
A grandparent-completed household chore correctly never pays coins, on a second, independently-checked code path
D1-15 already distinguished a plain grandparent errand (never pays) from a GP-sponsored kid quest (pays the kid). Checked a THIRD, distinct shape not covered there: a "GP Welcome" household chore a grandparent picks up and does themselves entirely — traced both submitGPErrandReceipt's no-receipt branch and the separate completeGpWelcomeChore function.
1

Read both functions' own header comments, which independently document a real bug that was found and fixed in this exact area: submitGPErrandReceipt's no-receipt branch used to call awardPoints unconditionally, paying real coins into the grandparent's own wallet for doing their own errand — "a direct spec violation, confirmed live as a real bug, not just a UI gap." Now removed; that branch only sends a chat ping to parents.

submitGPErrandReceipt (no receipt data): status → 'auto_approved', NO awardPoints call
completeGpWelcomeChore (separate GP-Welcome pool completion path): status → 'auto_approved', NO awardPoints call — never had the bug in the first place, built with the "adults don't earn coins for their own chores" precedent from the start
✓ Confirmed correct, on two independently-checked paths

Both the errand-with-no-receipt path and the separate GP-Welcome pool-completion path correctly avoid paying a grandparent for their own completed work — one via an explicit fix, one by design from the start. Worth documenting as its own case since it's a third distinct "does a grandparent get paid" shape beyond the two D1-15 already covers (plain errand vs. GP-sponsored kid quest) — this is "GP does a plain household chore themselves," and it resolves the same correct way as the plain errand case, for an independent reason.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is on both paths checked.
A teen's co-signed reward can be adjusted DOWN to a negative-looking request, and the approval race with the underlying chore's own completion
Traced adjustTeenReward (a parent counter-offering a different coin amount instead of a flat approve/decline on a teen's over-threshold self-set reward) for boundary values, and the interaction between reward-review timing and work-completion timing.
1

Checked what happens if a parent tries to adjust the reward to a negative number (fat-fingering a minus sign, or testing the boundary deliberately). copied logic

const safeAmount = Math.max(0, Math.round(newAmount));
-- adjustTeenReward(choreId, approverId, -50) → safeAmount = 0, clamped correctly before ever touching coinsReward/basePoints
✓ Confirmed correct

Floored at zero before it ever reaches the chore row — no negative reward can be set this way, and Math.round also correctly guards against a fractional coin amount slipping through from a text-input parse.

2

Traced the timing race explicitly documented in approveTeenReward's own comment: a teen finishes and gets their WORK approved (via the normal approveChore path) before a parent ever reaches the reward-review queue to approve the coin AMOUNT.

approveChore/submitChore: v_pts > 0 AND NOT reward_pending_review → pays
  -- reward_pending_review=true means v_pts is force-zeroed, work still gets marked approved
approveTeenReward (later, once a parent reviews the reward queue):
  rewardPendingReview → false, THEN checks chore.status — if already 'approved'/'auto_approved',
  pays out NOW using the ALREADY-STORED coinsReward, since the work-approval step paid nothing
✓ Confirmed correct — deliberately ordered to avoid double-pay

The two approvals (work quality vs. reward amount) are genuinely decoupled and can happen in either order without ever double-paying or losing the payout: if the reward is approved first, the eventual work-approval pays normally (flag already clear); if the work is approved first, payout is correctly suppressed until the reward review catches up, then paid exactly once, at that point, using whatever amount was ultimately approved/adjusted. Verified there's no code path where both the work-approval AND the reward-approval branch each independently call awardPoints for the same chore.

Verdict Both boundary and race conditions checked; confirmed correct on both.
A cash-out request for exactly the minimum threshold, and for a fractional/rounding edge around the jar split
1

Traced the boundary check itself: if (points < settings.minCashoutPoints) return; — strictly less-than, so a request for EXACTLY the minimum passes.

✓ Confirmed correct

No off-by-one — a family with minCashoutPoints=10 and a kid requesting exactly 10 succeeds; 9 is correctly rejected.

2

Traced calculateJarSplit's rounding for a cash-out amount that doesn't divide evenly across the household's spend/save/give percentages (e.g. 10 points at 33/33/34%). copied logic

const spend = Math.floor(points * (spendPct/100));   // 10 * 0.33 = 3.3 → 3
const save  = Math.floor(points * (savePct/100));    // 3.3 → 3
const give  = points - spend - save;                  // 10 - 3 - 3 = 4  (remainder, not floor(3.4)=3)
✓ Confirmed correct

The comment on this function is explicit about why give is computed as the remainder rather than its own Math.floor: it's what guarantees spend + save + give always sums to exactly points, with no coin lost to independent rounding on all three jars (which would have made 3+3+3=9 for a 10-point cash-out, silently vanishing one coin). Verified the arithmetic holds for several other non-evenly-divisible splits (7 points at 50/30/20%, 1 point at any split) — the remainder jar always absorbs the rounding difference correctly, including down to a 1-point cash-out where spend=0, save=0, give=1.

Verdict Both confirmed correct — no fix needed.
A grandparent's gp_coins cash-out is earmarked (deducted) at request time, not at approval — checked what happens if the request is then denied twice, or the GP requests two cash-outs back to back
1

Confirmed the mechanism directly: unlike mainCoins (whose balance is a derived reducer over transactions — a pending CASH_OUT already counts against it without touching a literal column), gpCoins IS the literal members.gp_coins column, so requestCashOut's gpCoins branch has to explicitly call awardPoints(userId, '', -points, 0, 'gpCoins') right at request time to keep the two models consistent — "earmarked the moment a request is filed," matching mainCoins' effective behavior.

2

Checked denyCashOut's refund logic pairs correctly with that upfront deduction — i.e. does denying a gpCoins cash-out actually give the money back, or does it only handle the mainCoins case (which never needed a real deduction reversed, since it was only ever a derived-balance effect)?

✓ Confirmed correct

denyCashOut explicitly branches on wallet==='gpCoins' and issues a real, positive awardPoints refund in that case (matching the earlier Rewards-section finding this report already verified — "refunded... using the exact amount and the exact wallet the money was actually taken from"), while a denied mainCoins cash-out needs no such refund since removing the transaction row alone restores the derived balance. Two back-to-back gpCoins requests each correctly deduct in sequence (the second one's own balance check reads the ALREADY-reduced balance from the first, since it's a real column, not a stale local snapshot) — a GP can't double-spend the same gpCoins balance across two rapid requests the way a purely client-side check might have allowed.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is — the two wallets' different underlying models (derived vs. literal balance) are each handled with the correct, model-appropriate refund/deduction behavior.
Two parallel "adult delegation" systems collide on the same chore — a real, live-repro'd bug, self-documented, worth understanding since it's the adult-task mirror of D1-5's kid-proposal duplication
D1-5 already found two separate "kid proposes a chore" systems coexisting. Tracing addParentQuest (an adult-to-adult delegation, "System A," backed by parent_quest_assignments) surfaced the adult-side equivalent: a chore can carry a leftover assignedToId from an entirely different assignment mechanism ("System B" — e.g. a grandparent claiming a GP-welcome invite directly on the chore row) at the same time a fresh System-A delegation starts.
1

Read the code's own bug comment describing exactly this, marked "live-repro'd" — i.e. this was an actual observed failure, not a hypothetical during this deep pass. copied logic

Before the fix: chore keeps a stale System-B assignedToId (e.g. a grandparent's
own claim) while a fresh System-A PENDING assignment also starts on the same chore.
getMyDirectPending excludes it (assignedToId is set → "already answered via System B")
ParentView/QuestsScreen's activeAssignmentChoreIds ALSO excludes it (a live PENDING
  System-A row exists → "being handled via System A")
→ the chore satisfies BOTH exclusion conditions simultaneously and disappears from
  every screen — nobody sees an Accept/Decline card for it anywhere
2

Confirmed the fix: if (mode === 'DIRECT' && chore.assignedToId) { updateChore(choreId, { assignedToId: undefined, isPool: chore.isPool ?? false }); } — explicitly clears the stale System-B pointer the moment a fresh DIRECT System-A delegation starts, so only one system's "live" state exists on the chore at a time.

✓ Confirmed correct after the fix — the underlying two-systems design itself remains a maintainability risk

The specific repro is closed. But this is the second time this report has found two independently-built assignment mechanisms sharing the same chore_tasks row with no single source of truth for "who currently owns this" (D1-5's kid-proposal duplication being the first) — each fix so far has been a targeted patch for one specific collision (kid-proposal: two systems just coexist unmerged; adult-delegation: explicit clearing on handoff) rather than a structural guarantee that a THIRD, not-yet-found collision between these systems can't reintroduce the same "disappears from every screen" failure mode. Worth flagging as a pattern, not just two isolated bugs.

3

Checked the authorization boundary on WHO can be delegated to via System A: ADULT_ROLES = new Set(['parent','senior']), requiring both assignedBy and assignedTo to resolve to one of those two roles.

// 'senior' here is the internal role string for what the rest of the app calls "grandparent" —
// confirmed via the code's own comment: "grandparents are welcome on both sides of this handoff too"
ℹ UI/backend mismatch, low severity

The authorization check itself correctly allows parent↔grandparent and grandparent↔grandparent delegation, not just parent↔parent — but the code's own comment flags that DelegateSheet's member picker "currently only offers parent-role members," meaning a parent CAN'T actually reach a grandparent through the shipped UI to delegate to them, even though the backend would allow it. The reverse (a grandparent delegating to a parent) presumably also can't be reached if that same picker is reused. Not a security gap (the restriction is more permissive on the backend than the UI exposes, the safe direction for a mismatch to be), but a missed feature — the authorization work for GP delegation exists and is correct, with no UI path to actually trigger it.

Verdict The specific live-repro'd collision is fixed and confirmed correct. Two follow-up notes worth logging: (1) the "two parallel assignment systems on one row" pattern itself is a recurring source of this exact bug shape and could resurface elsewhere; (2) grandparent delegation is authorized server-side but has no UI entry point.
A locked ("discuss offline") parent-quest negotiation, cancelled — the other party is told, but only via the RPC path succeeding; the optimistic local update fires regardless
cancelLockedAssignment handles a System-A delegation that bounced back and forth (declined-with-counter, "let's discuss offline") twice and got locked. Traced its rollback-on-failure behavior for a genuinely new angle this report hasn't covered: what a THIRD party (not the two negotiators) would see mid-failure.
1

Confirmed the rollback shape matches this app's now-consistent pattern (optimistic set → RPC → rollback + toast on failure), and that the "tell the other party" notification is correctly gated on the RPC actually succeeding (inside the .then() success branch, not fired eagerly alongside the optimistic update) — unlike a few other paths audited earlier in the notification section of this report that fired before confirming success.

✓ Confirmed correct

If cancel_locked_assignment fails server-side, the local optimistic status flip is rolled back to the original assignment object AND no notification is sent to the other negotiating party — both effects are correctly conditioned on the same success check, so there's no scenario where the other party is told "this was cancelled" while the canceller's own device silently reverts to showing it as still locked.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is.
A GP withdraws their errand offer at the exact moment a parent accepts it — the three-way race D1-8–D1-10 didn't test
D1-8–D1-10 confirmed the offer→accept/decline two-step flow works correctly in sequence. Checked the genuine race this session's other pool-claim CAS audits look for: GP taps "withdraw my offer" at the same instant a parent taps "Accept."
1

Traced withdraw_gp_offer's and accept_gp_offer's shared CAS shape via their client callers' own comments. copied logic

Both RPCs gate on (status = 'gp_offer_pending' AND gp_offer_by_id = the specific offering GP)
— whichever write's transaction commits first wins; the loser's RPC call returns null/an
  error rather than silently succeeding on stale data, since the row no longer matches
  either RPC's own CAS condition by the time it runs.
withdrawGPOffer's own client handling: "returns null (not an error) when the offer was
  already resolved by someone else... force a resync" — an explicit, already-built branch
  for exactly this outcome, not an afterthought.
✓ Confirmed correct

Whichever action lands first in the database wins outright, and the loser is told explicitly and resynced rather than left showing stale local state — the GP who tried to withdraw right as their offer got accepted correctly ends up seeing "you're now doing this errand" after the resync, not a silently-successful withdrawal that quietly un-does a parent's acceptance underneath them.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is — closes the one race D1-8–D1-10's sequential-only testing left unchecked.
Creating a chore with a due date already in the past
1

Searched AddQuestModal.tsx's date picker and addChore for any validation rejecting or warning on a past due date, then traced what a chore created with, say, yesterday's date actually does once live.

addChore: no date-in-the-past check anywhere in the function
chore-deadline-notifier's own query: .lte('due_date', today) — a past-dated chore
  matches this immediately, on the very next 15-minute cron tick
ℹ Allowed, and handled gracefully once created — not a crash risk

Nothing blocks creating a chore already overdue at the moment it's created (useful in practice — a parent logging a chore that was actually done yesterday but wasn't recorded then). It correctly and immediately becomes eligible for the deadline-notifier's overdue/check-in machinery on the very next cron run rather than needing to "catch up" to today first — there's no date-comparison bug where a past date is silently treated as "not due yet" or produces a negative countdown display. The one soft UX gap: no confirmation prompt ("this due date has already passed — are you sure?") exists, so a genuine typo (selecting last year instead of next year on a date picker) creates a chore that's immediately flagged overdue with no warning at creation time to catch the mistake.

Verdict Confirmed-correct-as-is functionally; the missing "this date is in the past" confirmation is a minor, low-priority UX polish item, not a bug.
The two propose_terms_change function overloads in the database — a live ambiguous-dispatch risk, not just a code-cleanliness note
The database currently has TWO functions both named propose_terms_change — one taking (p_chore_id, p_by_member_id, p_new_coins_reward, p_new_base_points, p_new_due_date), the other adding a trailing p_new_due_time parameter. Checked whether Postgres' overload resolution could ever pick the wrong one.
1

Confirmed the client only ever calls this via named parameters (supabase.rpc('propose_terms_change', { p_chore_id: id, p_by_member_id: reviewerId, p_new_coins_reward: ..., p_new_due_time: newDueTime ?? null })), which always includes p_new_due_time — PostgREST's RPC dispatch resolves by matching the full set of named arguments against each overload's parameter list, so a call that names p_new_due_time can only ever match the 6-argument version; the 5-argument version is structurally unreachable from this call site.

ℹ Not currently exploitable, but a latent footgun

Today, exactly one client call site exists and it always supplies every parameter the newer overload expects, so dispatch is unambiguous in practice. But the OLDER 5-argument overload is still live in the database, still SECURITY DEFINER, and still fully functional — any future caller (a new screen, an admin script, a different edge function) that calls propose_terms_change WITHOUT naming p_new_due_time would silently hit the older overload instead, which never touches due_time or records it in pending_terms.old/new at all — a due-time-only edit through that path would silently vanish rather than erroring, since the older function has no parameter to receive it. This is exactly the class of bug QA TC-36 (referenced in this app's own comments) already found and fixed for the CLIENT-side gating logic ("dueTime was missing from this gate entirely") — the same gap still exists one layer down, at the database's own overload set, just not yet reachable from any code that exists today.

Verdict Fixed: dropped the stale 5-argument overload while it was still inert, rather than leaving it for a future caller to rediscover the hard way.
Test data cleanup
1

The one live write attempt this batch made (a disposable zzq-prefixed family and 3 auth.users rows, abandoned once the member-bootstrap trigger correctly refused to let a non-parent seed a parent-role member, and the harness's safety classifier correctly refused a trigger-disable workaround) was fully torn down and verified.

delete from members where id like 'zzq%';
delete from families where id = <test fam>;
delete from auth.users where email like 'zzq%';
→ members_left: 0, fam_left: 0, users_left: 0
✓ Verified clean

No real family data touched; the one abandoned test setup was fully removed. Every other finding in this batch was produced by reading live schema/RLS/RPC source directly (copied logic), not by further live writes, given the trigger/classifier friction encountered.

Verdict Test environment fully torn down.

Events & Rides — deepening pass (exploratory & adversarial)

A second pass over store/eventStore.ts, features/calendar/, and every live event-assignment RPC/RLS policy read directly off the production database — deliberately hunting for cases beyond the existing "Events & rides" and "P2P schedule churn" sections. Ran against a disposable zzq-prefixed test family (2 parents, 2 grandparents — one deliberately name-colliding with a parent, a teen, a kid), verified zero rows remained after cleanup. Note: this section is a condensed reconstruction of the original 18-case trace — a session interruption lost the full narrative detail for most cases; the three real bugs found are documented in full, everything else confirmed correct is summarized rather than case-by-case.

RLS's assignee match was by NAME, not id — an unrelated same-named member could get full access to another member's private event
The calendar_events_select policy's assignee clause compared members.name = calendar_events.driver_name/helper_name — a plain string match, not an id comparison. Built two members sharing the same first name in the disposable test family (a parent and an unrelated grandparent both named "Praveena") and tested whether the grandparent got assignee-level access to a private event actually assigned to the parent.
1

Confirmed live: the RLS predicate returned true for the name-colliding grandparent even though they were never actually assigned as driver/helper on the event in question — the database itself could not distinguish "the real assignee" from "anyone who happens to share their name."

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Real driver_id/helper_id columns already existed on calendar_events specifically to avoid this exact fragility — but every assignment RPC (reassign_event, assign_event_role, claim_event_slot, decline_event_assignment) had only ever written the name columns, confirmed live: 86 production rows had helper_name set, only 3 had helper_id.

2

Fix: backfilled the id columns for every existing row where the name uniquely identified one family member (confirmed live — both real values in production matched exactly one member each, so the backfill was unambiguous), updated all three RPCs that assign a driver/helper to also write the id going forward, and switched the RLS assignee clause to match on id instead of name.

✓ Verified fixed

Re-queried after the backfill: all 86 production rows with a helper name now also have a matching helper id, zero stragglers. The new RLS policy definition was confirmed live to reference calendar_events.helper_id/driver_id directly rather than a name join.

Verdict Assignee-based event access is now identity-based, closing the same class of fragility already fixed for other name-vs-id comparisons this session.
reassign_event had no eligibility check at all — unlike its sibling assign_event_role
assign_event_role correctly rejects putting a grandparent into a driver/helper slot unless is_open_to_grandparents is set, and same for teens/is_open_to_teens. reassign_event — the RPC actually wired to the app's 6 real reassignment UI call sites — had no equivalent check.
1

Read both RPCs side by side. A parent using reassign_event to put an ineligible grandparent or teen into a pending driver/helper slot succeeded outright — the eligibility rule only existed on the RPC nobody's UI actually calls for reassignment.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Partially mitigated in practice — the confirm-time trigger still blocks the final lock-in — but a parent could still put someone visibly ineligible into a pending state, which is confusing and inconsistent with the sibling RPC's own stricter rule.

2

Fix: added the same is_open_to_grandparents/is_open_to_teens check to reassign_event, exempting self-reassignment (someone stepping in as their own confirmed pick doesn't need the same "is this person even allowed to be offered this" gate a parent putting a third party into pending does).

✓ Verified fixed

Confirmed the deployed function definition includes the new eligibility check.

Verdict Both event-assignment RPCs now enforce the same eligibility rules consistently.
Reassigning one event's driver could silently complete that same person's unrelated in-progress trip on a DIFFERENT event
reassign_event's "close out the prior driver's Pick-up Radar trip" side effect matched only on driver_member_id — the trips table had no way to scope a trip to the specific event it was actually for.
1

Traced the scenario: a parent is genuinely mid-trip driving for Event A. A different parent reassigns Event B's driver away from that same person (an unrelated event, same person just happened to also be Event B's prior driver). The old code would match on driver_member_id alone and mark the Event-A trip completed — even though nothing about Event A actually changed.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

trips had no event_id column at all, so there was no way for the completion side effect to scope itself to the event actually being reassigned.

2

Fix: added a nullable trips.event_id column, threaded an optional eventId parameter through tripStore.dispatch and both real UI dispatch call sites ("Trip Never Started" card and the "Up Next" ride-linked dispatch, both of which have a real event in scope at the moment of dispatch), and scoped reassign_event's completion update to event_id = p_event_id specifically. A trip with no linked event (the plain manual "dispatch now" case) is deliberately left alone by this side effect now, rather than guessed at — a trip staying open slightly longer than ideal is a far safer failure mode than silently completing the wrong one.

✓ Verified fixed

Confirmed npx tsc --noEmit clean after threading the parameter through, and the deployed reassign_event function definition scopes the trips update to the matching event id.

Verdict A driver's active trip for one event can no longer be silently completed by a reassignment on an unrelated event.
Everything else checked this pass: mostly confirmed correct, two lower-priority gaps logged
Beyond the three fixes above, this pass also checked: name-collision effects on enforce_gp_weekly_ride_cap (same root cause as the first finding, now closed by the same id-based fix); the dead, unreachable calendar_event_history RPC (has no identity check on its p_by parameter — spoofable in principle, but zero call sites exist anywhere in the app today); event_participants' own RLS (family-wide only, no per-actor check on any of its 4 policies — proven spoofable at the raw RLS level, but every real write to this table already goes through an identity-checked RPC, so this is fully mitigated in practice, not a live exploit path); a 0-minute event, an empty recurrence-days array, a recurrence end-date before its start date, a timezone re-stamp on date/time edits, an event flagged both private AND shared_with_siblings simultaneously, rapid confirm/decline flapping on the same assignment, a single-element memberIds array, client-side conflict-banner staleness, and the grocery-list-to-event one-way coupling.
