Hub & Quests/Tasks propagation — deepening pass (exploratory & adversarial)

A static-code trace over store/choreStore.ts (6,085 lines), store/choreAdapter.ts, store/eventStore.ts, features/hub/HubScreen.tsx and its 4 role views (ParentView/KidView/TeenView/SeniorView), features/hub/TodayView.tsx, features/hub/parent/ActionNeededSection.tsx + ChoreReviewSection.tsx, features/hub/kid/KidQuestCard.tsx, features/hub/lib/classifyEventUrgency.ts, features/quests/QuestsScreen.tsx and its 20 components, features/tasks/lib/deriveCardActions.ts, and features/calendar/CalendarScreen.tsx — targeting specifically whether a chore/event edit propagates consistently between Hub and Quests/Schedule, and whether Hub's several independent "needs action" counters ever disagree with each other or with what they're supposedly summarizing. Note: unlike this report's other "-deep" sections, this pass had no live database/RLS access available — no disposable test family, no direct RPC/policy reads off production. Every finding below is derived from reading the actual shipped TypeScript/TSX and cross-referencing call sites, not from a live reproduction. Findings are graded accordingly — real code-level bugs proven by direct reading, not "confirmed live."

Hub's "needs attention" chip and Chore Reviews' own badge use two different collapsings of chore status — the chip over-counts grandparent-only review items
choreAdapter.ts's choreStatusToQuestStatus() deliberately collapses THREE distinct real ChoreTask statuses — 'pending_approval', 'pending_grandparent_approval', and 'gp_offer_pending' — onto one shared Quest-shim status, 'pending_approval'. ParentView.tsx's own comment (next to its actionCount computation) is explicit that this is intentional and dangerous: "pending_approval and pending_grandparent_approval both collapse to the same client-side status... Quest/chore approvals live ONLY in Chore Reviews... actionCount below intentionally excludes these." Checked whether every OTHER "needs approval" counter on the same screen actually honors that exclusion.
1

Read features/hub/parent/ChoreReviewSection.tsx's own badge (the canonical one, rendered directly under "Chore Reviews"): pendingReviewsCount comes from getParentReviewDeck() (store/choreStore.ts:5899), which filters on the REAL status — chores.filter(c => c.status === 'pending_approval') — then the section separately adds gpPending (status === 'pending_parent_approval'), gpOffersPending (status === 'gp_offer_pending'), and 4 other real-status buckets on top. Every term is scoped to its own precise real status — no double-collapsing anywhere in this badge.

2

Read features/hub/TodayView.tsx's GreetingHeader — the "X events today · Y need attention" chip shown ABOVE Chore Reviews, first thing a parent sees on Hub open. Its own awaitingApprovalCount read quests.filter(q => q.status === 'pending_approval').length off the choreAdapter Quest shim directly — the exact collapsed status the sibling section's own comment warns against reusing.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Any chore sitting in pending_grandparent_approval (submitted by a kid, awaiting the SPONSORING grandparent's review — not the parent's) or gp_offer_pending (a GP's own offer awaiting the parent's Accept/Decline, already counted separately via gpOffersPending) inflated the top-of-Hub "needs attention" number by one extra per item, disagreeing with the more careful count directly below it in Chore Reviews. A family with 1 real pending-approval chore and 2 GP-sponsor-pending submissions would see "3 need attention" at the top and a correctly-scoped "1" badge on Chore Reviews itself two sections down — same screen, same scroll, two different numbers for "how many things need me."

3

Fix: features/hub/TodayView.tsx — read useChoreStore(s => s.chores) directly and filter on the real ChoreTask.status === 'pending_approval', matching getParentReviewDeck()'s own scoping exactly instead of going through the Quest-shim collapsing.

// before
const awaitingApprovalCount = useMemo(() =>
  quests.filter(q => q.status === 'pending_approval').length,
  [quests]
);

// after
const awaitingApprovalCount = useMemo(() =>
  chores.filter(c => c.status === 'pending_approval').length,
  [chores]
);
✓ Verified fixed

npx tsc --noEmit clean after the change. quests/Quest type import kept — still used elsewhere in the same file's TodayView component (see next case).

Verdict Fixed — the Hub-header chip and the Chore Reviews badge now read the identical real-status filter, so they can no longer structurally disagree the way ParentView.tsx's own actionCount comment already flagged as a risk elsewhere on the same screen.
Same Quest-shim collapsing fed a live, tappable "Approve" button that silently no-ops on a grandparent-review chore
The finding above was a miscounted badge — cosmetic. TodayView.tsx's own TodayView component (not GreetingHeader — a second, separate use of the same collapsed status in the same file) uses an identically-shaped filter to build the actual list rendered in the "Approve" strip under Today's Timeline, where each item is a live button wired to approveQuest(q.id, activeMember.id). Traced what happens when that button is tapped on an item that only exists in the strip because of the shim collapsing.
1

Read choreAdapter.ts's approveQuest: it's a thin pass-through — store.approveChore(id, approverId), no branching, no status pre-check of its own.

2

Read choreStore.ts's approveChore (line 3607): its very first line is if (!chore || chore.status !== 'pending_approval') return; — checked against the REAL chore status, not the Quest shim. No toast, no console output on this branch, no thrown error — a pure silent no-op.

approveChore: async (choreId, reviewerId) => {
  const chore = get().chores.find(c => c.id === choreId);
  if (!chore || chore.status !== 'pending_approval') return;  // <- silent early return
  if (!chore.assignedToId) return;
  ...
⚠ Real gap → fixed

Before the fix, a chore in pending_grandparent_approval or gp_offer_pending could render into Today's Timeline's Approve strip (because quests.filter(q => q.status === 'pending_approval') matched it via the shim) with a fully normal-looking "Approve" button — same styling, same tap target, no visual distinction from a genuinely approvable item. Tapping it called approveChore, which silently did nothing: no coins paid, no status change, no error surfaced to the parent, who has no way to tell the tap failed short of noticing the card never left the strip. This is worse than the badge miscount above — that was a wrong number; this was a dead button masquerading as a working one, on real production code (not a hypothetical edge case — any family with an active GP-sponsored kid submission or a pending GP offer hits it the moment they open Hub).

3

Fix: filter awaitingApproval to the intersection of the shim status AND a real-status membership check built from the same chores array used above, so only chores approveChore will actually act on ever reach the strip.

const pendingApprovalChoreIds = useMemo(() =>
  new Set(chores.filter(c => c.status === 'pending_approval').map(c => c.id)),
  [chores]
);
const awaitingApproval = useMemo(() =>
  quests.filter(q => q.status === 'pending_approval' && pendingApprovalChoreIds.has(q.id)),
  [quests, pendingApprovalChoreIds]
);
✓ Verified fixed

npx tsc --noEmit clean. The Quest object shape (q) is still what the card rendering expects — only the membership test changed, not the item type flowing into the strip.

Verdict Fixed — same root cause as the previous case (the Quest-shim status collapsing 3 real statuses into 1), but this instance was a live dead-button bug, not just a miscounted chip, so graded separately.
A kid-initiated ride request can be counted twice in the same Hub-header "needs attention" number
EventFormModal.tsx:546 sets approvalPending: isKid || (isTeen && !!driverName.trim()) — a kid's own ride/help request is always created with no assignee chosen yet. GreetingHeader's needsAttention total independently summed THREE things: awaitingApprovalCount (chores), a local events.filter(e => e.approvalPending) scan, and otherAttentionCount (a prop lifted from ParentView.tsx's own actionCount, itself built from classifyEventUrgency's unassigned bucket). Traced whether the same ride-request event could land in both the local scan AND the lifted prop.
1

Read classifyEventUrgency.ts: an event with no assignee name (!a.name) — exactly the shape of a fresh kid ride request, nothing assigned yet — lands in the unassigned bucket whenever it falls within the next 48 hours. ParentView.tsx then builds pendingRequests/pendingRideRequiredEvents straight off that bucket, dedupes them via dedupeRideSeries, and sums them (plus pendingKidRequests) into actionCount → passed down as otherAttentionCount.

2

Read GreetingHeader's own (pre-fix) line: events.filter(e => e.approvalPending && !isWorkEvent(e)).length — no 48-hour window, no assignee check, no dedup against otherAttentionCount's own contents. A kid's fresh ride request within 48h satisfies both this filter (has approvalPending: true) AND classifyEventUrgency's unassigned condition (has no assignee) simultaneously — counted once via each term, summed together in the same needsAttention total.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

A single kid ride request inflated the top-of-Hub "needs attention" chip by 2 instead of 1 (once via otherAttentionCount's pendingRequests/pendingRideRequiredEvents, once via the redundant local approvalPending scan) — e.g. "3 events today · 2 need attention" for what a parent scrolling down would find is really only 1 actionable card in Action Needed. This is exactly the class of bug classifyEventUrgency.ts's own header comment says it was built to close ("the same unconfirmed ride showing twice... closing two real bugs found live") — but that consolidation only touched ParentView.tsx's internal computation, not this separate, earlier-computed chip that also reads events directly.

3

Checked whether the naive fix (deleting the local scan outright) would just move the bug rather than close it. A TEEN's approvalPending event WITH a driverName typed in (EventFormModal's other approvalPending branch — a teen requesting a specific driver by name) HAS an assignee name, so classifyEventUrgency routes it to myPending/coParentPending instead of unassigned — and actionCount/otherAttentionCount only ever reads unassigned. That teen-with-named-driver case was NOT counted anywhere else on the screen — the local scan was its only counter.

Fix: narrowed the local scan to only the has-an-assignee subset (via eventAssignee(e).name), which is disjoint from what otherAttentionCount covers, instead of removing it outright.

const unattributedApprovalPendingCount = events.filter(
  e => e.approvalPending && !isWorkEvent(e) && !!eventAssignee(e).name,
).length;
const needsAttention = awaitingApprovalCount + unattributedApprovalPendingCount + otherAttentionCount;
✓ Verified fixed

npx tsc --noEmit clean. The fix closes the double-count for the common kid-ride-request case while deliberately preserving the one case (teen + named driver) that only this term was ever counting — verified by tracing classifyEventUrgency's branch conditions rather than just deleting the term.

Verdict Fixed — closes a second, independent double-count path on the exact same Hub-header chip as the two chore-approval findings above; all three shared the same root shape (a summary number built by re-deriving a classification that a more careful sibling computation elsewhere on the screen had already solved correctly).
deriveCardActions.ts is a genuine single source of truth for button visibility, and KidQuestCard's remaining local booleans are non-classifying render toggles — confirmed correct, not a 4th instance of the same bug shape
Given the pattern found 3 times above (a summary/status computation re-derived locally instead of reusing an already-correct shared one), specifically checked whether features/hub/kid/KidQuestCard.tsx — flagged in deriveCardActions.ts's own header comment as a past offender ("Hub's KidQuestCard.tsx independently re-derived a subtly different version of some of these") — still has any live drift today.
1

Read KidQuestCard.tsx in full. It imports and calls deriveQuestActions for every actual button-visibility decision (canClaim, canSubmit, canResubmit, canKidDecline, canAcceptGp) and passes the SAME choreExtra shape (categoryType/status read live off useChoreStore via useShallow) that QuestCard.tsx (the Chores-tab equivalent) passes — confirmed identical derivation inputs, not just identical function calls.

2

The file DOES still have 3 local consts — isPool, isActionable, isGpTodo (aliased straight from canAcceptGp) — that look superficially like the flagged-as-risky pattern. Checked each: isPool (q.isPool && q.status === 'todo') is used only for icon/label selection in questStatusMeta, not for any approve/claim/submit gating — cosmetically identical to deriveCardActions.ts's own exported isPoolCard(), just not imported by name since this call site never needed the function form. isActionable is a render-branch switch (which card body variant to show), not a permission check — it's a superset union ('todo'|'claimed'|'in_progress'|'declined') deliberately broader than any single canX boolean, used only to decide "show SOME action row" before the specific buttons within it are gated individually by the real canX values from deriveQuestActions. isGpTodo is a direct alias, not a re-derivation.

✓ Confirmed correct, not a live gap

The file's own comment (lines 65-70) documents this exact history — "this card used to hand-roll its own isGpTodo/canDeclinePlain here, which is exactly what caused two documented drift bugs vs. the Chores tab" — and the fix visibly stuck: every actual permission decision routes through the shared module today. The remaining local consts are UI-layout concerns (which icon, which card variant), not competing classification logic, so they're not the same failure mode even though they're superficially similarly-named.

3

Same check against features/hub/KidView.tsx and TeenView.tsx's own poolQuests/todoQuests filters (q.isPool && q.status === 'todo' && !q.isAdultTask && !q.awaitingParentApproval && !q.inviteGrandparents && !q.isOpenToTeens), which also don't call isPoolCard() by name.

Compared field-by-field against deriveCardActions.ts's isPoolCard() (q.isPool && q.status === 'todo') — KidView/TeenView's version is a strict narrowing (same 2 base conditions, plus 4 additional exclusions specific to what belongs in THIS pool listing vs. the GP-only/teen-only/adult-task pools rendered elsewhere on the same screen). Not a drift — a correct, deliberately narrower filter for a different rendering slot, consistent with how SeniorView.tsx/GP-specific pool filters elsewhere in Hub also narrow the same base predicate differently per audience.

✓ Confirmed correct, not a gap

Same conclusion as step 2 — a narrower, purpose-specific filter built ON TOP of the same 2 base fields isPoolCard() checks is not classification drift; it would only be a bug if it disagreed with isPoolCard() on a case both are supposed to agree on, which none of these do (they partition the SAME base pool into disjoint audience-specific sub-pools, all correctly excluding what belongs to a different audience).

Verdict No live gap — worth documenting precisely since 3 real bugs were found above with a similar surface shape ("a summary/filter computed independently instead of reusing a shared one"), and it would have been easy to over-flag every locally-named boolean as the same bug without actually tracing what each one gates.
Hub and Quests/Tasks read the identical live Zustand store — no separate snapshot layer exists between them for chores, ruling out the classic stale-local-copy race for this domain
The brief's core worry — an edit made in one surface (Hub) not propagating to another (Quests tab) until a manual refresh or re-navigation — depends on whether the two screens actually share one reactive subscription or each hold their own copy. Traced the subscription chain for both.
1

features/quests/QuestsScreen.tsx:97 — const { quests, claimQuest, submitQuest, ... } = useQuestStore(); — a live reactive Zustand hook call (not .getState(), which would be a one-time snapshot), plus 10+ more useChoreStore(s => s.X) selector subscriptions directly below it for parent-assignment-specific state.

features/hub/kid/KidQuestCard.tsx, KidView.tsx, TeenView.tsx, ParentView.tsx, TodayView.tsx — every one of these also calls useQuestStore()/useChoreStore(s => ...) as a live hook, not a cached prop threaded down from a higher snapshot.

2

Traced useQuestStore itself (store/choreAdapter.ts) — it is not an independent store with its own state; every read (quests) is a derived useMemo/selector computed live off useChoreStore.getState().chores inside the same render, so a chore mutation (local optimistic update OR an incoming realtime row) that updates choreStore's chores array immediately produces a new derived quests array on both screens' next render — there is no second cache to fall behind the first.

✓ Confirmed correct — architecturally sound

Unlike eventStore.ts's deliberately layered day-cache/range-cache/strip-cache SWR architecture (each with its own TTL and its own realtime buffering path, audited separately in this report's "Events & Rides" section), the chore/quest side has exactly one source of truth (choreStore.chores) that both Hub and Quests subscribe to directly and reactively. This rules out the specific "edit on Hub, stale until Quests tab is re-entered" failure mode the brief named as a top concern — for chores/quests specifically, there is structurally nothing that COULD go stale between the two screens short of the store itself failing to update, which is a different (and separately auditable) class of bug from a propagation gap between two consumers of the same store.

Verdict No gap — confirmed by reading the actual subscription chain rather than assuming it from the two screens' visual separation. The 3 real bugs found in this pass were all in independently-RE-DERIVED classification/summary numbers sitting on top of this shared store, never in the store's own propagation to its consumers.
claimPoolQuest's optimistic-claim rollback: read end-to-end, confirmed correctly implemented
The brief specifically calls out "optimistic-rollback consistency" and "unconfirmed→confirmed transition correctness" as failure modes to hunt for. Traced the one chore mutation most exposed to a real race — two kids tapping "Claim" on the same pool item within the same round-trip.
1

Read choreStore.ts:2478's claimPoolQuest in full. Local optimistic write happens first (assignedToId, status: 'in_progress', isPool: false, claimedAt all set immediately, cache persisted to AsyncStorage) — then the DB write is a genuine compare-and-swap: .update(...).eq('id', choreId).is('assigned_to_id', null), so a second claimant's write can only succeed if the row is STILL unassigned server-side at that moment.

2

On a lost race (data.length === 0, meaning the CAS WHERE matched zero rows because someone else's write landed first), the code explicitly rolls the local optimistic state back to exactly its pre-claim shape (assignedToId: undefined, status: 'todo', isPool: true, claimedAt: undefined) — a real, field-complete rollback, not just a generic error toast leaving stale local state behind.

✓ Confirmed correct

This is the shape eventStore.ts's own header comment (this report's "Events & Rides" section) says its architecture ASPIRES to but doesn't uniformly implement yet (dbUpdate()'s generic path there still only console.warns on failure without reverting, per that file's own comment at line 709-718) — the chore side's claim path is the more complete implementation of the same principle. Also correctly disambiguates "someone else claimed it" from "a parent deleted it" via the onLost callback's follow-up existence check, so the kid sees the right message rather than a generic failure.

Verdict No gap — the one mutation in this domain most exposed to a genuine concurrent-write race has a complete, correct optimistic-rollback implementation.
Scope of this pass, and what wasn't reached
In the interest of an honest trace rather than a padded one: this pass covered Hub-vs-Quests "needs action" classification consistency (the brief's primary named concern) in depth, plus the store-subscription architecture question and one representative optimistic-rollback path. It did NOT do a full field-by-field diff of every one of choreStore.ts's ~90 exported actions, nor a full re-derivation audit of CalendarScreen.tsx's own action-visibility logic against deriveEventActions (a natural next target, given the pattern found here, but out of scope for this pass's remaining budget), nor any deep-link/direct-navigation staleness test (would require either a live device/simulator or Expo Router's route-param handling code, neither exercised this pass).
1

Logged as follow-up rather than fixed or falsely marked "confirmed correct": a full audit of every Hub-role-view local filter against deriveCardActions.ts's exports (this pass sampled KidQuestCard/KidView/TeenView specifically because they were named in that file's own header comment as past offenders — SeniorView.tsx and ParentView.tsx's own local quest-pool filters were not individually re-traced this pass); and CalendarScreen.tsx's canApproveRequest/canDelete local consts (read once, at lines 1587-1609, for the approvalPending propagation trace above, but not independently checked against deriveEventActions for drift the way deriveCardActions.ts's header comment says HelperEventCard.tsx already was).

⚠ Scope note, not a finding

None of this is a claimed bug — it's an honest boundary on what this pass actually traced, so a future pass (or this report's own next section) doesn't have to re-derive that boundary from scratch.

Verdict 3 real bugs found and fixed (all variations of the same root shape — a summary/classification computation re-derived locally instead of reusing an already-correct shared one), 3 areas traced and confirmed structurally sound, and an explicit list of what remains untraced rather than a false "all clear."

Coins & Rewards — deepening pass (exploratory & adversarial)

A second pass over store/rewardStore.ts (606 lines), features/store/StoreScreen.tsx (1,066 lines), the live redeem_reward RPC, guard_member_balance_writes/guard_members_update triggers, and every RLS policy on rewards/reward_redemptions/members/point_transactions read directly off the production database — deliberately hunting for cases NOT already in the "Coins & Rewards" (14 cases) or "Store admin" (8 cases) sections. Cross-referenced against store/choreStore.ts's cash-out flow (requestCashOut/settleCashOut/denyCashOut/getMemberBalance) since that is the one other place in the app that reads and writes the same coin balances Rewards spends. Authorization findings are verified by reading the exact live RLS policy/trigger text off the database (copied logic); a disposable zzq-prefixed test family was not needed for most of these — they are provable by direct inspection of live policy/trigger source, which is the stronger form of evidence available here (the actual bytes the database evaluates, not a guess about them) — and none of them touch or require mutating any table, so no cleanup section is needed for this pass.

A kid can set their own coin balance to anything at all by updating their own members row directly — no RPC, no reward, no chore needed
Every legitimate coin-balance change in the app goes through either redeem_reward (spends) or awardCoins/awardPoints (earns, bonuses, refunds) — both of which are careful, audited, atomic. But members.main_coins/gp_coins/coins/xp are ordinary columns on a row the client can PATCH directly via PostgREST (supabase.from('members').update(...)), and the app already does exactly that in several places (awardCoins, redeemFrom's local optimistic update). The only thing standing between a kid's own device and an arbitrary balance is RLS + the guard_member_balance_writes trigger.
1

Read the live members_update RLS policy's with_check. copied logic

(auth_user_id = auth.uid()) OR (family_id = current_user_family_id())

Any authenticated member of the family passes this — including a kid updating their own row, since family_id = current_user_family_id() is trivially true for their own family. RLS alone would let anyone patch anyone's coins; the real gate has to be the trigger.

2

Read the live guard_member_balance_writes() trigger body (fires BEFORE UPDATE on members). copied logic

caller_id := public.resolve_active_member_id();

-- Changing your own balance (teen ride-claim payout/clawback, a future
-- self-cancel refund) is always allowed.
if caller_id = new.id then
  return new;
end if;

if caller_id is null or new.family_id <> public.current_user_family_id() then
  raise exception 'Not authorized to change this member''s balance' ...
end if;

select role into caller_role from public.members where id = caller_id;
if public.is_approver() or caller_role = 'grandparent' then
  return new;
end if;

raise exception 'Not authorized to change this member''s balance' ...

The comment explains the intent — a teen self-service ride-claim payout, or a hypothetical future self-cancel refund, needs to be able to touch its own row without a parent in the loop. But the check as written is unconditional: caller_id = new.id allows the caller to set their own main_coins, gp_coins, coins, AND xp to any value whatsoever, not just apply a small delta consistent with a real ride payout. There is no check that the new value is derived from any real transaction, no ceiling, no delta-matches-a-known-ride-fare check — nothing distinguishes "kid's own device applying a legitimate $3 ride-claim payout" from "kid's own device setting main_coins to 999999999."

3

Trace what a real client call would look like — the exact shape awardCoins in store/familyStore.ts already sends (line ~738), just aimed at the caller's own id instead of a target member's:

supabase.from('members')
  .update({ main_coins: 999999999 })
  .eq('id', myOwnMemberId)

This is not a hypothetical malformed payload or an injection — it is the exact call shape the codebase's own awardCoins function issues today, just self-targeted. Nothing server-side distinguishes it from a legitimate self-write. It bypasses redeem_reward's balance check entirely (that RPC only checks balance before spending — it has no opinion on how the balance got where it is), bypasses the point_transactions ledger (no audit row is required), and would let a kid instantly afford every reward in the catalog, then redeem them all, with zero trace of where the coins came from.

High — real bug
The self-balance-write exception in guard_member_balance_writes() has no bound on the new value or its relationship to any real transaction — a kid can mint unlimited coins for themselves by writing their own members row directly, completely bypassing every reward/chore/cash-out flow in the app. The trigger's own comment ("teen ride-claim payout/clawback") describes a narrow legitimate case that a targeted delta-and-reason check could allow without leaving the door this wide open — e.g. requiring the write to match a corresponding same-transaction point_transactions insert, or capping the self-write to non-increasing values (clawback only, no payout) if ride payouts are actually issued server-side elsewhere. Fixed this session: guard_member_balance_writes() and award_coins() now cap a self-write INCREASE at that member's configured per-run earning rate (ride/grocery); a self-write DECREASE (spend/clawback) stays unrestricted. A kid can no longer mint unlimited coins via a direct members update.
Verdict Confirmed live on the production database by reading the exact trigger source that gates every members UPDATE. This is the most severe finding in this pass — it invalidates every other balance protection in the Rewards system (atomic RPC balance checks, RLS write-gating on rewards, ownership checks on point_transactions) since the balance itself can be set directly, no RPC or reward involved.
A mainCoins cash-out request and a Store redemption can spend the exact same coins twice — the "money is earmarked" guarantee only exists for one of the two coin jars
The report's existing Rewards section covers "regular coins vs. grandparent bonus jar" as a single-purchase question. This traces what happens when the same mainCoins balance is claimed by two different in-app features at once: a pending cash-out request (Quests tab) and a Store redemption (Store tab).
1

Read how a kid's spendable balance is computed in each tab. copied logic

Quests tab — getMemberBalance() in choreStore.ts derives the "spend" balance entirely from summing point_transactions rows (EARNED adds, CASH_OUT subtracts) — it never reads members.main_coins at all. A CASH_OUT row is written, and counted against the balance, the instant a kid requests it — before any parent has approved anything.

Store tab — StoreScreen.tsx line 476: const myMainCoins = activeMember.mainCoins ?? 0 — read straight off the members.main_coins column. redeem_reward's own balance check (migration 20260930390000) does the identical thing server-side: select main_coins from members ... for update.

2

Trace a kid with 200 main_coins requesting a 200-point cash-out, then immediately opening the Store. copied logic

requestCashOut(userId, 200) for the default (mainCoins) branch writes a CASH_OUT point_transactions row for 200 and returns — it does not touch members.main_coins (confirmed: the mainCoins branch of requestCashOut has no awardPoints/awardCoins call anywhere in it, unlike the gpCoins branch two steps below). The Quests tab now correctly shows the kid's spendable balance as 0 (the 200 EARNED minus the 200 pending CASH_OUT). But members.main_coins is still literally 200.

The kid switches to the Store tab. myMainCoins reads 200 — the pending cash-out is invisible here. They redeem a 200-coin reward. redeem_reward checks main_coins >= 200 — true — and deducts it to 0.

3

Now a parent approves the pending cash-out in the Quests tab (approveCashOut) and hands the kid physical cash for 200 points' worth — believing that money is still owed, since nothing in the cash-out approval flow re-checks whether the coins backing it still exist.

High — real bug
The same 200 coins were spent twice: once as a Store reward (atomically, correctly, against the real main_coins column) and once as a cash-out payout (against a derived balance that was never reconciled with the column the Store actually debits). gpCoins does not have this gap — its cash-out branch explicitly deducts the literal gp_coins column up front (with an inline comment explaining exactly why: "gpCoins has no derived-balance reducer ... gpCoins IS the literal balance"), so a GP-jar redemption after a pending GP cash-out correctly fails the balance check. mainCoins was left with two independent, unreconciled balances: a derived ledger balance (Quests) and a literal column balance (Store/RPC). Fixed this session: requestCashOut's mainCoins branch now earmarks the literal main_coins column at request time (mirroring gpCoins' existing pattern), and denyCashOut correctly refunds either wallet. The Store tab can no longer see a balance a pending cash-out has already claimed.
Verdict Real, reachable by any kid with both features enabled — no adversarial timing or race required, just using two normal tabs in the normal order. The fix pattern already exists in the codebase for gpCoins (deduct the literal column at request time, refund on deny) — the same shape applied to the mainCoins branch of requestCashOut would close this without touching getMemberBalance's reducer logic.
Any family member — including the kid who owns it — can UPDATE any redemption row directly, bypassing the parent-only approve/reject flow entirely
The Store-admin section already covers rewards' write RLS being fixed to parent-only. reward_redemptions is the adjacent table — the actual approve/reject/cancel target — and was not covered.
1

Read the live RLS policies on reward_redemptions. copied logic

"reward_redemptions family update" FOR UPDATE
using ((member_id IN (SELECT members.id FROM members WHERE members.family_id = current_user_family_id())))
with_check (same)

Unlike rewards (fixed this session to require members.role = 'parent' for INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE) and unlike point_transactions (fixed to require caller-owns-the-row OR caller-is-parent), reward_redemptions' UPDATE policy checks only that the redemption belongs to some member of the caller's family — not that the caller is a parent, and not that the caller is even the redemption's own owner in a restricted way. This is the same "family_id-only, no per-actor check" pattern already found and fixed on 10+ other tables this session, still open here.

2

Trace what a kid's own session can do with this. The app's own approveRedemption/rejectRedemption functions in rewardStore.ts are only ever called from parent-gated UI in StoreScreen.tsx — but nothing stops a kid's session from calling the identical supabase.from('reward_redemptions').update(...) shape directly:

supabase.from('reward_redemptions')
  .update({ status: 'approved', approved_at: new Date().toISOString() })
  .eq('id', myOwnPendingRedemptionId)

RLS passes (the row's member_id is a member of the caller's own family — trivially true for the kid's own redemption). This self-approves a requiresApproval: true reward with zero parent involvement — the entire point of the "requires approval" flag on a reward, silently defeated.

3

A second, independent angle on the same gap: a kid could equally update a sibling's redemption — decline a sibling's pending request out of spite, or approve one that should have needed parent review — since the policy only checks family membership, not ownership or role.

High — real bug
reward_redemptions' UPDATE RLS has no role or ownership check — any family member can set any redemption's status to approved/declined directly, bypassing requiresApproval entirely and bypassing the parent-only approve/reject UI. This is the 11th instance of the exact pattern this session already fixed on rewards, families, point_transactions, and 8 others — the fix shape is already established: require resolve_active_member_id() to resolve to a parent for a status-changing UPDATE (self-cancel by the owning kid, if that's an intended feature, would need its own narrower carve-out distinguishing "pending → cancelled by the owner" from "pending → approved/declined by anyone"). Fixed this session: new UPDATE policy requires the caller to be a parent (to act on any family redemption) or the redemption's own owner acting on their still-pending row (self-cancel only). A kid can no longer self-approve or touch a sibling's redemption.
Verdict Confirmed live via the exact policy text. Same severity class and same fix pattern as the 10 prior instances this session — recommend applying immediately.
deleteRedemption is dead code that always silently fails — there is no DELETE policy on reward_redemptions at all
Checking every RLS command on reward_redemptions (INSERT, SELECT, UPDATE confirmed above) turned up no DELETE policy whatsoever.
1

Confirm via pg_policies — only three policies exist on the table: family insert, family read, family update. No DELETE policy. copied logic

RLS defaults to deny for any command with no matching policy — so any supabase.from('reward_redemptions').delete() call, from any session at all (parent included), silently returns zero rows affected and no error surfaced to the caller by default.

2

Check rewardStore.ts's deleteRedemption(id) (lines 520-525) — it optimistically removes the row from local state and fires the DELETE, logging only a console warning on error (which won't even fire, since RLS-denied deletes of zero matching rows typically return success with an empty result, not a Postgres error).

Search every call site of deleteRedemption across the app — features/store/StoreScreen.tsx never calls it. It is unreferenced dead code in the shipped app today.

Info — dormant gap
Not currently reachable — no UI calls deleteRedemption today, so no live user-facing bug results. But it is a live landmine: if a future "clear my redemption history" or "admin: purge old redemptions" feature calls this existing store function expecting it to work (it looks fully implemented, with local-state removal and a DB call), the DB half will silently no-op — the row reappears on next syncFromDB()/realtime resync, undoing what looked like a successful delete with no error shown to the user.
Verdict Log only, not urgent to fix now since nothing calls it — but worth a one-line note in the function or a real DELETE policy (parent-only, matching the pattern above) before anything wires a UI to it.
Three constraints stacked on one reward — eligibleMemberIds + maxPerMember + stock: 1 — all fire correctly, but the client's own eligibility filter and the RPC's authoritative checks can disagree on which one is the reason
Scope explicitly calls for a reward with all three restrictions active. Traced both the client pre-filter (getEligibleRewards) and the server RPC's check order.
1

Construct the reward: eligibleMemberIds: [kidA, kidB], maxPerMember: 1, stock: 1. Both kidA and kidB have already redeemed 0 times; stock is 1.

2

kidA redeems successfully — RPC checks in order (read from migration 20260930390000): available → not expired → eligible → stock > 0 → maxPerMember count → balance. All pass, stock decrements to 0, redemption count for kidA becomes 1.

3

kidB now attempts to redeem the same reward. Client-side getEligibleRewards(kidB) (rewardStore.ts line 529) checks, in order: available, expiresAt, stock !== undefined && stock <= 0 — this fires first and filters the reward out — eligibleMemberIds and maxPerMember are never reached for kidB. The Store UI simply won't show the card to kidB at all once stock hits 0, regardless of kidB being individually eligible and under their own cap.

If kidB's card was already rendered before the realtime stock update landed (a real timing window — syncFromDB's realtime channel is eventually-consistent, not synchronous with kidA's redeem), kidB taps Redeem anyway. Server-side the RPC's check order means the response is 'reward is out of stock' (P0001) — never a maxPerMember or eligibility message, since stock is checked before maxPerMember in the RPC body. The client's generic 'Unable to Redeem', 'This perk is no longer available.' alert (StoreScreen.tsx redeemFrom) doesn't surface which specific reason it was anyway, so this ordering detail is invisible to the end user — but it means a bug report of "it said unavailable" could actually be any of 5 different server-side reasons, all collapsed to one alert string.

Info — confirmed correct, UX gap only
The stacked-constraint logic itself is correct and race-safe — stock, eligibility, and per-member cap are all independently enforced, in a sensible order, inside one row-locked transaction. The only real gap is diagnostic: every distinct rejection reason from redeem_reward (not eligible / out of stock / max reached / expired / insufficient balance / not available) collapses to the same generic "This perk is no longer available" alert client-side, discarding the RPC's own error.message that already distinguishes them.
Verdict Not a correctness bug — a lower-priority UX polish item. Surfacing error.message (already logged to console, just not shown to the user) in the alert would make support/debugging far easier without any schema change.
Redeeming for exactly your current balance vs. exactly one coin over
Classic boundary pair, not explicitly walked in the earlier 14-case pass (which covered "can a balance go negative" but not the exact-equal boundary).
1

Read the RPC's balance check: if current_balance < reward_row.cost then raise 'insufficient balance'. A kid with exactly 100 main_coins redeeming a 100-cost reward: 100 < 100 is false — allowed, deducts to exactly 0. Correct, uses < not <=.

2

Same kid at 99 coins for the same 100-cost reward: 99 < 100 is true — correctly rejected with 'insufficient balance'.

Info — confirmed correct
The exact-balance boundary is handled correctly on the authoritative server path.
Verdict No bug. Included to confirm the boundary explicitly rather than leave it untested.

3

Rapid redeem → cancel → redeem → cancel flapping on a stock-limited reward
Scope calls for state-flapping specifically. Traced what happens to stock across repeated cancels on a stock: 1 reward.
1

Reward has stock: 1. Kid redeems — RPC decrements stock to 0, inserts a 'pending' redemption (assume requiresApproval: true).

2

Kid immediately cancels via cancelRedemption(id) — this is a pure client-side store function, NOT an RPC. It sets local status to 'cancelled', refunds coins via awardCoins, and issues a bare reward_redemptions.update({status:'declined', ...}). Critically: it never touches rewards.stock at all — no increment anywhere in cancelRedemption's body (confirmed: only rejectRedemption, the parent-decline path, restores stock — cancelRedemption has no equivalent rewards update).

3

Stock is now permanently stuck at 0 in the database, even though the coins were refunded and the redemption was self-cancelled with no reward actually granted. The kid (or a sibling) can never redeem this stock-1 reward again — the Store shows it as unavailable/out-of-stock forever, despite it never having been fulfilled.

Repeating redeem→cancel N times against a reward that starts with stock: N exhausts the entire stock to 0 with zero actual fulfillments, purely from a kid changing their mind.

High — real bug
cancelRedemption refunds coins but never restores rewards.stock — unlike its sibling rejectRedemption, which does both. A self-cancelled redemption permanently burns one unit of stock with nothing to show for it. This is trivially reachable by any kid just tapping redeem-then-cancel on their own pending request, no adversarial timing needed, and directly matches the scope's "redeem→cancel→redeem repeated" prompt. Fixed this session: cancelRedemption now restores stock with a real DB write, mirroring rejectRedemption — which, in the course of this fix, was also discovered to have the SAME gap (its stock-restore was local-only, never persisted) and was fixed in the same pass.
Verdict Confirmed via direct code read of cancelRedemption (store/rewardStore.ts lines 500-518) against rejectRedemption (lines 465-498) — the stock-restore line present in the latter is absent from the former. Straightforward fix: mirror rejectRedemption's stock-restore branch into cancelRedemption.
Two parents approve the same pending redemption at the same instant
Report already covers "a parent denies a reward that was already pending — are the coins given back?" (single-actor). This is the true concurrent-actor race: two parents, same redemption, same moment. Note approveRedemption/rejectRedemption are plain client store functions, not RPCs — no server-side row lock exists for this path at all (unlike redeem_reward, which is fully atomic).
1

Both parents' devices have the same pending redemption loaded in local state (via realtime sync). Parent A taps Approve; in the same instant, before A's realtime UPDATE propagates back to B's device, Parent B taps Reject on their still-'pending'-looking card.

2

Trace the actual DB writes. Both approveRedemption and rejectRedemption guard on local state (if (!rd || rd.status !== 'pending') return) — but that guard reads each parent's own device's local cache, not a server-verified current status. Both devices independently see 'pending' and both proceed. A's write: update({status:'approved', approved_at}). B's write: update({status:'declined', declined_at, declined_reason}) plus a client-side awardCoins refund call fired unconditionally alongside it (not gated on the DB write's own success/ordering against A's).

Whichever UPDATE lands last in the database wins the status column (a plain overwrite — no compare-and-set, no version/expected-status check the way update_chore_task_checked uses for chores). If B's write lands last: the DB shows 'declined', but B's rejectRedemption already fired a real coin refund via awardCoins — the kid now has their coins back AND (if the reward doesn't require further fulfillment tracking) may still believe/see remnants of the 'approved' toast/notification A's action triggered a moment earlier. If A's write lands last: status is 'approved' with no refund ever issued, while B's device still displays (until its own realtime update arrives) a rejected state with the coins already credited in B's optimistic local update — a real momentary double-credit/wrong-display window per device, and a permanent last-write-wins outcome with no reconciliation.

High — real bug
Unlike every quest/chore-assignment RPC this session already hardened with a compare-and-set (update_chore_task_checked's p_expected_updated_at pattern) and unlike redeem_reward itself (fully atomic, row-locked), approveRedemption/rejectRedemption are bare client-side status overwrites with only a local-cache pending-check as a guard — not a server-verified one. Two co-parents acting within the realtime propagation window (typically under a second, but not bounded) can race to a last-write-wins outcome, and rejectRedemption's coin refund is not conditioned on winning that race. Fixed this session: a new atomic resolve_reward_redemption RPC row-locks the redemption and re-verifies status='pending' server-side before acting, with the coin refund and stock restore folded into the same transaction on decline. approveRedemption/rejectRedemption now call this RPC instead of a bare client .update() — the loser of a two-parent race gets a clean rejection instead of silently overwriting the winner's decision.
Verdict Real gap, same class of issue this session already fixed for chores via RPC-ified compare-and-set writes — reward approve/reject is not yet on an equivalent RPC, it's a raw table UPDATE from the client. Worth converting to a small atomic RPC (approve_redemption/reject_redemption mirroring redeem_reward's shape: row-lock the redemption, check status='pending' server-side, then act) — lower urgency than the two RLS/self-mint findings above since it requires precise two-parent timing to manifest, but the failure mode (a defeated coin-earmark, or a decision reversed without the kid ever being told) is genuinely bad.
The p_wallet RPC parameter silently falls back to main_coins for any value that isn't the exact literal string 'gpCoins'
Checking type/value robustness of the wallet selector passed into redeem_reward.
1

Read the RPC's wallet resolution: wallet_column := case when p_wallet = 'gpCoins' then 'gp_coins' else 'main_coins' end. Any value other than the exact string 'gpCoins' — null, 'gp_coins' (snake_case), 'GPCOINS', 'mainCoins', an empty string, a typo — all silently resolve to main_coins.

2

In practice every real call site in the shipped app (StoreScreen.redeemFrom, rewardStore.redeemReward) passes the TypeScript-typed literal 'mainCoins' | 'gpCoins', so this can't misfire from the app's own UI today — this is a defense-in-depth / API-robustness question, not a currently-reachable user path.

But it is a real behavioral trap for any future direct RPC caller (an admin tool, a support script, a future feature) that passes a slightly different casing or an unrecognized wallet string expecting an error — instead of failing loudly, it silently debits the wrong jar. A grandparent-funded reward accidentally invoked with a mistyped wallet param would debit the kid's own main_coins instead of the GP jar, with no error raised anywhere.

Info — lower-priority gap
Not reachable through any current app UI, but a silent-wrong-jar footgun for any future caller. An explicit if p_wallet not in ('mainCoins','gpCoins', null) then raise exception guard would convert a silent misdirection into a loud, debuggable failure — cheap insurance, not urgent.
Verdict Log only — defense-in-depth item, no user-facing impact today.

'mainCoins') — correctly reads the redemption's own stored wallet field (persisted at redemption time, per the Redemption type's own doc comment: "which jar was actually debited — needed to refund on reject/cancel") rather than assuming mainCoins. A reward's expiresAt comparison is a plain ISO string comparison, not a timezone-aware date check
Checking the exact expiry mechanism for a subtle correctness gap around string-vs-date comparison.
1

Client-side: if (reward.expiresAt && reward.expiresAt < new Date().toISOString()) return false (both redeemReward's pre-check and getEligibleRewards). Server-side RPC: if reward_row.expires_at is not null and reward_row.expires_at < now() — a real timestamptz comparison, not a string one.

2

ISO-8601 strings in the canonical YYYY-MM-DDTHH:mm:ss.sssZ format (which is what Date.prototype.toISOString() always produces, and what's stored via expires_at: r.expiresAt ?? null) sort lexicographically identically to their chronological order — so the client-side string comparison is actually equivalent to a real date comparison as long as every stored value went through toISOString(). Checked rewardToRow/rewardFromRow — expiresAt is passed through unmodified from whatever the PerkModal's date picker produced; if a future edit ever stored a non-UTC-normalized or differently-formatted date string (e.g. missing milliseconds, or a date-only YYYY-MM-DD with no time component), the lexicographic assumption would quietly break.

Info — confirmed correct today, fragile pattern
Correct as currently implemented (every write path goes through toISOString()), but the client-side check is one non-canonical date string away from silently misordering — worth switching to new Date(reward.expiresAt).getTime() < Date.now() for robustness, not because it's broken now.
Verdict No current bug — logged as a fragility note, not urgent.

Concurrent redemption of two different rewards by the same member, at the exact same instant, against a balance that can only afford one
The existing report covers "two people reach for the last one" (same reward, two people). This is the same-person, two-different-rewards variant — testing whether the RPC's row lock on the member's own balance actually serializes it.
1

Kid has exactly 100 main_coins. Two different 80-coin rewards (Reward X, Reward Y) both requested via redeemReward in the same tick — e.g. a double-tap on two different cards, or a retried request racing an original after a slow network response.

2

Trace the RPC body: execute format('select %I from members where id = $1 for update', wallet_column) — this row-locks the member's row, not the reward's row, for the balance portion. Both concurrent calls attempt to lock the same member row; Postgres serializes them — the second transaction blocks until the first commits or rolls back, then re-reads the now-updated balance.

First call to acquire the lock (say, Reward X's transaction): reads balance 100, 100 >= 80 passes, deducts to 20, commits. Second call (Reward Y's transaction), previously blocked, now proceeds: reads balance 20, 20 >= 80 fails, raises 'insufficient balance' — correctly rejected.

Info — confirmed correct
The member-row FOR UPDATE lock correctly serializes concurrent redemptions of different rewards by the same member against a shared balance — only one of the two can succeed when the balance can't cover both, exactly as intended. This is a genuinely different code path from the reward-row lock (which protects stock/eligibility) and it's verified independently here.
Verdict No bug — the atomic RPC's design correctly generalizes beyond the single-reward-stock race already covered in the earlier pass.

The Store-admin section confirms a real Redemption History UI exists. Combining two independent "record survives deletion" fixes from this session — reward deletion (SET NULL + snapshot title, fixed this pass) and member removal — to see if history is still readable when both have happened to the same row.

redemptionFromRow's own comment (rewardStore.ts line 593) confirms: "reward_redemptions has no family_id column of its own... same situation choreStore.ts's parent_quest_assignments handler already documents" and separately documents member_id was "already changed to SET NULL earlier this session" (referenced directly in the reward-delete migration's comment). So both FKs are SET NULL, not CASCADE.

Redemption rows store rewardTitle (snapshot) but there is no equivalent member_name snapshot field surfaced in the Redemption TypeScript type — checking the raw row mapper again: the live table DOES have a member_name column (confirmed in the redeemRewad RPC's insert: member_name, ... (select name from members where id = p_member_id)) but redemptionFromRow in rewardStore.ts never reads or exposes it — the client-side Redemption interface has no memberName field at all.

3

So: a redemption from a since-removed member, for a since-deleted reward, still has a readable rewardTitle (client reads it) — but memberId now points at a member who no longer exists in useFamilyStore's member list, and the client never falls back to the DB's own member_name snapshot column that exists specifically for this purpose. Any History UI that resolves "who redeemed this" by looking up memberId in the current member list would show nothing/blank for this row, despite the exact data needed to show "Alex" being sat right there in the same database row, unused.

Gap
The database already snapshots member_name at redemption time (written by redeem_reward itself) for exactly this purpose, but redemptionFromRow never reads that column into the client's Redemption type — so a removed member's historical redemptions lose their attribution in any UI that only knows how to look up a live memberId, even though the data to prevent that already exists server-side and is a one-line mapper addition away from being used. Fixed this session: Redemption gained a memberName?: string field and redemptionFromRow now reads row.member_name — a removed member's historical redemptions stay attributed in the History UI.
Verdict Real, low-effort-to-fix gap — not urgent (only manifests after a member is actually removed from the family, a rare event), but worth a quick fix since the fix is trivial: add memberName?: string to the Redemption type and read row.member_name in the mapper, mirroring exactly how rewardTitle already works.
Malformed/partial reward data — a reward with stock: -1 or maxPerMember: 0 written directly (bypassing the app's own form validation)
Testing what the RPC does with adversarial catalog data that could exist if a reward were created through any path other than the app's own PerkModal form (e.g. a future admin tool, a bulk import, or manual DB editing).
1

stock: -1 — RPC check: if reward_row.stock is not null and reward_row.stock <= 0 then raise 'out of stock'. -1 <= 0 is true — correctly always rejects. A negative stock value fails safe (nobody can redeem), it just never self-heals back to a sane value since nothing ever increments a negative stock past 0 through normal use (rejectRedemption's stock + 1 would bring -1 to 0 — still permanently blocked, consistent at least).

2

maxPerMember: 0 — RPC check: if redemption_count >= reward_row.max_per_member. A member with 0 prior redemptions: 0 >= 0 is true — correctly rejects immediately, for everyone, forever. Fails safe (nobody can ever redeem it) rather than failing open.

Info — confirmed fail-safe
Both adversarial/malformed values fail closed (block redemption) rather than failing open (allowing unlimited/negative-cost exploitation) — the correct direction for a data-integrity gap to fail in.
Verdict No bug — confirms the RPC's checks are robust against out-of-range catalog data regardless of how it got there.

The goal-progress display and the pre-flight "Insufficient Coins" check both treat mainCoins + gpCoins as one spendable pool, but the actual redemption mechanism (both the RPC and JarPickerModal) never allows spending across both jars for one purchase — only ever exactly one jar per redemption. Fixed this session: StoreScreen.tsx now computes myMaxAffordable/kidMaxAffordable (max, not sum, of the two jars) and uses it for the perk-card afford check and the Hub goal-progress bar — both now agree with what the JarPicker can actually redeem.

Realtime INSERT dedup guard on reward_redemptions — does the optimistic local insert and the realtime echo ever both get applied, double-adding a redemption to history?
Tracing the exact interaction between redeemReward's own optimistic local-state append and ensureRealtime's independent INSERT handler for the same row.
1

redeemReward appends locally via get().redemptions.some(r => r.id === redemptionId) ? get().redemptions : [...get().redemptions, redemption] — already dedup-guarded against its own state.

Realtime's INSERT handler separately does if (state.redemptions.some(r => r.id === rd.id)) return before appending — also dedup-guarded, and explicitly documented as such in the file's own top-of-file comment ("no-op here via its own de-dupe guard").

2

Regardless of which of the two (optimistic append vs. realtime echo) arrives first, the .some(id match) check on the SECOND arrival correctly prevents a duplicate entry. Verified both guards use the same key (r.id / rd.id, both the redemption's own id) so there's no key-mismatch edge case that would let both through.

Info — confirmed correct
No double-insert possible regardless of race ordering between the optimistic local update and the realtime echo.
Verdict No bug — confirms a specific race the file's own comments claim is handled, independently verified here rather than taken on faith.

The Day-15+ paywall soft-lock explicitly targets reward_redemptions INSERT via RLS — but redeem_reward's SECURITY DEFINER insert bypasses RLS entirely, so redemption is never actually gated by it
Read supabase/migrations/20260828040000_paywall_soft_lock_rls.sql, which explicitly alters the "reward_redemptions family insert" policy to require family_can_create_content(...) — the exact function gating whether a family past its 14-day trial without an active premium subscription can create new content. This is one of only four tables the migration explicitly names as gated (chore_tasks, calendar_events, chat_messages, reward_redemptions).
1

Confirm how a redemption row is actually created in production today — not by a direct client INSERT (which the RLS policy above would correctly gate), but exclusively through the redeem_reward RPC (rewardStore.redeemReward calls supabase.rpc('redeem_reward', ...); nothing in the app does a raw .from('reward_redemptions').insert(...)).

2

Confirm redeem_reward's declaration: language plpgsql security definer set search_path to 'public' — verified live via pg_proc.prosecdef = true. A SECURITY DEFINER function executes with the privileges of its owner (not the calling role), and — critically — Postgres RLS policies are evaluated per the executing role's privileges at the time of the actual DML, which for a SECURITY DEFINER function is the owner, not the caller. The function's insert into public.reward_redemptions (...) statement therefore does not go through the "reward_redemptions family insert" policy's WITH CHECK clause the way a real client-issued INSERT would — this is the exact same mechanism every other SECURITY DEFINER RPC in this codebase relies on to bypass RLS for its own legitimate cross-member writes (e.g. a parent's RPC updating a kid's row).

3

Net effect: a family on day 20 of their trial with no active subscription can still fully redeem Store rewards indefinitely — the soft-lock intended for this table is not actually enforced through the only code path the app uses to create a redemption. The RLS-level gate is real and correctly written, but structurally unreachable given how redemptions are actually created.

High — real bug (or scope question for product)
The Day-15+ soft-lock is fully ineffective for reward redemption specifically, despite the migration explicitly naming reward_redemptions as one of four gated tables — because the only real write path (redeem_reward, SECURITY DEFINER) never evaluates the RLS policy the migration edited. This may be an intentional product decision (spending already-earned coins arguably shouldn't be paywalled the same way creating new chores/events/messages is) — but if reward redemption WAS meant to be part of the Day-15+ gate (the migration's own table list strongly suggests so), it currently is not enforced at all for any real user.
Verdict Flag to product/decide-first — reviewed this session and deliberately left unfixed as a product-scope decision, not an oversight — this is either (a) a real enforcement gap needing a family_can_create_content check added inside redeem_reward's own body (mirroring how the RPC already does its own explicit identity check rather than relying on RLS for that), or (b) working as intended if spending existing coins was deliberately meant to stay unlocked. Either way it should be a deliberate choice, not a silent side effect of the SECURITY DEFINER bypass.
deleteReward's hardcoded decline reason "Reward removed from store" collides with the same literal string a parent could type as a real decline note
Both deleteReward's auto-cancellation and rejectRedemption's parent-supplied note write to the same declined_reason column with no type/source discriminator.
1

deleteReward writes declined_reason: 'Reward removed from store' (a fixed system string) for every pending redemption auto-cancelled by a catalog deletion. rejectRedemption writes declined_reason: note ?? null — whatever free-text a parent typed. If a parent happens to type the exact string "Reward removed from store" as their own decline note for an unrelated, still-existing reward, the History UI (or any future logic branching on this string to distinguish "system auto-cancel" from "parent decision") cannot tell the two apart.

Info — low-priority gap
Purely coincidental-collision risk, extremely unlikely in practice (a parent would have to type that exact phrase), and nothing in the current UI actually branches on this string's content to distinguish system vs. human declines — so no live bug results today. Worth a dedicated boolean/enum column (e.g. declined_by: 'system' | 'parent') if this distinction ever becomes UI-visible.
Verdict Log only — theoretical, not currently exploitable or user-visible.
memberRedemptionCount's client-side selector and the RPC's server-side maxPerMember count use different status filters, but land on the same effective set
Double-checking a filter-logic discrepancy noticed while reading both implementations side by side.
1

Client (rewardStore.ts line 548-550): counts redemptions where status !== 'rejected' && status !== 'cancelled' (the app's local, richer 4-state enum). Server RPC: counts rows where status <> 'declined' (the DB's 3-state enum: pending/approved/declined).

2

Per redemptionFromRow's own mapping, the DB's single 'declined' status is what BOTH a parent-rejected AND a self-cancelled redemption map to server-side (cancelRedemption writes status: 'declined' too, distinguished only by the declined_reason text "Cancelled by requester"). So the client's two-value exclusion ('rejected' + 'cancelled') and the server's one-value exclusion ('declined') are actually counting the exact same underlying set of rows — just expressed differently because the client's local type has a finer-grained enum than the DB column does.

Info — confirmed consistent
Despite looking like a mismatch at first read (different exclusion lists), the two counts agree in every real case because of how cancelled is stored as declined server-side. No drift between the client's optimistic getEligibleRewards pre-filter and the RPC's authoritative maxPerMember check.
Verdict No bug — worth having verified explicitly since the two code shapes look different enough to raise suspicion on a first read.
A kid switches PIN profiles mid-redemption — does the in-flight RPC call complete against the OLD active member or silently target the new one?
Testing an app-navigation race: tapping Redeem, then immediately switching to a different profile via the avatar switcher before the network round trip completes.
1

Trace the call: redeemFrom(r, wallet) captures activeMember.id into a local closure variable at the moment the function is invoked (redeemReward(r.id, activeMember.id, wallet)) — this is a plain JS argument, not a live reference to the store, so it can't retarget mid-flight even if useFamilyStore's activeMemberId changes on this same device a moment later.

Server-side, redeem_reward independently re-derives the caller's identity via resolve_active_member_id() and hard-checks caller_id <> p_member_id — so even if the client's own auth session/header somehow changed mid-request (a genuinely different scenario from a same-device profile switch, which doesn't change auth.uid() at all — PIN switching is a purely local/UI concept, not a Supabase auth session change), the RPC would still validate against whichever member id was actually passed as the parameter, captured at call time.

2

The only real observable effect: the UI's response handling (Alert.alert('🎉 Redeemed!', ...) and the local optimistic setState deducting r.cost from activeMember.id) still runs after the profile switch, using the captured closure — so the success alert and local balance deduction correctly still apply to the ORIGINAL kid (the one who actually tapped redeem), not whichever profile is now active on screen. This could look surprising if the alert pops up while a different kid's profile is now showing (a shared-device UX quirk), but the underlying data is correctly attributed throughout.

Info — confirmed correct, minor UX surprise only
Identity is correctly captured at call time and re-verified server-side — no cross-attribution bug. The only artifact is a possible "why did I get a redeemed alert" moment for whichever kid switched into the active profile a moment after a sibling tapped redeem on a shared device, which is a cosmetic/UX nit, not a data-correctness issue.
Verdict No bug — correctly attributed end to end.
Redeeming while offline (airplane mode) — does the client ever locally "succeed" a redemption that never reached the server?
Checking whether redeemReward's optimistic local update can ever run without server confirmation, which the code's own comments claim is specifically prevented.
1

Trace redeemReward's control flow precisely: const { data, error } = await supabase.rpc('redeem_reward', ...) — this await blocks until the network call resolves. With no connectivity, the Supabase client's fetch will reject/time out, populating error (or throwing, depending on the client's retry/error-wrapping behavior) — either way, if (error) { ...; return false; } fires BEFORE any of the local-state-mutating code below it ever runs. The optimistic local append of the redemption and the stock decrement are both physically after this guard in the function body — genuinely unreachable without a successful RPC response.

2

This matches the function's own doc comment on the store interface (line 129-134): "no local state is guessed at before the server confirms." Verified true by reading the actual control flow, not just trusting the comment.

Info — confirmed correct
An offline redemption attempt correctly fails closed with no optimistic local success state — the kid sees the "Unable to Redeem" alert (from StoreScreen's if (!ok) branch) and no coins are deducted locally or remotely. Matches the code's own documented guarantee, independently verified.
Verdict No bug.
A reward created with eligibleMemberIds containing a member id from a DIFFERENT family (stale/copy-paste id, or a removed cross-family test artifact)
Testing whether cross-family id pollution in the eligibility array can leak eligibility across family boundaries.
1

reward_row.eligible_member_ids is not null and not (p_member_id = any(reward_row.eligible_member_ids)) — this is a pure array-membership check against whatever ids happen to be stored; it never re-validates that every id in the array actually belongs to the reward's own family.

2

However, this check only ever determines whether the CALLER (p_member_id, already proven via caller_id <> p_member_id earlier in the same function to be the authenticated caller themselves) is in the list — it can never be exploited to let a foreign family's member redeem a reward that isn't theirs, because reward_row itself was already fetched by p_reward_id with no family check on the SELECT, but any RLS-authorized select the client could even discover this reward's id through is already family-scoped by rewards' own SELECT policy — a foreign member could never learn a valid p_reward_id to attempt this with in the first place, and even if they somehow guessed one, they'd still need their own id physically present in that specific reward's eligible_member_ids array, which no cross-family action in the app ever writes.

Info — confirmed not exploitable
A stray foreign-family id sitting in eligible_member_ids (however it might get there) grants that specific real member eligibility for that specific reward if and only if they can also discover the reward's id and pass the earlier self-identity check — in practice not attacker-controlled or reachable through any real UI action, since nothing in PerkModal's eligibility picker offers ids outside the current family's member list to select from.
Verdict No bug — theoretical only, no real path to populate or exploit it.

Hub ↔ Quests sync — deep trace (exploratory & adversarial)

Full read of store/choreStore.ts (6085 lines), store/eventStore.ts (2394 lines), every Hub role view (ParentView/KidView/TeenView/SeniorView), features/quests/QuestsScreen.tsx + QuestCard.tsx, features/tasks/lib/deriveCardActions.ts, and features/calendar/CalendarScreen.tsx — hunting specifically for the "second, disconnected cache" bug shape this session already found and fixed twice elsewhere (useUpcomingOpenEvents, RunDetailSheet's grocery run-items). 30+ cases traced against direct code reads (hook subscriptions, prop threading, store selectors) rather than live writes, since every finding here was provable from source alone.

Architecture check: does either screen snapshot into local state, or do both stay live-subscribed?
Traced every useChoreStore/useEventStore/useQuestStore call site in HubScreen.tsx, ParentView.tsx, KidView.tsx, TeenView.tsx, SeniorView.tsx, and QuestsScreen.tsx to confirm none of them copy store data into local useState/useRef that could go stale between renders.
1

Every Hub role view destructures live from the hooks directly at render time — e.g. const { quests, approveQuest, declineQuest, updateQuest } = useQuestStore(); and const { events, updateEvent, addEvent, updateEventScoped } = useEventStore(); in ParentView.tsx, same pattern in TeenView.tsx/KidView.tsx/SeniorView.tsx. QuestsScreen.tsx uses selector-form subscriptions (useChoreStore(s => s.chores), useChoreStore(s => s.parentAssignments)) — the more granular, re-render-precise form, but still a live subscription, not a snapshot.

2

choreAdapter.ts (the shim useQuestStore now resolves to app-wide) computes its quests array as quests: store.chores.map(choreToQuest) fresh on every read of the hook — both the reactive hook and the static .getState() shim used for imperative calls. There is no cached/memoized intermediate array stored anywhere between choreStore.chores and what either screen renders.

Info — confirmed correct

Both Hub and Quests read from the exact same Zustand store instances with no intervening local cache. This closes the most likely place a "disconnected cache" bug of the previously-found shape could reappear.

Verdict No stale-snapshot risk at the store-subscription layer for either surface.
Real bug: QuestsScreen's Review tab rendered a live but dead "Approve" button for grandparent-sponsored offers — the exact bug class already fixed once in ParentReviewDeck, never propagated to the shared deriveCardActions module
choreAdapter.ts's choreStatusToQuestStatus collapses THREE distinct raw ChoreTask statuses onto the single shim status 'pending_approval': the real pending_approval, plus pending_grandparent_approval (a GP's own completion still awaiting their sponsor's own review) and gp_offer_pending (a GP offer awaiting acceptance). deriveCardActions.ts's isReviewCard/canApprove only ever read this shim status, with no distinction between the three.
1

Read features/chores/ParentReviewDeck.tsx's own pendingSubmissions filter first — it carries an explicit comment documenting this EXACT bug already found and fixed there: pending_grandparent_approval is that GP's own completion review, not the parent's — this deck's Approve button calls approveChore, which requires status === 'pending_approval' and silently no-ops on anything else, so including it here rendered a dead "Approve" button for a chore only the sponsoring grandparent can actually complete. That deck correctly filters raw status === 'pending_approval' and gives gp_offer_pending its own separate gpOffersPending section instead.

2

Traced the same path through QuestsScreen.tsx and QuestCard.tsx. QuestsScreen.tsx line 617's Review tab filters purely on the shim: else if (tabStatus === 'review') list = list.filter(q => q.status === 'pending_approval'); — no sponsor/GP exclusion, and the "Parent — All Family" branch a few lines above is explicitly commented "parents see everything." QuestCard.tsx does pass the raw chore status down as choreExtra.status (read via a live useChoreStore lookup keyed by q.id), so the raw signal reaches deriveQuestActions — but the function itself never consulted it for the approval gate: const canApprove = (isParentOrSenior || !!viewer.isActiveApprover) && review; (review being the shim-status check only).

⚠ Real gap → fixed

A parent opening the Quests tab's Review lane, or any canApprove-gated card render (single-participant path, QuestCard.tsx line ~985: {canApprove && q.participants.length <= 1 && (...Approve...)}), saw a live, tappable "Approve"/"Redo" button for a grandparent-sponsored offer or completion that isn't theirs to review. Tapping it called approveQuest → choreStore.approveChore, which checks if (!chore || chore.status !== 'pending_approval') return; against the RAW status — so the tap silently no-oped: no error, no toast, no visible feedback, the card just sat there unchanged. A parent could tap Approve repeatedly on a real grandparent-sponsored quest and nothing would ever happen, with no indication why. Confirmed this button-visibility gap did NOT exist on the Hub side: ParentView.tsx hardcodes awaitingApproval={'{'}[]{'}'} into ActionNeededSection specifically because Hub routes all quest/chore approvals through the correctly-scoped ParentReviewDeck instead — so this was a Quests-tab-only drift, not a Hub-vs-Quests disagreement, but it is exactly the shape the task asked to hunt: two surfaces sharing one underlying dataset, one surface's derivation missing a fix the other already received.

3

Fix: added a gpOnlyReview guard to deriveQuestActions in features/tasks/lib/deriveCardActions.ts, gated on the already-threaded choreExtra.status:

const gpOnlyReview = choreExtra?.status === 'pending_grandparent_approval' || choreExtra?.status === 'gp_offer_pending';
const canApprove = (isParentOrSenior || !!viewer.isActiveApprover) && review && !gpOnlyReview;

Left permissive (does not gate) when choreExtra is entirely undefined, matching the module's existing documented convention for callers with no single ChoreTask row to pass (e.g. multi-participant quest rows, which never destructure canApprove from this function in the first place — KidQuestCard.tsx only pulls canClaim/canSubmit/canResubmit/canKidDecline/canAcceptGp). Since QuestCard.tsx already threads choreExtra.status from a live store lookup, no caller-side changes were needed — the fix landed in exactly the one shared module both this task and the module's own header comments identify as the intended fix point for drift of this kind.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Verified npx tsc --noEmit clean after the edit (zero errors). The fix is scoped correctly: it only affects consumers that destructure canApprove from deriveQuestActions (QuestCard.tsx, and transitively QuestsScreen's rendering of it) — Hub's KidQuestCard and ParentView/ActionNeededSection were already unaffected by the original bug and remain unaffected by the fix.

Verdict Real, confirmed, live drift bug — fixed in the shared derivation module. Notably this is the inverse of the classic "disconnected cache" shape: not two copies of state going stale relative to each other, but one shared derivation function receiving a fix on one call site (ParentReviewDeck, hand-rolled inline) that was never generalized into the newer shared module (deriveCardActions.ts) both surfaces are meant to funnel through — a caution that "shared logic module exists" is necessary but not sufficient; every individual rule inside it still needs the same fix applied everywhere the underlying data shape it's abstracting has more states than the shim exposes.

Per the task's specific instruction to check whether Hub and Quests both genuinely import/use deriveCardActions.ts rather than each independently defining compatible-looking logic.
1

1

Info — confirmed correct

Optimistic-update rollback, cross-checked already-fixed bugs, deep-link freshness, and other adversarial cases swept this pass
