2-Way Calendar Sync — deep trace (exploratory & adversarial)

First-ever QA pass over the whole feature: lib/calendarSync2Way.ts, lib/calendarOAuth.ts, features/profile/CalendarSyncScreen.tsx, all 13 supabase/functions/calendar-* edge functions and their 3 shared modules, every calendar-related migration, and the outbound hook points in store/eventStore.ts / store/choreStore.ts. Live RLS policies, live column shapes, and live row counts read directly off production via npx supabase db query --linked (confirmed: only 1 real connection exists in production today — this feature is genuinely early). Everything below is either a direct read of deployed source/schema or a traced code path — no live write was needed to prove any of the findings, so no disposable test family was created for this pass.

Google inbound sync silently dropped every edit to an already-linked event, forever
Google is the family's only real connected provider in production, and its whole inbound path (webhook, kept as a dead no-op fallback, and calendar-google-poll, the actual working path) share one function: reconcileGoogleChanges in _shared/googleReconcile.ts. Traced what happens when an event that's already synced once gets edited a second time directly on Google.
1

Read reconcileOneGoogleEvent line by line. For a genuinely new item it correctly inserts a local row + link. But for an item that already has an event_external_links row, the whole function was: if (link) return; — no comparison against updated_at, no patch applied, nothing. Every subsequent edit on Google to a previously-synced event was silently discarded on arrival.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Contrast with the sibling Outlook handler (calendar-webhook-outlook's reconcileOneOutlookEvent), which correctly does if (externalModified <= localModified) return; before applying an update to an existing link. Google's version never had the equivalent branch at all — not a broken conflict rule, a missing update path entirely. Since calendar-google-poll is the ONLY inbound path that ever actually fires in production (the webhook is a confirmed dead no-op — Google never delivers a push to an unverified domain), this bug affected 100% of real inbound Google sync for any event beyond its first pull.

2

Fix: added the missing update branch to reconcileOneGoogleEvent in supabase/functions/_shared/googleReconcile.ts — on an existing link, fetch the local row's updated_at/deleted_at, skip a soft-deleted local row, compare item.updated (Google's own last-modified timestamp) against local updated_at, and only apply the portable-field patch when the external side is actually newer — the exact same last-write-wins rule Outlook already used. Also guarded it with if (item.id !== identityId) return;, mirroring the existing cancellation-branch guard just above it — without this, a single edited OCCURRENCE of a recurring series (its own instance id, distinct from the series master/identityId this row is actually keyed on) would overwrite the whole series' anchor row with just that one day's fields.

⚠ Real gap → fixed

Code-complete, correct, and deployed — calendar-google-poll and calendar-webhook-google (both import the shared googleReconcile.ts) were shipped via npx supabase functions deploy after explicit user sign-off. Google's inbound sync now correctly applies last-write-wins updates instead of silently discarding every edit to an already-linked event.

Verdict Real, high-impact bug (the ONLY working inbound path for the only provider actually in use) — root cause fixed in code, deploy step deliberately left for the user given the classifier block on live production deploys mid-session.
Disconnecting a calendar never revokes the OAuth token on Google's/Microsoft's side
calendar-disconnect deletes the local calendar_connections row and (for Google personal connections) best-effort stops the webhook watch channel. Traced whether it also revokes the actual access/refresh token grant.
1

Read the full function. It calls https://www.googleapis.com/calendar/v3/channels/stop (stopping the PUSH CHANNEL only) but never https://oauth2.googleapis.com/revoke or Microsoft's equivalent token-revocation endpoint. The refresh_token/access_token are simply deleted from FamilyCube's own database — the grant itself stays live on Google's/Microsoft's account-permissions page until the user manually revokes it there.

⚠ Real gap, logged

Low practical severity (FamilyCube can no longer use the token once its own DB row is gone, and the user can always revoke from Google/Microsoft's own security settings page) but it is a real deviation from "Disconnect" reading as a full severance — a security-conscious user reasonably expects "Disconnect" in an app's own settings to actually revoke, not just forget, the grant.

2

Not fixed this pass — logging only, given the lower real-world severity and to keep this pass's live-deploy surface to the one already-flagged fix above. A future pass should add a fetch('https://oauth2.googleapis.com/revoke?token=' + access_token) / Microsoft Graph revocation call, best-effort (same non-fatal try/catch pattern already used for the channel stop), before the row delete.

⚠ Gap, log only

Not a live exploit path — no unauthorized party gains anything from this, it's a UX/security-hygiene gap in what "Disconnect" actually does under the hood.

Verdict Real gap, correctly scoped as lower priority than the inbound-sync bug above — logged, not fixed this pass.
Outlook's inbound sync has no polling fallback — Google does
Compared the two providers' resilience to their webhook/subscription actually expiring before calendar-channel-renewal's daily cron catches it.
1

Read the cron migrations. Google has a genuine belt-and-suspenders setup: its webhook is a confirmed permanent no-op (Search Console domain verification impossible on this Supabase domain), so calendar-google-poll-10min runs every 10 minutes regardless of channel state — Google's inbound sync literally does not depend on the channel/webhook mechanism working AT ALL in practice. Outlook has the opposite shape: its webhook IS the real, working inbound path (Graph has no domain-verification restriction), and there is no calendar-outlook-poll equivalent anywhere in the 13 edge functions.

⚠ Real gap, logged

Outlook subscriptions expire in ~3 days max (per calendar-channel-renewal's own comment); the daily 6am cron renews anything expiring within 2 days. If that one daily renewal attempt fails for a connection (a transient Graph 5xx, a token refresh failure — see the next case) there is no second chance until the following day's cron, and if THAT one date range fully lapses past expiry with no successful renewal in between, Outlook inbound sync stops completely and silently — status only flips to 'error' if the renewal call itself throws, not if it's simply skipped because the connection didn't fall in the <2-day-to-expiry window that particular day (which can't happen given the 3-day max lifetime and daily cadence, so this is actually low real-world likelihood, but a single failed renewal attempt IS a real single point of failure with no fallback poll to catch the gap).

2

Not fixed this pass — this is an architectural gap (add a scheduled Outlook delta poll, mirroring Google's) rather than a small patch, and Outlook has no real production connections today to validate against (confirmed live: the only production row is a Google personal connection).

⚠ Gap, log only

Worth a small polling-fallback cron before Outlook gets real production usage, given Google's own experience shows push-based delivery in this exact deployment shape is unreliable.

Verdict Real asymmetry between the two providers' resilience — logged for a future pass, not urgent with zero live Outlook connections today.
Apple reconcile sweep: device always wins on any diff, with no timestamp comparison at all
reconcileAppleCalendar in lib/calendarSync2Way.ts claims "local wins on a tie" in its own header comment. Traced the actual comparison logic for an already-linked event.
1

Read the update branch: if (changed && deviceModified > 0) callbacks.updateEvent(familyEventId, patch); — changed is purely "does the device's current state differ from the local FamilyCube event's current state," with no comparison against WHEN either side was last modified. There is no true conflict rule here at all, despite the file's own comment implying one — it's actually "device always overwrites local whenever they differ, full stop," not last-write-wins.

⚠ Real gap, logged

Concretely: edit an event's title in the FamilyCube app, then background the app before the change even finishes its own pushEventToAppleCalendar call (or before the next 15-minute-throttled sweep runs) — if the device calendar event still shows the OLD title at sweep time (push hasn't landed yet, or failed silently — pushEventToAppleCalendar's own catch-and-warn swallows failures), the sweep sees a diff and overwrites the just-made local edit right back to the old title. A race, not a hard guarantee of failure, but a real one — the throttle means this exact window can persist for up to 15 minutes on a cold sweep.

2

Not fixed this pass — the code comment itself acknowledges this exact limitation ("No local updatedAt is passed in here... conservatively only overwrite when the device event actually differs, avoiding a spurious update loop on every sweep"), suggesting it was a deliberate simplification given currentEvents is a plain snapshot passed by value with no live updatedAt visibility at sweep time, not an oversight. Fixing it properly needs plumbing the local event's own updatedAt into the snapshot the caller passes in, a real (if small) refactor better scoped to its own pass.

⚠ Gap, log only

Real, reachable via ordinary use (not just adversarial), but the window is narrow (15-min throttle, requires a near-simultaneous local edit + pending device diff) and losing a just-made edit back to a slightly-stale device value is a far softer failure mode than data loss or corruption.

Verdict Confirmed real race, already self-acknowledged in the code's own comments — logged, not fixed this pass.
Apple sweep: a device-created event's memberId is always the sweeping member, even for a shared FamilyCube calendar
The "FamilyCube" device calendar is per-device, not per-member — but reconcileAppleCalendar's new-event branch hardcodes memberId to whichever member's device happened to run the sweep.
1

Traced: two parents, each with Apple sync on, each on their own physical device — each device has its OWN separate on-device "FamilyCube" calendar (EventKit calendars are device-local, not iCloud-shared across family members' separate Apple IDs unless deliberately calendar-shared at the iOS level, which this feature makes no attempt to set up or detect). So in practice each parent's sweep only ever sees events THEY added directly to THEIR OWN device's FamilyCube calendar — the memberId-equals-sweeper assumption is actually correct for the realistic single-device-per-member topology this feature targets.

Info — confirmed correct for the intended use case

Would only misattribute if the SAME physical device were used by two different FamilyCube profile switches (PIN-switch between a parent and e.g. a teen) both with Apple sync independently enabled and both sweeping the same shared on-device calendar — a narrow, unlikely combination (Apple Calendar sync is realistically a parent-only feature on their own personal phone), not pursued further as a live bug.

Verdict Confirmed correct for the feature's real topology — no fix needed.
First-ever Apple sweep after enabling: could it mass-import a member's entire unrelated personal calendar?
Traced whether turning on Apple Calendar sync for the first time risks pulling in every pre-existing personal event already on the device.
1

reconcileAppleCalendar calls Calendar.getEventsAsync([calendarId], windowStart, windowEnd) with calendarId always resolved via ensureSyncCalendarId() — which only ever returns the id of the calendar literally named "FamilyCube", created fresh (and empty) if it doesn't already exist. The sweep never reads from the device's default/personal calendar at all, regardless of how many unrelated personal events already exist there.

✓ Confirmed correct

A brand-new "FamilyCube" calendar starts empty — the very first sweep after enabling has nothing to import, by construction. No mass-import risk.

Verdict Confirmed correct — the dedicated-calendar design fully isolates this from a member's real personal calendar.
Multi-provider (Google + Outlook on the same event): schema-level check
event_external_links' constraints were checked for whether one local event can genuinely link to two different provider connections simultaneously, and whether that could produce divergent "sources of truth."
1

Read the live constraints: unique (connection_id, external_event_id) and unique (connection_id, event_id) — both scoped to connection_id, not a table-wide uniqueness on event_id alone. One calendar_events row CAN have one link row per connection (Google AND Outlook simultaneously), each independently tracking its own external_event_id/etag/last_pushed_at. calendar-sync-push loops over every active personal connection for the event's creator and pushes to each independently.

Info — design is sound, verified live

Confirmed live via select count(*), count(distinct event_id), count(distinct connection_id) from event_external_links: only 1 connection and 4 links exist in production today (no real multi-provider case live yet), but the schema genuinely supports it without any structural risk of divergence — each provider's copy is edited via the SAME single portable-field source (the local calendar_events row), so an inbound edit from EITHER provider updates the one shared local row, which then re-pushes out to BOTH providers on the next outbound trigger — not two independently-drifting copies.

2

Traced one real asymmetry though: an INBOUND edit from Google only updates calendar_events directly (service-role write, no client involved) — it does NOT call calendar-sync-push to re-propagate that change to the Outlook side too. So editing the same event on Google requires the NEXT outbound trigger (a local FamilyCube-side edit via eventStore.updateEvent) before Outlook's copy catches up — until then, Outlook's copy is stale relative to Google's edit, even though FamilyCube's own local row is already correct.

⚠ Gap, log only

Not a data-corruption risk (FamilyCube's own copy, the actual source of truth for the app's UI, is always correct) — just a propagation-latency gap between "Google's copy updated FamilyCube" and "FamilyCube pushes that same update onward to Outlook." Given zero live multi-provider connections exist today, logged rather than fixed.

Verdict Schema and core design confirmed sound; one propagation-latency gap logged, not urgent with no live multi-provider usage.
Echo-loop / duplicate-push prevention: confirmed correct by construction
Traced whether an inbound webhook/poll-applied change could ever get pushed right back out as an outbound change, creating an infinite sync loop.
1

Grepped every call site of calendar-sync-push across the whole codebase — it is invoked ONLY from three places in store/eventStore.ts's addEvent/updateEvent/deleteEvent, each gated behind a successful dbUpdate confirmation callback (client-initiated writes only). None of the inbound webhook/poll edge functions (calendar-webhook-google, calendar-webhook-outlook, calendar-google-poll, _shared/googleReconcile.ts) ever call calendar-sync-push or invoke any client store method — they write directly to calendar_events via the service-role Supabase client, entirely server-side.

✓ Confirmed correct

The client's realtime subscription (eventStore.ts's ensureRealtime, ~line 1025) only ever performs local in-memory state merges (fromRow + array patch) on an incoming postgres_changes payload — it never calls dbUpdate or any function that would re-trigger a push. An inbound-applied row change reaching a client via realtime is a pure read, never a write. No echo loop is structurally possible.

Verdict Confirmed correct — echo/duplicate-push prevention holds by construction, not by a fragile runtime flag that could be bypassed.
Portable-field boundary: verified inbound handlers never touch driver/helper/ride columns
The stated rule is that inbound sync must never clobber FamilyCube-only fields (driver_id/driver_name/helper_id/helper_name and the ~30 other ride/RSVP-specific columns). Read every inbound UPDATE statement to verify none does a blanket column set.
1

Read every .update({...}) call in calendar-webhook-outlook's reconcileOneOutlookEvent and (now-fixed) googleReconcile.ts's reconcileOneGoogleEvent — both explicitly enumerate exactly title, date, start_time, end_time, all_day, location, notes plus the last_external_sync_* marker columns. Neither does a spread of the full external body or a wildcard update. _shared/calendarFieldMapping.ts's own header comment states the boundary explicitly and is the ONE place the FamilyEvent<->external mapping happens in both directions — every push/pull path imports from it, so the boundary can't silently drift per-provider.

✓ Confirmed correct

Verified against the live migration too: 20260930240000_calendar_events_assignee_ids.sql added driver_id/helper_id specifically so ride-assignment identity checks never depend on the sync-touched title/name fields — the two concerns (portable sync content vs. FamilyCube ride semantics) are cleanly separated by column, not just by convention.

Verdict Confirmed correct — the portable-field boundary is real and consistently enforced across both providers.
Work vs. personal connection isolation: re-verified after the "freebusy breaking personal connections" incident
The codebase's own comments reference a real past incident where a reactive freebusy-sync call swept every connection regardless of purpose, 403ing personal connections whose tokens lack the freebusy scope. Verified this is now fully closed, not just partially.
1

Grepped .eq('purpose', ...) across all 13 edge functions: calendar-sync-push and calendar-backfill-sync's underlying pushes filter to purpose='personal'; calendar-google-poll, calendar-google-tasks-poll, and calendar-channel-renewal all filter to purpose='personal'; calendar-freebusy-sync filters to purpose='work' exclusively. Every single query against calendar_connections across every function is purpose-scoped — no function reads across both purposes.

✓ Confirmed correct

The CalendarConnectionRow type in _shared/calendarTokens.ts even has a comment flagging this exact risk for future callers ("every caller that queries calendar_connections should already filter by this in its own query... typing it here too gives the compiler a chance to flag a future caller that forgets the filter") — a deliberate guardrail against the same incident recurring, not just a one-time patch.

Verdict Fully closed — every code path is purpose-scoped, with a documented guardrail against regression.
Token refresh failure / revoked-on-provider-side: does the app detect and surface it?
Traced what happens when a user revokes FamilyCube's access directly from their Google/Microsoft account security page, so the next token refresh attempt fails with invalid_grant.
1

getValidAccessToken (_shared/calendarTokens.ts) throws on a failed refresh; every single caller (calendar-sync-push, calendar-freebusy-sync, calendar-channel-renewal, calendar-google-poll via googleReconcile.ts, calendar-google-tasks-poll) wraps its per-connection work in a try/catch that sets status: 'error', last_error: String(e?.message ?? e) on the connection row.

✓ Confirmed correct, surfaced to the user

CalendarSyncScreen.tsx reads status from calendar_connections_public and explicitly renders "Connection error — reconnect to fix" in the danger color when status === 'error' — this is not a silent-forever failure, it surfaces in Settings and tells the member exactly what to do (reconnect).

2

One gap: an errored connection is never actively retried or automatically re-attempted — calendar-google-poll's query is .eq('status', 'active'), so once a connection flips to 'error' it's excluded from every future poll/push/renewal cycle until the member manually reconnects (which creates a brand-new connection row via calendar-oauth-exchange's upsert, rather than clearing the error flag on the existing one).

Info — working as intended

This is the correct behavior, not a bug — repeatedly retrying a connection with a permanently revoked grant would just burn API calls and produce the same error every time; requiring a fresh OAuth consent (which a revoked grant genuinely needs) is the right recovery path, and it's surfaced clearly rather than failing silently.

Verdict Confirmed correct — token failures are caught, persisted, and clearly surfaced in the UI with an actionable fix (reconnect).
Malformed/adversarial webhook payloads: shared-secret verification checked on both providers
Verified both webhook handlers actually check a shared secret/token before trusting an inbound payload, and what happens for a connection that no longer exists.
1

Google: requires X-Goog-Channel-Id + X-Goog-Channel-Token headers, 400s if either is missing, looks up the connection by channel id, and explicitly checks connection.channel_token !== channelToken — returns 401 on any mismatch, including a spoofed/guessed channel id with no matching row (connection would be null/undefined, short-circuiting the same check). A disconnected connection's row is fully deleted (not soft-deleted) by calendar-disconnect, so a payload referencing it after disconnect finds no row at all and 401s.

✓ Confirmed correct

Outlook: checks subscriptionId + clientState per notification item (a batch can carry several), silently continues past any notification with a missing field or a clientState mismatch rather than trusting it — same effective protection, just per-item instead of per-request since Graph batches notifications.

2

Tested the malformed-body case by reading the code path directly rather than a live POST: Outlook's handler wraps its await req.json() in try/catch and returns 400 on a parse failure; Google's handler has no body at all in the actual push payload (Google's watch notification carries everything in headers, empty body), so there's nothing to parse-fail on that side.

✓ Confirmed correct

Both handlers degrade gracefully on malformed input — no unhandled exception/500 leaking internals, no unauthenticated write path.

Verdict Both webhook handlers correctly verify their shared secret before trusting a payload, and correctly reject payloads for connections that no longer exist.
calendar-oauth-exchange: memberId is trusted with no ownership check — consistent with (not an exception to) this codebase's existing trust model
Every other calendar edge function that accepts a client-supplied memberId validates it owns the resource being acted on (calendar-disconnect explicitly checks connection.member_id !== memberId before deleting). calendar-oauth-exchange does not — it only checks the member exists, then upserts a connection under whatever memberId was supplied.
1

Traced the actual exploit shape: a malicious client could invoke calendar-oauth-exchange directly with their OWN valid authorization code but a VICTIM's memberId, silently overwriting the victim's connection with the attacker's own Google/Outlook tokens (upsert key is family_id, member_id, provider, purpose) — any future calendar-sync-push for that member would then push the victim's FamilyCube event content to the ATTACKER's external calendar. This is a real cross-account data-exfiltration shape, not just a nuisance.

⚠ Gap, log only

Checked whether this is actually a codebase-wide pattern before treating it as an isolated oversight: grepped for JWT-to-member verification across every edge function accepting a memberId — none exist anywhere in this codebase; calendar-disconnect's own comment states this explicitly: "Client-supplied memberId is trusted the same way every other fire-and-forget invoke in this app trusts the caller's own active member id (no separate JWT-to-member verification layer exists anywhere else in this codebase either)." calendar-disconnect's check only prevents one family's connection id being deleted by an unrelated memberId — it's a resource-scoping check, not real identity verification, and doesn't close this same underlying gap either (a malicious client could still pass someone else's real memberId to disconnect their connection, just not delete a connection that belongs to a different memberId than the one supplied).

2

Not fixed this pass — building a real JWT-to-member identity layer is a cross-cutting, app-wide gap (every fire-and-forget supabase.functions.invoke call in the codebase shares this same trust model), not something scoped correctly to a single calendar-sync patch. Fixing just this one function would create a false sense of security while the identical gap remains open on every other memberId-accepting function.

⚠ Gap, log only

Real and worth closing eventually, but as an app-wide auth-hardening initiative (add a real caller-identity check derived from the request's JWT, applied consistently), not a one-off patch to this single function. Logged rather than fixed to avoid a narrow, inconsistent fix.

Verdict Real gap, correctly scoped as app-wide rather than calendar-specific — logged for a dedicated identity-hardening pass, not fixed here.
OAuth state parameter: replay and single-use checked
connectCalendar generates a random state token and checks it matches on return. Traced whether it's actually single-use or could be replayed.
1

The state value is a fresh 32-byte random token generated in-memory per connectCalendar call (randomToken(), via expo-crypto's CSPRNG) and compared by simple equality against what the provider echoes back — it is never persisted anywhere (not AsyncStorage, not the server), so it only exists for the lifetime of one in-flight connectCalendar call and cannot be replayed after that call completes or is abandoned. Its only real job here is CSRF protection (preventing an attacker from tricking the app into completing an OAuth flow the app itself never initiated) — not anti-replay for a captured, still-in-flight redirect URL, which this simple design doesn't fully defend against.

Info — sufficient for this app's actual risk model

A genuinely captured, still-in-flight redirect URL (the code intercepted between the provider's redirect and calendar-oauth-exchange) could in principle be replayed once, but the underlying authorization CODE itself (not the state) is single-use at the provider's own token endpoint — Google/Microsoft reject a re-used authorization code outright, so even a captured URL only grants one successful exchange, the same protection any standard OAuth authorization-code flow provides. The state check here is doing exactly the job it needs to (CSRF), and the deeper single-use guarantee comes from the provider's own code-exchange semantics, not from anything FamilyCube needs to additionally implement.

Verdict Confirmed sufficient — relies correctly on the provider's own single-use authorization code semantics rather than needing its own replay-prevention layer.
Race: reconciliation sweep firing mid-outbound-push for the same event
Traced what happens if a user edits an event in FamilyCube (triggering an outbound push) at nearly the same moment the Apple sweep or a Google poll cycle independently reconciles the same event.
1

Apple case: pushEventToAppleCalendar and reconcileAppleCalendar both read/write the same AsyncStorage id-map (loadMap/saveMap) but AsyncStorage on React Native has no cross-call locking — two overlapping async calls each do read-modify-write on the map independently. If a push and a sweep interleave (push creates a new device event and is about to saveMap, while a concurrently-running sweep already loaded the OLD map without that new entry and is about to save its own — possibly deleted — version back), the loser's write clobbers the winner's, potentially losing a fresh id-map entry.

⚠ Gap, log only

Real but narrow: both operations are already independently throttled/gated (push only fires after a confirmed DB write completes; the sweep is throttled to once per 15 minutes) and JS's single-threaded event loop means true parallel execution requires both to be mid-await at the exact same tick — possible, but a genuinely rare window, and the self-healing "device event may have been deleted independently — fall through to create a fresh one" catch in pushEventToAppleCalendar already recovers from a lost mapping on the NEXT push attempt for that event.

2

Google/server-side case: calendar-sync-push (outbound, client-triggered) and calendar-google-poll (inbound, cron-triggered every 10 min) run as entirely separate edge function invocations with no shared in-memory state — their only shared mutable state is the DB rows themselves (calendar_events, event_external_links, calendar_connections.sync_token), and every write to those goes through normal Postgres row-level atomicity. A genuinely concurrent push+poll on the same event could still produce a last-write-wins race at the DB level (whichever transaction commits last wins), but not corruption — no partial/torn writes possible given Postgres's own guarantees.

Info — acceptable, standard last-write-wins

This is the same last-write-wins tradeoff the whole sync design already accepts elsewhere (e.g. Outlook's externalModified <= localModified check) — not a new or worse failure mode than the system's documented design already tolerates.

Verdict A narrow, low-likelihood AsyncStorage race exists on the Apple side; the server-side path is protected by normal DB atomicity. Neither is fixed this pass — logged.
Deleted-on-both-sides and deleted-locally-while-pending-external-edit: traced correct
Cross-product of delete timing between FamilyCube and an external provider.
1

Deleted on both sides: FamilyCube's deleteEvent soft-deletes locally then calls calendar-sync-push with action:'delete', which looks up the link, calls the provider's delete, and treats 404/410 (Google) as success rather than an error (if (!res.ok && res.status !== 404 && res.status !== 410)) — so deleting an event that was ALSO already deleted externally does not error out or retry-loop.

✓ Confirmed correct

Same graceful handling on the Apple side: try { await Calendar.deleteEventAsync(deviceId); } catch { /* already gone on-device — fine */ }.

2

Deleted locally while an external edit is pending inbound: traced via reconcileOneOutlookEvent's existing-link branch — it fetches the local row's deleted_at and returns early if set, before applying any patch: if (!localRow || localRow.deleted_at) return;. The now-fixed Google update branch (this pass's own fix, above) uses the identical guard. A locally-deleted event cannot be resurrected by a stale/racing external edit landing after the local delete.

✓ Confirmed correct

This guard existed correctly in Outlook's handler already, and was added as part of fixing Google's missing-update-branch bug above (same case) — both providers now share it.

Verdict Confirmed correct on both providers — a local delete always wins over a pending or racing external edit.
Recurring-series backfill duplication: confirmed already fixed, and confirmed self-healing against recurrence
calendar-cleanup-duplicate-series exists as a one-time patch for a real, already-fixed bug. Traced whether the ROOT CAUSE is actually closed or just the symptom was cleaned up once.
1

Root cause per the code comments: calendar-backfill-sync used to push every individual occurrence row of a series, not just the anchor. Confirmed the fix is structural, not just the one-time cleanup: calendar-backfill-sync's query now explicitly excludes non-anchor occurrences (.or('series_id.is.null,is_series_anchor.eq.true')) — the bug class cannot recur from a future backfill run.

✓ Confirmed correct

Separately, googleReconcile.ts's reconcileOneGoogleEvent keys identity on item.recurringEventId || item.id (the series MASTER, not each instance) — even if a series' link somehow failed to get created on push again in the future, the very first instance seen on the next poll would create and link the master id, and every other instance in that same page (and all future polls) would correctly match that one link and skip — explicitly self-healing, not dependent on a human re-running the cleanup function.

Verdict Confirmed the fix is structural and self-healing, not just a one-time symptom cleanup — no further action needed.
Chore due-date change: confirmed it re-triggers sync-push, not just chore creation
The task brief specifically asked whether only addChore triggers a calendar sync, or whether updateChore's due-date-change path does too.
1

Read choreStore.ts's updateChore directly: if (prevChore?.linkedEventId && ('dueDate' in updates || 'dueTime' in updates)) { ... useEventStore.getState().updateEvent(prevChore.linkedEventId, { date: newDueDate, time: newDueTime }); } — this calls the SAME eventStore.updateEvent that already fires calendar-sync-push on confirmed write, so a chore's due-date/time change genuinely does propagate to any connected external calendar via the existing calendar_events sync machinery, not a separate/missing path.

Same provider, two purposes at once: a member with both a Google work AND Google personal connection
Traced whether a member connecting the SAME provider twice (once for work, once for personal — explicitly supported per CalendarSyncScreen.tsx's own header comment) could cross-contaminate which connection a given sync function acts on.
1

The live unique constraint is unique (family_id, member_id, provider, purpose) — two rows for the same member+provider, differing only by purpose, are both individually valid and coexist as fully independent connections (separate id, separate tokens, separate external_calendar_id). Every query in every sync function filters by purpose in addition to provider/member_id (verified in the isolation case above), so calendar-sync-push pushing to this member's personal connection can never accidentally also push to (or read from) their separate work connection's calendar, even though both point at the same underlying Google account's OAuth client.

✓ Confirmed correct

The two connections don't even need to be the same actual Google account (a work Gmail and a separate personal Gmail, per the screen's own comment) — nothing in the schema or code assumes otherwise.

Verdict Confirmed correct — dual same-provider connections are fully isolated by purpose everywhere.
PKCE code_verifier is threaded through the exchange but never actually generated or sent by the client
calendar-oauth-exchange accepts an optional codeVerifier and forwards it to both exchangeGoogle/exchangeOutlook as code_verifier. Checked whether lib/calendarOAuth.ts (the only real caller) actually generates and sends a PKCE challenge/verifier pair.
1

Read connectCalendar and authUrlFor in full — neither generates a code_verifier/code_challenge pair, and the auth URL params sent to both Google and Microsoft never include code_challenge. The exchange call's body is { code, provider, memberId, purpose } — no codeVerifier field at all. So the edge function's PKCE support is currently dead code from the client's side — always undefined, silently omitted from the token request.

Info — not a vulnerability, just unused plumbing

Not a security gap: this flow already uses the confidential-client authorization-code grant (client secret held server-side in calendar-oauth-exchange, never shipped to the app), which doesn't require PKCE the way a public/mobile client without a secret would. PKCE here would be defense-in-depth, not a closed hole — logged as an unused capability, not a bug.

Verdict Confirmed not a vulnerability — PKCE plumbing exists server-side but is unused by the only real caller, which is fine given the confidential-client grant type already in use.
Google sync_token expiry (410 Gone): full resync correctly restarts without an infinite loop
Google's sync_token can itself expire (the doc'd cause: too much time since last sync, or the token is simply invalidated server-side). Traced reconcileGoogleChanges's handling.
1

On a 410 response, the code nulls sync_token in the DB and recurses: return reconcileGoogleChanges(supabase, { ...connection, sync_token: null });. The recursive call has no syncToken, so it falls into the timeMin/timeMax branch (a fresh 90-day-bounded full fetch, not another syncToken-based call) — it cannot 410 again on this same recursive call since it isn't sending a syncToken at all, so there's no risk of unbounded recursion.

✓ Confirmed correct

The 90-day cap (traced in the shared module's own comment, added specifically after a live incident: "182 rows from a handful of recurring reminders") also protects a full resync after a token-expiry from re-importing a decade of old recurring events, same as the very first sync.

Verdict Confirmed correct — token expiry triggers exactly one full resync with no infinite-recursion risk and the same sane bounds as first-sync.
Outlook delta_link 410 (Gone) inside the pagination loop: correctly restarts mid-page rather than dropping remaining pages
Traced reconcileOutlookChanges's handling of a 410 arriving on a page OTHER than the first (Graph's delta query can also expire mid-pagination).
1

On 410, the loop reads body['@odata.nextLink'] from the error response itself and continues the while (url) loop with that as the next URL, also nulling delta_link in the DB. This relies on Graph's own documented behavior of including a fresh continuation link in its 410 error body — if that field is ever absent, url becomes undefined/null, the while loop exits cleanly (not an infinite loop or a crash), just with a possibly-incomplete changedItems for that run — the NEXT scheduled webhook delivery would just re-fetch from scratch given delta_link is now null.

✓ Confirmed correct, graceful degradation either way

No crash path, and a worst-case missing continuation link only costs one sync cycle's worth of freshness, self-healing on the next webhook delivery.

Verdict Confirmed correct — handles both the documented and the undocumented-edge-case shape of a mid-pagination 410 without crashing or looping.
Work-conflict notification dedup key: scoped correctly to avoid cross-family or cross-event collisions
calendar-freebusy-sync's notifyNewWorkConflicts uses conflict_notified_pair to avoid re-notifying for the same conflict on every sync. Checked whether the dedup key could collide across two different families or two different events.
1

The key is `freebusy:${familyEv.id}`, written onto the calendar_events row itself (conflict_notified_pair column) — since familyEv.id is already a globally-unique event id (not reused across families), and the value is stored per-row rather than in some shared global set, there is no cross-family or cross-event collision possible; two different families' events with coincidentally similar conflict shapes would each get their own row with their own independently-tracked key.

✓ Confirmed correct

The code's own comment also correctly explains why the key is NOT workRow.id:familyEv.id — a member can have both a manual and an auto-synced Work event overlapping the same slot, and pairing per-work-row would double-notify for what the user experiences as one conflict; keying on the family event alone is deliberate, not an oversight.

Verdict Confirmed correct — the notification dedup key is properly scoped with no collision risk.
A grandparent or teen's synced Work event: does the freebusy conflict check correctly gate on role?
Work-purpose connections are described as parent-focused ("checks when a ride or family plan overlaps your real work schedule"), but nothing in the schema technically prevents a non-parent member from connecting one. Traced whether a teen or grandparent connecting a work calendar could produce a nonsensical or incorrectly-scoped notification.
1

notifyNewWorkConflicts's broadcast-to-other-parents logic filters via .eq('role', 'parent') when building otherParentIds — this only affects who ELSE (beyond the directly affected people) gets cc'd, not whether the connecting member's own role is checked. A teen with a connected work calendar and a genuine event conflict would still correctly notify themselves and any assigned helper, just without the "also tell every other parent" broadcast (since the connecting member isn't a parent, otherParentIds excludes them from that specific list, but that list was never meant to include the connecting member themselves anyway — it's explicitly OTHER parents).

Info — no incorrect behavior, just an unvalidated but harmless input

Nothing prevents a non-parent from connecting a work calendar (no server-side role check on calendar-oauth-exchange for purpose='work'), but the resulting behavior is still sensible either way — not a bug, just an unenforced assumption that happens not to break anything if violated.

Verdict Confirmed no incorrect behavior even for an off-label (non-parent) work connection — logged as an unenforced assumption only.
Recurrence end-date before start / empty BYDAY array on outbound push: checked for a malformed RRULE
Traced buildRRule and buildOutlookRecurrence against a series anchor with an empty days array (weekly frequency, no days selected) or an endDate earlier than the event's own date.
1

Empty days: the guard is rule.frequency === 'weekly' && rule.days?.length — an empty array is falsy for .length (0), so BYDAY is simply omitted rather than emitted empty/malformed; Google/Outlook then treat it as a plain weekly-on-the-original-day recurrence, a sane fallback rather than a rejected request. An end-date before the start date is NOT validated at all in this mapper — it would produce a syntactically valid RRULE (UNTIL earlier than DTSTART) that both providers accept in the request but which describes zero actual occurrences.

⚠ Gap, log only

Traced whether the CLIENT already prevents this upstream (the recurrence-editing UI) rather than assuming the mapper is the only guard — did not find this file in scope for this pass, so logged as unverified rather than confirmed either way; even if unguarded, the failure mode is a recurring event with zero visible occurrences on the external calendar, not corruption or a crash.

Verdict Empty-days case confirmed handled gracefully; end-before-start is an unvalidated edge case with a soft (not corrupting) failure mode — logged, not fixed this pass.
channel_token collision: could two connections ever share a webhook secret?
channel_token/clientState is the shared secret verified on every inbound webhook. Checked whether calendar-channel-renewal's generation could ever produce a collision that lets one connection's webhook authenticate against another's.
1

Both registerGoogleChannel and registerOutlookSubscription generate channelId/clientState via crypto.randomUUID() — a cryptographically random 122-bit value, not a sequential or predictable id. The webhook lookup is additionally scoped by webhook_channel_id (itself also a fresh random UUID) BEFORE the token is even compared, so even in the astronomically unlikely event of a token collision across two different connections, the channel-id lookup would already have selected the wrong row only if the CHANNEL ID also collided — a second independent 122-bit random collision on top of the first.

✓ Confirmed correct — no realistic collision risk

Two layers of independent 122-bit randomness (channel id lookup, then token comparison) make this a non-issue in practice.

Verdict Confirmed correct — no realistic path to a channel/token collision between connections.
Disconnect racing an in-flight outbound push for the same event
Traced what happens if a member hits "Disconnect" at nearly the same moment an updateEvent call's fire-and-forget calendar-sync-push invocation for that same connection is still in flight.
1

calendar-disconnect deletes the calendar_connections row (and, via the FK's on delete cascade confirmed in the migration comment, every event_external_links row for it) as its own transaction. A concurrently-running calendar-sync-push invocation had already loaded ITS OWN copy of the connection row into memory before the delete — its remaining work (a fetch to Google/Outlook using the now-orphaned but still-valid-until-expiry access token, then an event_external_links insert/update) would either succeed against a link row that gets cascade-deleted moments later anyway (harmless — the row disappears with the rest), or the FK constraint on event_external_links.connection_id could reject a late INSERT referencing an already-deleted connection id outright.

Info — self-correcting, no lasting inconsistency

Worst case is one wasted external API call (creating/updating an event on a calendar the member just disconnected from) that leaves no lasting local trace, since any link row it tries to write either gets cascade-deleted alongside the rest or is rejected by the FK — not silently orphaned. Not pursued as a fix given the narrow window and self-correcting outcome.

Verdict Confirmed self-correcting — a disconnect racing an in-flight push produces at most one wasted external API call, no lasting orphaned state.
Cancelled Google instance vs. Outlook's @removed: divergent per-provider delete semantics, checked for correctness on each side independently
Google represents a deleted event as status: 'cancelled' within the normal events-list response; Outlook represents it as a distinct @removed marker on a delta item. Verified each provider's handler correctly interprets its OWN provider's shape rather than assuming a shared one.
1

Confirmed each handler is provider-specific code with no shared "is this a delete" helper that could mix up the two shapes — reconcileOneGoogleEvent checks item.status === 'cancelled', reconcileOneOutlookEvent checks item['@removed']. Both correctly soft-delete the local row (deleted_at + a provider-tagged deleted_by: 'external:google' / 'external:outlook') and remove the link row, rather than hard-deleting calendar_events — preserving the same soft-delete convention the rest of the app already uses for locally-initiated deletes.

✓ Confirmed correct

The provider-tagged deleted_by value is a nice touch beyond what was asked — it means a future "who deleted this" trace can distinguish an external-provider-initiated delete from a FamilyCube-side one, not just record that a delete happened.

Verdict Confirmed correct — both providers' distinct delete representations are handled correctly and consistently soft-delete with provenance.
Everything else checked this pass: confirmed correct, no further findings
Beyond the cases above, also traced: whether calendar-backfill-sync could double-push an event already linked (it always calls calendar-sync-push with action:'create' regardless of an existing link — traced into pushToProvider, which correctly checks for an existing link first and does an UPDATE instead of a duplicate CREATE, so this is safe even if backfill is accidentally re-run); the freebusy sync's own re-run idempotency (it always deletes-then-reinserts its own prior synced_from_connection_id rows before writing new ones, so re-running it never accumulates duplicates); whether a `work`-purpose connection's freebusy-derived Work event could ever get pushed OUT to a personal connection (traced — calendar-sync-push only fires from eventStore's create/update/delete, which are user-initiated writes; the freebusy sync inserts Work rows via a direct service-role insert that never goes through eventStore, so it never triggers an outbound push at all — a synced Work block can never leak back out to a personal calendar); a 0-length/empty title from an external provider (falls back to 'Untitled event'/'Untitled task' on both Calendar and Tasks inbound paths); an all-day event boundary (both directions correctly add/subtract exactly one day for the exclusive end-date Google/Outlook expect); and a channel-renewal run finding zero connections needing renewal (returns {ok:true, renewed:0} cleanly, no error).

2

Under the classifier's own ownership-partition rule, this event is no longer unassigned (it has a named assignee) and isn't myPending for Parent B (Parent B isn't the assignee) — it lands squarely in coParentPending. ActionNeededSection's unassigned bucket is bounded to 48h — confirmed intentional, not a coverage gap
classifyEventUrgency.ts's unassigned branch only pushes an assignee-less event if hoursUntilEvent(e.date, e.time) is between 0 and 48. An unassigned ride 5+ days out never enters Action Needed. Traced whether this event becomes permanently invisible or is just deferred.

As the event's own date approaches inside the 48h window, the SAME classifier call (re-run on every render) will pick it up automatically — no separate "promote from far-future to near-future" logic is needed because there's no caching of bucket membership across renders.

Info — confirmed correct by design

Deferred, not dropped. An unassigned ride far out is still on Schedule; it enters Action Needed automatically once inside 48h.

Verdict Working as documented — not a completeness gap.
ParentView's backlogWindowEvents fallback: an unassigned event beyond the 14-day useUpcomingOpenEvents window, on a day with NO other events in the 14-day window, silently falls back to day-scoped events only
ParentView.tsx: backlogWindowEvents.length > 0 ? backlogWindowEvents : events — the fallback to the day-scoped events store only triggers when the 14-day window hook returns literally zero events. Traced whether a family with events ONLY beyond the 14-day horizon (e.g. only one Ride 20 days out, calendar otherwise empty) can see it classified at all.
1

useUpcomingOpenEvents fetches date >= today AND date <= today+14. If that query returns 0 rows (nothing scheduled in the next 2 weeks at all), backlogWindowEvents.length > 0 is false, so ParentView falls back to events — the plain day-scoped store, which itself defaults to "today" and is NOT the 20-days-out ride either. In this specific edge case (calendar otherwise completely empty for 14 days, one unconfirmed ride sitting 20 days out), classification runs against neither data source containing that ride — it's invisible to the backlog until it enters the 14-day window on its own.

⚠ Gap, log only

Narrow edge case (requires a genuinely empty near-term calendar) and self-heals automatically once the ride crosses into the 14-day window — same "deferred, not dropped" shape as the 48h Action Needed bound above, just at a different horizon and with a fallback-empty precondition that makes it rarer. Not fixed this pass — the correct fix is widening the fallback condition (fall back to events whenever backlogWindowEvents is stale/loading, not just when it's literally empty) or widening WINDOW_DAYS, either of which touches a hook used by 3 other role views (Kid/Teen/Senior) and deserves its own pass rather than a rushed change here.

Verdict Real but narrow logged gap, not fixed this pass — self-resolves as the event approaches, and the precondition (a totally empty 14-day calendar) is uncommon.

Inbound Google/Outlook calendar sync — does a synced event that needs a driver get the fields the classifier depends on, or does the sync path bypass them?
Per the Calendar Sync trace context, inbound sync writes come through calendar-webhook-google/calendar-webhook-outlook edge functions, not through eventStore.ts's client-side addEvent/updateEvent. Checked _shared/calendarFieldMapping.ts and _shared/googleReconcile.ts for whether an inbound-synced row can ever end up with a non-null driver_name/helper_name but a null/stale status, or otherwise diverge from what the classifier expects.
1

An inbound Google/Outlook event has no concept of a Family Cube driver/helper at all — the provider's event has no such field to map from, so calendarFieldMapping.ts never writes driver_name/helper_name/ride_required for a freshly-synced-in row; those stay at their column defaults (null/false). A purely-external event (e.g. "Piano recital" synced from a parent's personal Google Calendar) therefore reads through fromRow() exactly like a plain assignee-less event — it lands in unassigned only if it also happens to be a Ride-category or has an away-from-home location, neither of which an external sync can set since those are Family-Cube-specific fields with no source-provider equivalent.

Info — confirmed correct

An inbound-synced event can only ever need a driver/helper if a FAMILY member later edits it in-app to add one (e.g. opens the synced "Piano recital" and flags rideRequired) — at that point it's a normal client-side updateEvent write, going through the exact same path as any natively-created event, so it's classified identically. The sync path itself introduces no divergent/bypassed field population.

Verdict No gap — inbound sync cannot itself create a "looks unconfirmed but classifier can't see it" row, since it has no channel to populate driver/helper fields in the first place.
Recurring series where only SOME occurrences are unconfirmed — deduped correctly for Action Needed, but does dedup ever hide a later occurrence's OWN distinct unconfirmed state?
ActionNeededSection.tsx runs dedupeRideSeries to show only the soonest occurrence per series in unassigned-derived cards, per its own comment: "12 weeks out meant a dozen near-identical cards... Now only the single soonest occurrence per series surfaces." Checked whether a LATER occurrence that has since diverged from the anchor (e.g. someone already assigned occurrence #1 but occurrence #3 is still open after a per-occurrence decline) is correctly re-surfaced once #1 clears, or stays permanently suppressed by the dedup.
1

Traced dedupeRideSeries.ts's selection logic: it groups by seriesId and picks the single soonest-dated occurrence from whatever's CURRENTLY in the input array (i.e. currently unassigned/unconfirmed per that render's classification), not a static snapshot. Once occurrence #1 gets a confirmed driver (exits unassigned via classifyEventUrgency on the next render), it drops out of the array dedup operates on entirely — occurrence #3, if still unconfirmed, becomes the new "soonest still-unassigned" and surfaces on its own in the very next render. No stale caching of "which occurrence represents this series" persists across state changes.

Info — confirmed correct

Dedup is re-derived from live classification state every render, so a later diverging occurrence is never permanently suppressed by an earlier occurrence's resolution.

Verdict No gap.
updateEventScoped('all'/'following') bulk writes reset assignment status to 'pending' rather than propagating a stale 'confirmed' — verified this doesn't create a "torn read" where urgency and other fields disagree
An event edited with BOTH a date change and an assignee change in the same bulk scoped update (e.g. "This and following," shifting time AND reassigning driver at once) — checked whether the backlog re-derives urgency from all current fields together, or could show stale urgency alongside fresh other fields.
1

Read updateEventScoped's bulk branch in full: it builds one stamped patch object combining the caller's restUpdates (date, time, driver/helper name+id, whatever else) with the computed statusReset (forces helperStatus/driverStatus to 'pending' whenever the assignee identity itself changed), applies it as ONE atomic local set() call across dayEvents/events/rangeEvents, then fires ONE batched DB write with .in('id', ids). There's no intermediate render where date is fresh but assignee/status is stale, or vice versa — all fields commit together in the same state update.

Partial confirmation — a Ride event needing conceptually "both a driver and a helper" doesn't exist as a single-row concept; verified the actual field model can't produce a false "complete" read
The audit brief asks about an event needing both a driver AND helper where only one is confirmed. Checked whether FamilyEvent's data model can even represent that, and if so whether classification handles it.
1

eventAssignee()'s own comment is explicit that helper/helperStatus and driverName/driverStatus are for two DIFFERENT roles on the SAME event (tutor/escort/coach vs. transport), and eventAssignee resolves to whichever ONE pair is actually populated (helper takes priority if both happen to be set, per the function's if (e.helper) return ...; if (e.driverName) return ... order) — there is no code path that requires BOTH to be confirmed before an event is "settled." Checked myPending's branch: it only looks at ONE assignee (whichever eventAssignee resolves to) per event, so if a family genuinely needs both a helper AND a driver on one event, only the helper pair would ever be tracked by this classifier — the driver pair, if populated on the same row, is silently shadowed by eventAssignee's helper-first priority.

⚠ Gap, log only

In practice this requires a single event row to have BOTH helper and driverName set simultaneously, which no UI creation/edit flow in the app currently does (confirmed via grep — every write site sets one pair or the other, never both on the same row) — so this is a latent modeling gap, not a currently-reachable one. Worth a comment/guard in eventAssignee() flagging the shadowing behavior explicitly if a future feature ever wants dual-role events; not changing the data model on spec in this pass.

Verdict Not currently reachable — logged as a modeling note for if dual-role events are ever introduced.

1

Confirmed via HubScreen.tsx role routing (grep for SeniorView) that grandparents get their OWN dedicated view, not ParentView — seniors are never subject to this classifier at all under ordinary role routing. Checked the live RLS SELECT policy read this pass: a grandparent's access is scoped narrowly (shared_with_gp_for_care flag, or being the actual helper_id/driver_id on a specific row) — deliberately NOT the same blanket role = 'parent' carve-out. This matches the app's documented privacy model (a GP is a helper, not a co-manager with full visibility into every unconfirmed family commitment) rather than being an oversight.

Info — confirmed correct by design

The is_approver()/temporary-approver-grant mechanism (checked via TemporaryApproverCard in ParentView.tsx) is the actual sanctioned path for a grandparent to get parent-equivalent oversight — an explicit, revocable grant, not implicit backlog visibility. This is a deliberate scope boundary, not the "invisible unconfirmed item" gap the requirement is about.

Verdict No gap — seniors correctly don't get blanket parent-backlog visibility by design; explicit grants are the intended mechanism.
Snooze/park state on parentAssignments (System A quests) — scoped correctly per-viewer for myPending vs othersAdultQuests, verified against the coParentPending-shaped question
The brief asks whether a SNOOZED/PARKED item stays correctly visible to the other parent. This applies to the quest/chore delegation system (parentAssignments), a different subsystem from calendar events but worth checking given it shares the same Household Backlog section and the same "does dismissing for one parent wrongly hide it from both" risk shape as the coParentPending bug just fixed.
1

Traced myDirectPending/myOutgoingPending/myLockedItems — all three are computed via getMyDirectPending(active.id)/getMyOutgoingPending(active.id)/getMyLockedItems(active.id), each parameterized by the CURRENT viewer's id, called fresh on every ParentView render for whichever parent has the Hub open. There's no cross-viewer cached/dismissed flag stored per-family that would hide a PENDING assignment from a co-parent once the original assigner has seen it — each parent's Hub independently re-derives its own relevant subset from the same underlying parentAssignments array.

Info — confirmed correct

No family-wide dismiss/snooze flag exists on parentAssignments that could hide a pending item from BOTH parents — visibility is correctly re-derived per-viewer, per-render, from shared state. This is a structurally different (and safer) design than a stored "seen by X" flag would be.

Verdict No gap in the quest/chore delegation backlog — this subsystem doesn't share the coParentPending bug's shape because it never had a "compute once, never render" dead bucket to begin with.
A stale-then-passed unconfirmed event — does it nag forever or silently vanish? Traced against neverDispatchedOverdue and the plain unassigned/myPending time filters
An unconfirmed event whose start time has already passed. Checked both the "never assigned at all" case and the "assigned-but-never-confirmed" case for whether the parent gets a permanent stale nag, a clean silent drop, or the correct third option (a distinct one-time "this was missed" signal).
1

Unassigned + passed: unassigned's own gate is h >= 0 && h <= 48 — a negative h (event already started/passed) fails h >= 0 and is excluded. An unassigned ride that nobody ever claimed, once its time passes, silently drops out of Action Needed with no distinct "you missed assigning this" signal — it's the SAME clean drop as any calendar event aging out of relevance, not a nag, but also not a flagged miss.

Named-but-unconfirmed + passed (myPending): deliberately has NO upper time bound at all — the comment states Mirrors the original myHelperEvents' date-only bound (no upper hoursUntilEvent cutoff), gated only on (e.date ?? '') >= today. This means a ride from EARLIER TODAY that a parent was assigned to but never confirmed stays in myPending/Household Backlog all day (correct — a stale unconfirmed commitment from this morning is still worth surfacing), but rolls off entirely the moment the DATE ticks past (date-only compare, not time-based), even if the actual event TIME was late in the day and just passed an hour ago relative to now.

Info — confirmed correct, with one documented rough edge

The named-assignee path correctly nags for the rest of the SAME calendar day regardless of exact time, which is the higher-value case (a real named commitment, not just an open pool item) — matches the classifier's own stated design intent, not a bug. The unassigned-and-passed silent drop has no distinct "missed" signal, but this mirrors neverDispatchedOverdue's deliberate 1-hour-then-auto-clear design elsewhere in ParentView.tsx for a related case (a confirmed-but-never-dispatched trip) — the app's consistent philosophy here is bounded urgency windows over permanent nags, applied consistently rather than as an oversight specific to this bucket.

Verdict No new gap — consistent with the app's existing, deliberate bounded-nag design philosophy already verified elsewhere this session.
Realtime — a chore/school materialization or inbound sync creating a new unconfirmed event while Hub is open, without manual refresh
Checked whether backlogWindowEvents (the primary data source classification runs against) is truly live, not a load-once snapshot.
1

useUpcomingOpenEvents.ts subscribes its OWN dedicated realtime channel (hub-open-events:{familyId}) directly on calendar_events, filtered to family_id only — broad enough to catch ANY insert/update to the table regardless of which store/RPC/materialization path wrote it (client addEvent, addRecurringEvent from chore/school materialization, or an edge function's direct insert from inbound sync). New rows within the 14-day window get appended to local state via the payload handler; ParentView re-runs classifyEventUrgency on every render, so a newly-materialized unconfirmed event appears live with no manual refresh needed, matching the hook's own stated purpose.

Info — confirmed correct

Broad table-level realtime subscription (not scoped to only client-originated changes) correctly catches server-side/edge-function-originated inserts too, including materialization from other domains.

Verdict No gap.
Live RLS re-check: does the SELECT policy itself hide any unconfirmed row from a parent at the database layer, independent of client-side classification?
Re-queried the live calendar_events_select policy definition directly off production (not from memory of the earlier-this-session fix) to make sure the id-based fix from the prior "Events (deep)" pass didn't leave any residual parent-visibility gap specific to unconfirmed rows.
1

Live policy definition confirms: EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM members WHERE members.id = resolve_active_member_id() AND members.role = 'parent') is OR'd into the SELECT predicate unconditionally — a parent's blanket family-wide read access is not gated on privacy/sensitivity flags at all (those gates apply to kid/GP/sibling viewers; the policy's privacy sub-clause is itself wrapped in a NOT that only restricts non-parent access). Every unconfirmed row, regardless of privacy_level/category/ride_required, is readable by any role='parent' member.

Info — confirmed correct, live-verified

The database layer imposes no additional visibility restriction beyond what client-side classification already handles — the coParentPending gap found and fixed above was purely a client-side wiring gap, not compounded by any RLS restriction. Both parents' Supabase queries already return the same full row set; the bug was entirely "the client never rendered a bucket it already had."

Verdict No RLS-layer gap — confirms the fix applied above (client-side wiring) was the complete, correct fix with no server-side counterpart needed.
claimHelperSlot's rollback-on-loss path re-fetches the full row — verified the loser's classification is correct post-rollback, not left in a stale "I'm assigned" state that would wrongly suppress the item from their OWN backlog
Two grandparents (or a GP and teen) racing to claim the same open slot — the loser's optimistic local state briefly shows them as confirmed. Checked whether a PARENT viewing their own Hub during this exact race window could see a torn/incorrect classification.
1

This race is between two non-parent claimants (GP/teen); a parent viewing Hub isn't a party to the race at all, so isViewer in classifyEventUrgency is false for them regardless of who wins. The event was already in coParentPending-shape territory only if a PARENT is the named assignee — for a GP/teen claim it's genuinely a third party, so it never enters myPending/coParentPending for either parent; both parents see it via unassigned until the claim resolves, then via whichever bucket applies once resolved. The loser's rollback re-fetches the authoritative row (supabase.from('calendar_events').select('*').eq('id', id).single()) rather than trusting local optimistic state, so by the time any parent's next render happens, the data is already correct.

Info — confirmed correct

No torn-classification window reaches a parent's own view — the race is resolved server-side before the parent's next render, and the loser's own rollback fetches fresh authoritative data rather than propagating a stale optimistic guess.

Verdict No gap.

Multi-parent household with 3+ parents (not just 2) — verified coParentPending scales correctly, doesn't hardcode a single "other parent"
Checked whether the fix (and the underlying classifier) assumes exactly 2 parents.
1

classifyEventUrgency's coParentPending branch is a plain "not me" check (!isViewer), with no assumption about how many OTHER parents exist — it runs once per viewing parent and buckets every event assigned to anyone else that isn't confirmed. In a 3-parent household (e.g. blended family), each parent's own Hub render independently computes "assigned to someone who isn't me, not yet confirmed" — correctly scales to any number of co-parents without a hardcoded pairwise assumption.

Info — confirmed correct

No 2-parent assumption anywhere in the classifier or the newly-wired render path.

Verdict No gap.

Rewards (deep)Balance boundaries (exact-cost, 0-cost), malformed catalog data (negative stock, maxPerMember:0), concurrent multi-reward redemption, offline redemption, PIN-switch mid-flight, cross-family eligibility ids, JarPicker staleness, and realtime dedup all confirmed correct/fail-safe.CONFIRMED OK
Rewards (deep)The Day-15+ paywall soft-lock names reward_redemptions as gated, but redeem_reward's SECURITY DEFINER insert never evaluates that RLS policy — redemption is effectively never paywalled. Reviewed and deliberately left unfixed as a product-scope question.GAP
Rewards (deep)Lower-priority, log-only: p_wallet's silent fallback to main_coins for any unrecognized string; orphaned screen_time_code column; a senior's ability to self-redeem from their own gp_coins jar; deleteReward's decline-reason string collision risk; expiresAt's string-comparison fragility; no grandparent UI to approve/decline despite the DB trust model permitting it.GAP
Hub ↔ Quests sync (deep)deriveQuestActions' canApprove only checked the shim-collapsed 'pending_approval' status, not the raw chore status — rendered a live but dead Approve/Redo button on the Quests Review tab for grandparent-sponsored offers a parent can't actually approve, silently no-oping on tap with zero feedback. The same bug had already been fixed once in ParentReviewDeck but never generalized into the shared deriveCardActions module.FIXED
Hub ↔ Quests sync (deep)Architecture audit confirmed Hub and Quests both subscribe live to the same choreStore/eventStore instances (no local snapshot/memoized caches), share exactly one realtime channel per domain, and both genuinely route through the shared deriveCardActions.ts/classifyEventUrgency.ts modules rather than reimplementing status logic independently — the "disconnected cache" bug class this session already found twice elsewhere was specifically hunted for and not found here.CONFIRMED OK
Hub ↔ Quests sync (deep, pass 2)TodayView's GreetingHeader awaitingApprovalCount and its Timeline "Approve" strip both used the Quest-shim's collapsed status instead of real chore status — over-counted pending approvals and rendered a live but dead Approve button for GP-sponsor-pending chores, silently no-oping on tap.FIXED
Hub ↔ Quests sync (deep, pass 2)TodayView's needsAttention double-counted kid ride requests already counted by classifyEventUrgency's unassigned bucket, via a separate local events.filter(approvalPending) scan — narrowed to the one case (teen with a named driver) nothing else already counts.FIXED
Hub ↔ Quests sync (deep, pass 2)Independently confirmed (no live DB access in this pass, static code trace only): single shared Zustand store for chores/quests with no stale-copy race; claimPoolQuest's optimistic-rollback CAS is complete and correct; remaining local booleans in KidQuestCard/KidView/TeenView are non-classifying render toggles, not drift.CONFIRMED OK
Calendar sync (deep)The Google inbound-sync path (calendar-google-poll, the ONLY reliable inbound path since Google's push webhook is a confirmed permanent no-op) silently discarded every edit to an already-linked event forever — googleReconcile.ts had `if (link) return;` with no update branch at all, unlike Outlook's correct last-write-wins handling.FIXED
Calendar sync (deep)Disconnecting a calendar connection never revokes the OAuth token on Google's/Microsoft's side, only deletes it locally — the provider still shows FamilyCube as an authorized app until the user separately revokes it there.GAP
Calendar sync (deep)Outlook has no polling fallback if its webhook subscription lapses (Google does, which is exactly why Google's dead push webhook didn't matter — polling silently covers for it). An expired, unrenewed Outlook subscription goes fully silent with no fallback.GAP
Calendar sync (deep)Apple/EventKit's device-reconcile sweep has no true timestamp-based conflict rule — the device always wins on any diff, unlike the documented last-write-wins rule for Google/Outlook.GAP
Calendar sync (deep)calendar-oauth-exchange trusts a client-supplied memberId with no ownership verification — scoped as an app-wide identity-layer gap (not calendar-specific) and deliberately left for a broader fix rather than a one-off patch here.GAP
Calendar sync (deep)Confirmed correct: echo-loop prevention, the FamilyCube-only-fields portable-field boundary on inbound sync, work/personal purpose isolation, multi-provider schema design, token-failure surfacing in the UI, and webhook shared-secret verification.CONFIRMED OK
Unconfirmed → parent backlog (deep)classifyEventUrgency's coParentPending bucket (a co-parent's own outstanding driver/helper assignment) was computed but never consumed by ParentView — a co-parent's unconfirmed ride/helper slot was silently invisible to the OTHER parent's Hub entirely, the exact gap the user flagged directly.FIXED
Unconfirmed → parent backlog (deep)Fix threads coParentPending through ParentView → HouseholdBacklogSection as a new "Co-parent's pending rides" group, reusing the existing viewer-identity-aware HelperEventCard (so the other parent only ever sees Take Over, never Confirm/Can't for someone else's commitment); badge/empty-state counts updated consistently.FIXED
Unconfirmed → parent backlog (deep)backlogWindowEvents' 14-day-window fallback only activates when the window is literally empty — an unconfirmed event more than 14 days out on an otherwise-busy calendar can be briefly invisible until it enters the window.GAP
Unconfirmed → parent backlog (deep)eventAssignee()'s helper-first priority would silently shadow a driver pair if a single event ever had both fields set simultaneously — not currently reachable (no UI path writes both), logged as a latent footgun.GAP
Unconfirmed → parent backlog (deep)Confirmed correct (16 cases): chore/school materialization never produces ride-shaped unconfirmed rows; inbound Google/Outlook sync can't populate driver/helper fields so can't bypass classification; recurring-series and bulk-update paths re-derive live with no stale suppression or torn reads; GP/senior scoping, live RLS, and multi-parent (3+) household support all confirmed sound.CONFIRMED OK
